"We will not make any Justices, Sheriffs or Bailiffs, but of such as KNOW THE LAW OF THE REALM and mean duly to observe it." 

-Magna Charta, Clause XLV. 

"The history of the law. . . points out that almost the only thing that can be assumed as certainly to be wished is that 

MEN SHOULD KNOW THE RULES BY WHICH THE GAME WILL BE PLAYED." -Mr. Justice Holmes.* *Collected Legal Papers (1920), Harcourt, Brace Co., publishers.

MAXIMS OF LAW AND EVIDENCE 

"It is the drawing of distinctions that do not exist that brings the law into confusion and the courts into contempt." Ethridge,、 

J., Poplarville Sawmill Co. v. Marx & Sons, (1918) 117 Miss. 10, 77 So. 815, 819. ""The object of a judicial trial is to enable the State to establish and enforce justice between party and party; * * * when a party appeals to the sovereign for justice he impliedly consents to the doing of justice to the other party, and impliedly agrees in advance to make any disclosure which is necessary to be made in order that justice may be done'-the latter, of course, not including any privileged matter." Griffith, J., Kress v. Sharpe, (1930) 156 Miss. 693, 126 So. 650, 68 A. L. R. 167. ""Testimonial duty to the community is paramount to private interests, and * no man is to be denied the enforcement of his rights merely because another possesses the facts without which the right cannot be ascertained and enforced.' Smith, C. J., Du Bois v. Thomas, (1929) 154 Miss. 286, 122 So. 495. "The rules of evidence should be so moulded as to make it at least possible to detect every description of forgery or counterfeits; otherwise, only the clumsily executed ones would ever meet with detection or condemnation. * * Both government and law presuppose human weakness, and at least the possibility of human depravity, and those connected with the administration of the law know, perhaps from actual observation, that what is but a theory in government is in many cases true as a fact. It is indeed part of the very law of evidence itself that it will adapt its rules to every variety of case or question, which may arise for investigation in a court of justice. * * Narrow or contracted rules may enable persons of even tolerable skill to defy the scrutiny of a legal investigation, when under different rules detection would be certain." Fisher, J., Moye v. Herndon, (1855) 30 Miss. 110.

"In the case before us, as it often happens in legal science, it will be found much more difficult to reconcile the apparently conflicting opinions of courts-swayed by the peculiar hardship of the individual cases they have been called to decide-with each other, or with true principles, than to ascertain the principles themselves, which should guide us." Harris, J., N. O., J. & 

G. N. R. Co. v. Albritton, (1859) 38 Miss. 242, 276. "But as mere pride of consistency-especially in adhering to error-is no part of the meed we covet in our judicial labors, we have given full consideration to the arguments and authorities relied on to establish the conclusion contended for, without reference to our former opinion." Harris, J., Jeff v. State, (1860) 

39 Miss. 593. ii

PREFACE 

Over five thousand decisions on evidence have been rendered in Mississippi, but the rulings are not accessible in convenient form. No one-volume work exists, treating the system of rules on evidence in Mississippi. The rulings are digested under many substantive and adjectival headings in standard digests, and are scattered through fifteen volumes of the Mississippi digest. Neither do standard treatises present continuously the law in a given jurisdiction. There is a need for a systematic presentation of this subject in a form as concise as the accurate statement of the rulings permits. The law of a single jurisdiction may not have the theoretical attractions and symmetry of a treatise giving composite rules, taken from many jurisdictions and in force as law in no one jurisdiction; nevertheless, for trials, and for the purpose of wise revision of rules, a summary statement of existing rules of evidence in this jurisdiction has a place. 

The present book condenses the existing rules of proof in Mississippi. In preparing the work, the writer examined and digested more than five thousand Mississippi decisions on evidence, and selected the principal rulings and significant points for inclusion in the summary statement. Although it was necessary to omit something of detail which the larger digest contains, the effort has been made to present all possible factual detail within the permissible limits of a handbook, in order that a mere statement of abstract rules might not be given. Scope In theory, rules of evidence are designed to secure trustworthy observation and report of relevant acts and events. 

The subject matter of evidence consists of facts-all manifestations of knowledge, desire, and volition on the part of persons, iii

and of conditions, or states, and events in the physical universe, to the extent relevant under the issues. 

The facts are made available through testimony, and its substitutes, i. e., view, real evidence.

Consequently, the rules of evidence may be classified according as they deal with facts as defined above, or with the qualifications of witnesses and with other means of observing and reporting facts, and with various related policies and procedures. 

In pursuance of this familiar and simple and comprehensive analysis, the rules are treated under the headings: Facts In General (theory of admissibility and relevancy, presumptions, circumstantial evidence, and judicial notice); Documentary Evidence (parol evidence, best evidence, rules on formalities required in manifesting legal acts); Testimonial Evidence (qualifications of witnesses, opinion evidence, the hearsay rule and its exceptions, impeachment, persons, and privileges); Real Evidence and View; and Burden and Procedure of Proof. 

acts. 

Substantive law defines status and the legal consequences of Although rules defining the permissible form of manifestation of contractual or other intent properly may be considered substantive when by abstraction only the legal consequences, or changes of legal relations, are contemplated, nevertheless these rules are but the conventional recognition of manifestation of intent; and define the form by which intent may be manifested or expressed; and thus are evidentiary in nature. For the foregoing reason, rules of form are included in this summary. 

Rules of law excluding evidence in order to prevent infringement of some other recognized interest, as the marriage status, privacy of the home, or other substantive right, are not properly rules of evidence, in the sense of relating to the connection between things observed and the intent of persons or physical facts. 

However, by practice such rules are usually treated under the heading of evidence. Strictly, such rules are incidents of various privileges and immunities connected with personal status and ownership of property, exist to prevent infringement of some substantive right, and operate to suppress the truth. iv

For convenience, rules of procedure in offering evidence and in objecting to the offer of evidence are usually treated as ancillary to the law of evidence. 

But rules of the probative effect of evidence on the issues of cases in the hands of juries, and the respective provinces and functions of court and jury, are not part of the law of evidence as here defined. Cognizance of the connection between manifestations of acts and events and the things manifested is taken in order to find that trustworthy observations and reports are offered for consideration by the jury, and when the rules are satisfied the offered evidence is admissible. But after the finding that evidence is admissible, the rules relating to the sufficiency of the evidence to convince the jury are not treated as a part of the rules of evidence in this work. 

Jackson, Mississippi 

May 29, 1941 

J. B. H. Jr.

Preliminary 45 

SUMMARY STATEMENT 

OF 

EVIDENCE IN MISSISSIPPI I. PRELIMINARY 

1. The proper law.-Lex fori is the proper law of evidence (1). The decisions do not yet differentiate the rulings of exclusion based on substantive right which may be governed by the law of the place, and rulings based on strictly evidentiary grounds (Metropolitan Life 

Ins. Co. v. McSwain, (1928) 115 So. 555, 149 Miss. 455). 

2. Privilege to invoke rules of evidence.-Generally, a party has the privilege to invoke rules of evidence without liability to adverse inference in argumentation (2). But exceptions exist, as in comment on a party's not calling a physician, approved in Killings v. Metropolitan Life Ins. Co., (1940) 192 So. 577, 187 Miss. 265; and in cases in which a party does not call a witness available to himself alone (See Presumptions, 78).

II. FACTS IN GENERAL A. ADMISSIBILITY AND RELEVANCY A-1. Test. The test of admissibility is relevancy and pertinency, not weight or conclusiveness (4). 

Relevancy is determined by logic and is the application of principles of reason, judgment, and systematic arrangement. Facts are relevant if they afford a reasonable basis of inference as to the principal fact in issue (5). 

The law furnishes no test of relevancy. Specific cases are governed by statutes, precedents, logic and experience (6).

The experienced connection between the situation of the witness and the truth or falsity of his testimony

is one of the general principles underlying rules as to the competency of witnesses. In general, the law excludes those witnesses whose evidence is more likely to mislead juries. This is the principle underlying general rules of admissibility of evidence (3). 

B. PRESUMPTIONS 

1. Theory. The law infers or presumes the existence of unknown facts from known facts where human experience affords a basis for recognizing the connection (10). 

The word "presumption” may denote (1) a conclusive inference drawn by law, (2) a ruling of the judge that an inference will be drawn unless the other party rebuts or throws doubt on it by some evidence, or (3) a mere permissive inference that may be drawn by the jury or court respectively in weighing or reviewing the evidence at trial or on appeal (7). 

Wher. evidence opposing a presumption of the second type, imposing a duty to come forward with some evidence, equals in value and weight the evidence upon which the presumption rests, the presumption is satisfied and vanishes (8). 

Caution.-Except in dealing with legal presumptions, cautionary instructions and other technical matters, the court now holds that it should not charge the jury as to the inference to be drawn from evidence. The present rules as to presumptions, cautionary instructions, and other technical matters will not be enlarged hereafter, because of the increased range of observation of the present-day jurors (9). Unless an instruction has been given in a decided case it is more prudent not to ask for one in a new case under existing rulings. The disfavor at 

Court of instructions expressly authorizing inferences of fact is shown also in a recent opinion of Chief Justice Smith. The opinion states an instruction is on the "weight of evidence" and is erroneous which charges that the jury may disbelieve all of the testimony of a witness if the jury believes he has sworn falsely to any

Presumptions 

47 material fact, knowingly, maliciously, and corruptly. The 

Court states that former rulings had not noticed that the instruction was on the weight of the evidence (Metropolitan L. Ins. Co. v. Wright, (1940) 199 So. 289). This view of the Court narrows judicial power in dealing with the evidence before the Court as recognized in Perry v. 

Clark, (1841) 5 H. 495, in the numerous cases approving the specific instruction authorizing rejection of testimony of a perjurer, and in the numerous cases authorizing cautionary instructions on evidence. Hence, the tendency of the Court is to disapprove instructions expressly authorizing special inferences from the facts, although previously approved (1968).

Another source of difficulty is in the several meanings of the word "presumption" in the decisions. The loose use of the word is confusing, as the sections treating specific presumptions will show. Unless an instruction has been given, it would seem that the word "presumption" should be taken in the third sense, supra, of a mere permissive inference, in all cases in which the Court does not indicate the exact denotation of the term "presumption." 

2. Practical difficulties in handling the foregoing rules on inferences.-Abstract instructions are erroneous (11).

Written instructions on request of counsel applying the law to the facts and outlining the permissible legal consequences are required, but the court must not sum up the evidence or comment on the tendency or weight of the evidence (12).

It is error in an instruction to sum up facts and to state the tendency of evidence. It is most difficult to apply the law to the facts and not to comment upon the tendency of the facts (13). It is error to assume a fact. 

To avoid this, the word "if" is preferable to "when" or the like, in referring to facts (14). Where inferences are permissible but not conclusive and depend upon the general knowledge and experience of men, the judgment and experience of the jury, not of the judge, should be

appealed to, and it is error to instruct on the probative effect, as the value of a corroborated confession (15). 

3. Singling out facts. It is error to single out or emphasize unduly a single fact, and it is error not to restrict the jury to facts which reasonably warrant a verdict (16). 

4. Cautionary instructions on evidence distinguished. Certain cautionary instructions on evidence have been approved by the court, although by implication this expresses some disfavor toward certain kinds of evidence. Thus, the testimony of the prosecutrix in an action for rape, or of conversations with a deceased person, or of an accomplice, or circumstantial evidence is to be received with caution, and the jury may be so instructed (17). The Court has discretion to refuse the cautionary instruction, on accomplices (17-a). 

i. Specific Presumptions on Public Law 

5. Legislative power. It is presumed that legislative power under the Mississippi Constitution of 1890 is plenary, unless the contrary appears from the State or 

Federal Constitution (18). 

6. Enactment of a Statute.—It is presumed that statutes are properly enacted (19). 

7. Common law the same in other states.-It is presumed that the common law of Tennessee is the same as that of Mississippi (20). 

8. Common law and the Constitution.-There is a presumption that the common law was present to the minds of the members of the Constitutional Convention in writing the Constitution as, for example, in requiring that the accused shall be confronted by his accusers, and that common-law, evidentiary exceptions to this rule, viz., dying declarations, were tacitly recognized as a part of the Constitution. Hence, the common law is a part of the 

Constitution on matters of evidence, except where repealed expressly or by necessary implication (21).

Presumptions 

49 9. Statutes consistent with the Constitution.-Statutes are construed as intended to be consistent with the Constitution (22). 

10. Statutes prospective. Statutes are construed as prospective unless the contrary clearly appears (23). 

10-a. Statutes reasonable.-It will not be presumed in construing a statute that the legislature intended to bring about a result that is useless and confusing (23-a). 

11. No double taxation. It is assumed that statutes were not intended to impose double taxation unless expressly so declaring (24). 

12. Knowledge of law by legislature, policeman jury, trustees, etc.-For some purposes, it is presumed that all men know the law, viz., the legislature, police, juries, trustees, persons dealing with supervisors, highway directors, purchasers at tax sales, and persons making purchases within the sales-tax law (25). 

13. Civil actions.-But in civil actions in per- sonam it is proper to instruct that there is no presumption of actual knowledge of the law or of individual rights (26). 

14. Knowledge of meaning of words.-Juries and private persons are presumed to know the meaning of words (27). In reviewing evidence, where the parties to contracts are all engaged in the same business or trade, they are presumed to have knowledge of the trade significance of terms employed relating to that business, but as to those not affiliated with such trade or business, the terms must be construed in their ordinary and popular sense (27-a). 

15. Briefs exhaust the law.-Briefs are assumed to exhaust the law, and if no brief on a point is filed, then, it is assumed that no law supports the point (28). 

16. Decisions refer to facts.—The language of decisions and treatises is taken to refer to, and to be limited

by, the facts before the court in the cases adjudicated, or cited (29). 

17. Existence of ordinance when basis of pleadings. -An ordinance is presumed to exist when made the basis of pleadings (30). 

18. Prejudice from error of law. The reviewing court will assume that prejudice resulted from error of law. In chancery, an erroneous ruling on evidence will be assumed to have influenced the decision, though to a less degree than before a jury (31). 

ii. On Status 

19. Citizenship from residence.-Residence and ownership of property for a year raises an inference of citizenship (32). 

20. Age of accountability.-A person under 7 years of age is conclusively presumed to be incapable of crime. 

Between 7 and 14 it may be inferred that a person is capable, but prima facie he is incapable, of crime (33). 

Cf. Sec. 19, Ch. 300, Laws, 1940, providing that no child under the age of fourteen shall be proceeded against criminally. 

21. Sanity. Every man is presumed prima facie to be sane (34). The presumption vanishes when evidence of a reasonable probability of insanity is offered (34-a). 

iii. Criminal Law 

22. Innocence.-Every man is presumed prima facie to be innocent (35) (Instruction given). This presumption has evidentiary force: it goes throughout the trial and is to be considered by the jury after all the evidence is in (36).

In civil cases the presumption of innocence vanishes when all the facts are in evidence (37). 

23. Killing not justifiable.-There is no presumption that killing with a deadly weapon is justifiable (38).

Presumptions 

51 

24. Malice from killing.-Homicide is presumed to be malicious (Instruction given) (39). 

25. No presumption of manslaughter from killing.

There is no presumption of manslaughter from killing a man (40). 

26. Use of deadly weapons in homicide.—The use of deadly weapons in homicide is prima facie evidence of malice and the jury should be so instructed (Instruction given) (41). 

27. Vanish theory.-When the facts and circumstances are disclosed the presumption of malice from the use of a deadly weapon in a homicide is inapplicable and the case is to be decided from the facts in evidence (42). 

28. Intention of natural consequences. It is proper to instruct that a person is presumed to intend the natural consequences of his acts, applying the law to the facts (43). 

29. Malice from killing after a threat.-It is proper to instruct that a killing after a threat is presumed to be malicious and is in consequence of the previous threat (44). 

30. A person intends to do what he does. The law presumes that every person intends to do what he does (Instruction given) (45). 

31. Fraud. One filing a false claim may reasonably be considered to have intended fraud (46). 

32. No presumption from carrying a weapon.—There is no presumption of criminality from a man's wearing a deadly weapon (47). 

33. Presumption from flight.-It is proper to instruct that guilty knowledge and fear may be inferred from the flight of the defendant (48). 

34. Chastity of prosecutrix in seduction.-Girls under 18 years of age are presumed prima facie to be chaste (49).

35. Age of consent.-Girls over 10 years of age are presumed to be capable of consenting to sexual intercourse (50). 

36. No burglarious intent from manipulating a window.-Manipulating and rattling a window, which the defendant sought to enter, does not warrant the inference that the defendant intended burglary (51). 

37. Possession of goods recently stolen.-It is proper to instruct that the unexplained possession of recently stolen goods will support an inference by the jury that the possessor is guilty of larceny. It is a question of fact as to whether the possession was recent (52). 

38. Embezzlement from shortage.-It is proper to instruct that a shortage of funds held by an officer of State makes a prima facie case of embezzlement. A decision contra is found in a case involving an employee of a business corporation (53). 

39. Sale of liquor from possession of appliances. 

It is proper to instruct that possession of appliances for the sale of liquor is presumptive evidence of selling liquor unlawfully. The vanish theory applies (54). 

40. Inference of knowledge from possession of liquor. -It is permissible for the jury to infer knowledge from the possession of liquor (55). 

41. Sale of liquor from Federal liquor license.-Under Section 1747, Code, 1906, it is proper to instruct that ownership of a Federal liquor license constitutes presumptive evidence of being engaged in keeping intoxicants for sale, but apparently this statute was dropped from the Code of 1930 though such evidence is still relevant (56). 

42. Violation of liquor law from keeping or selling in house owned or leased.-Unlawful keeping of liquor in a building or boat constitutes presumptive evidence of guilt of the owner, lessee, sublessee, or occupant who does not give information required by statute, when he knows these facts (57).

Presumptions 

53 43. Prescription records.-Prescription records for liquor are prima facie true (58). 

44. Possession of burglar's tools. By statute the carrying of concealed burglar's tools is prima facie evidence of intention to use them for burglary (59). 

45. Possession of opium or appliances for its sale.- 

By statute, possession of opium cocaine, or their derivatives, or appliances adapted to their sale is presumptive evidence of keeping the same for sale (60). 

46. Penning cow or calf.-By statute, penning another's milch cow or calf is prima facie evidence of an intent to procure milk illegally (61). 

47. Reputation of house of ill fame charges owners, lessees, occupants and agents with knowledge. By statute, owners, lessees, occupants, and agents of houses of ill fame are charged with knowledge of its reputation, and the ill repute is prima facie evidence of their participation in the criminal activity thereof (62). 

48. Possession of hunting or fishing equipment.- 

By statute, possession of hunting or fishing equipment, or dead game in field or stream or highway is prima facie evidence of hunting or fishing (63). 

48-a. Unlawful taking of oysters.-Where a vessel is operated with propelling power other than sail or hand while on the public reefs of the state catching or taking oysters or when the oyster-taking device is in the water, or while the propelling machinery is unsealed, the captain “shall be presumed prima facie to be taking or catching oysters in violation of the law," under Ch. 213, Sec. 1, Laws, 1940. 

49. Knowledge that land is posted.-Posting land for three months shall constitute prima facie evidence of knowledge of the fact by a trespasser (64). 

50. Gross usury from charging over the legal rate. -Charging interest over the legal rate is prima facie

evidence of charging more than 20 per cent, which works forfeiture of principal and interest (65). 

51. Unfair trade practice from discriminatory rates. -Unfair trade practice is shown prima facie by discriminatory charges in certain localities, by trusts and combines (66). 

52. Unsafeness of railway from declaration of railway commission. Declaration by railway commission of unsafeness of railway is prima facie evidence of culpable negligence, justifying punitive damages (67). 

iv. Procedural 

53. Personal jurisdiction.-A judgment of a court of general jurisdiction is presumed to be based on jurisdiction over the persons affected. A different rule prevails in courts of special jurisdiction (68). 

54. Return true. There is a prima facie presumption that an officer's return is true (69). 

55. Jurisdiction of courts of general jurisdiction.- 

There is a presumption that a judgment of a court of general jurisdiction as contradistinguished from courts of special jurisdiction is based on the proper jurisdictional facts. This presumption is conclusive on collateral attack (70). 

Proceedings to annul a judgment and attacks for fraud in procurement are not collateral within this rule (71). 

56. Jurisdictional recitals of courts of special jurisdiction. Recitals of jurisdictional and of other facts by courts or bodies of special jurisdiction are prima facie true (72) similarly, of courts of general jurisdiction (73). 

57. Pleading judgment of courts of special jurisdiction. For purposes of pleading, judgments of courts of special jurisdiction are taken to be based on jurisdictional facts; on the trial the facts are to be shown (74).

Presumptions 

55 

58. Time of day of a judgment. It is presumed that a judgment was rendered on the earliest possible hour in the course of the court (75). 

59. Scope of judgment must appear.-There is no presumption as to the scope of a judgment unless the facts appear expressly therein, or, it would seem, by necessary implication (76). 

60. No abandonment bankrupt jurisdiction. There is no presumption a trustee in bankruptcy has abandoned a claim to a constructive trust and that Federal jurisdiction has been relinquished merely because the claim might be unprofitable (77). 61. 

Preliminary procedural presumptions.—In gen eral, it is presumed that the proper procedure has been observed in returning indictments and other preliminaries. Specifically, it is presumed that proper steps were taken in empanelling the grand jury (78), returning the indictment into court (79), execution and return of process (80), drawing of the jury by the Board of 

Supervisors (81), good faith of bailiff summoning the jury (82). 

62. Improper communications with the jury.-Improper communications are presumed to be prejudicial (83). 

63. Prejudice from kinship of juror to a party.- 

It is presumed that kinship of a juror to a party to the suit is prejudicial (84). 

64. Error in favor of co-defendant.—It is presumed that the jury were not misled by an erroneous peremptory instruction for a co-defendant upon a different issue which did not affect the defendant (85). 65. 

Trial procedure. (See Sec. 103, below) In general it is presumed in absence of a contrary showing, that the trial procedure was proper.

Specifically it is presumed that the following procedures were properly taken:

Having defendant present at view in criminal trial (86). (87). (89).

Having defendant present at preliminary hearing 

Declining to change venue (88).

Securing another than the regular judge to act Qualification and acceptation or nonacceptation of jurors (90). 

Refusal of continuance (91). 

Securing costs as ruled (92). 

Quashing a bond (93). 

Courts criticizing an attorney before the jury. (94).

That the court informed the jury of the nature of the charge (95). 

Allowing new parties to be joined (96). 

Allowing amendments (97). 

Allowing a demurrer to be withdrawn (98). 

Dismissing a case (99). 

Granting proper instructions (100). 

Proper decree was entered (101). 

Denying writ of habeas corpus (102). 

Confirming partition sale (103). 

Refusing to sign a bill of exceptions (104). 

Due docketing of motion ruled on (105).

Hearing before a jury of a reward-claim in another case (106). 

Adjournment from day to day (107).

And generally all other matters where the record does not show facts on which a verdict, judgment or decree is based (108). 

66. Amount of verdict, of verdict, and arbitrariness.-The amount of a verdict is presumed to be correct unless the court can say it is so improper as to show passion or prejudice.

Presumptions 

57 An arbitrary verdict of guilty is presumed to be based on a sense of community vengeance (109). 

67. Influence of trial judge. It is presumed that the expression of the leaning of the trial judge is prejudicial (110). 

68. Chancellor's findings.-A chancellor's findings of fact are presumed to be correct (111). 

69. Referee's report.-A referee's report is prima facie evidence of the matter therein (112). 

69-a. Eminent Domain Award. Compensation awarded in eminent domain proceedings is conclusively presumed to include all damages resulting from the taking of the land and its legitimate use afterward (112-a). 

70. Judgment.—A judgment is presumed to be correct (113). 

71. Rulings of law. There is no presumption of the correctness of a ruling of law. The application of law to facts is presumed to be correct (114). 

71-a. Inquiry of fact by officer issuing search warrant.—In reviewing record it is presumed an officer issuing a search warrant inquired into the facts before the issuance of the warrant where the affidavit contains matters of fact sufficient on its face (114-a). 

72. Pleadings support judgment and writs.-It is presumed that the pleadings support writs and judgments (115). 

73. Presumption of the correctness of action of trial judge is rebuttable. The presumption of the correctness of the action of the trial judge is rebuttable (116). 

74. Construction of pleadings.—Pleadings are taken most strongly against the pleader (117). 

75. Nonpayment of alimony.-Nonpayment of alimony is prima facie evidence of contempt of court (118).

Presumptions Regarding Evidence 

76. Truth of offered evidence.—Evidence offered is taken as true for the purposes of relevancy; and when admitted is taken as true for the purpose of giving hypothetical instructions for the offering party (119). 

77. Disfavor toward policies requiring exclusion of evidence. Every intendment is made against the exclusion of evidence because of public policy, and the extrinsic policy must be specific and clear before it prevails over the search for the truth (120). 

78. Nonproduction of witnesses.-Nonproduction of a friendly witness or of one not accessible to the other side warrants an inference that the witness' testimony would be adverse. This rule applies to the failure of a party to call his physician, and an instruction has been held proper to this effect (121). The rule does not apply where the witness is accessible to both sides (122). 

It is error to comment in argument on the failure of the opponent to introduce a witness equally available to the other side (122-a). It does not apply in certain privileged relations, as when the defendant does not testify (123). 

See other specific privileges for rulings in regard to comment on the exercise of the privilege. 

79. Fabrication of evidence. It is admissible to show a party's fabrication or suppression of proof, for this is some evidence tending to show the facts are against the party (124). 

80. Communications between husband and wife. 

Communications between husband and wife when alone are presumed to be confidential and privileged (125). 

81. Defendant's testifying voluntary. It is presumed that the defendant's testifying in a criminal case was voluntary until the contrary is proved (126). 

82. Presumptions for a party conflicting with his proof.-Proof offered by a party inconsistent with pre-

Presumptions 

59 sumptions in his favor destroys the presumptions. Obiter: unsuccessful attempts to prove presumed facts destroy the presumption (127). 83.

Waiver of objection by not requesting ruling. -An objection is waived if a ruling is not insisted on (128). 

84. Ruling on evidence presumed to be correct. 

Rulings on evidence are presumed to be correct unless the contrary is shown by the record (129). 

On Appeal 

85. Record complete.-On appeal the record of evidence is presumed prima facie to be complete (130). 

86. Statements of court true.-Statements of the court in the record as to what counsel said as to the defendant's not testifying are taken to be true (131). 

87. Steps in inferior appeal. It is presumed in the

Supreme Court that an appeal to the Circuit Court was properly taken, from an inferior court (132). 88.

Presumption in favor of appellee.-Conflicting evidence on appeal is resolved in favor of the appellee if at all reasonable and not conjectural (133). 

89. Majority vote required to reverse.-The decision of the trial court will not be reversed until a majority of the judges of the Supreme Court participating so vote (134).

Miscellaneous 

90. On motion for peremptory instruction. In ruling on a motion for a peremptory instruction the facts established by the evidence of the opponent of the motion are taken as true, together with reasonable inferences therefrom (135).

91. Damage from infringement of a right. The law presumes damage from the infringement of a legal right (136). 

92. No presumption of payment of a judgment. 

There is no presumption that a nine-months old judgment has been paid (137). 

93. Levy.-A levy is prima facie satisfaction of a judgment (138). 

94. Place for execution sale.-There is no presumption that an order was obtained authorizing a marshal of the United States to sell land at the Federal Court House instead of at the County Court House, or that the price was paid or a deed executed (139). 

95. Bond at execution sale. In ejectment against a purchaser at an execution sale, it is not presumed that the bond was given and approved in the execution sale and that the sale was valid (140). 

96. Sheriff's deed. A sheriff's deed to land is prima facie evidence that the requisite steps were taken (141). 

97. Damage from failure to return execution.-The amount of the judgment is prima facie the amount of damages for failure to return an execution on it (142).

Conflicting Presumptions 

98-a. Theory.-Presumptions of equal dignity neutralize one another. The presumption an officer performed his duty in delivering an execution to his successor and the presumption that the successor performed his duty by making return on the execution are of equal dignity (142-a). 

98-b. Correctness decision of the trial court; innocence. Where the defendant is found guilty the presumption of the correctness of the trial decision overcomes the presumption of innocence (143).

Presumptions 

61 

99. Correctness of ruling on evidence in motion for peremptory instruction; truth of evidence of the opponent of the motion. Since there is no presumption of the correctness of the ruling of the trial court on a point of law, the presumption on appeal that the trial ruling is correct on motion for a peremptory instruction goes to the weight of evidence submitted to the court for decision, and interferes with the presumption that the opponent's evidence is to be considered as true, together with the reasonable inferences therefrom (144). 

100. Oath to perjurer; innocence.-The presumption of innocence is stronger than the presumption that officers did their duty and administered an oath to the defendant as witness before a grand jury. The state must prove the oath was administered in an action for perjury (145). 

101. Statute in force entire day of enactment; innocence. The presumption of innocence is stronger than the presumption that a statute was in force the entire day of its enactment; and the State must prove the exact time of enactment in prosecuting the defendant for an act on that day (146). 

102. Continued life of first spouse; innocence.-The presumption of innocence, in contracting a second marriage, prevails over the presumption of the continued life of the first spouse. Actual life of the first spouse must be shown on the day of the second marriage of the other party (147). 

102-a. Chastity; correctness birth and death certificates. In reviewing evidence it is held that there is a presumption of honesty and chastity on the part of an unmarried woman and against her being pregnant, and the jury is entitled to consider this presumption as against the prima facie presumption of the correctness of a death certificate stating that she had been pregnant six months at the time of her death, three months after marriage (147-a).

Official Acts 

103. Regularity.—In general, official acts are presumed to have been done properly. Specifically, the following are presumed to have been done correctly: 

Management of election (148).

Marks on ballots, other than those signifying the vote (149). 

Designation of place of election (150).

Governor will sign claimant's warrant for service on building commission (151).

Issuance of warrant for extradition (152). 

Pardoning power will be used in proper case (153). 

Municipal allowance of a salary (154). 

Municipal contract (155). 

Municipal proceeding to forfeit charter (156). 

Valuation of property by officer in replevin (157).

Execution writ of replevin in proper locality (158). 

Correct procedure in returning writ of attachment (159). 

Finding of cause of death by a coroner (160). 

Imprisonment (161). 

True receipt for prisoners hired (162). 

Arrest (163).

Constable acts as constable, although also a deputy sheriff (164). 

County will maintain county road (165). 

Making rules for schools by trustees (166). (167).

Attendance of pupils at school of another district Certificates of election of official character, true (168). 

Official certificates, true (169). 

Notary's certificates, giving address (170). 

Delivery of letter duly mailed (171).

Acts of Board of Development (171-a).

Presumptions 

63 

104. Failure to exhibit privilege license.-A transient's failure to exhibit his privilege license on officer's demand is prima facie evidence he has none (172). 

105. Presumptions in regard to taxation.- (a) TAXES.-Taxes are presumed by the court reviewing evidence to be reasonable until the contrary appears (173). 

(b) LEVY AUTHORIZED.-A tax levy is presumed to be authorized (174). (c) 

ASSESSMENTS VALID.-Estimates of value and damage of land in a drainage district are prima facie correct (175). 

Ad valorem assessment rolls are prima facie valid (176). 

(d) RECITALS.-Recitals in publication in drain age district proceedings are prima facie true (177). (e) NO EXEMPTION.-There is no presumption of tax exemption (178). 

(f) TAX SALE LISTS.-Tax collectors' conveyances and lists of land sold are prima facie evidence that the assessment and sale are valid, but not of notice to redeem (179). 

Tax collectors' lists of persons delinquent on poll tax are prima facie true (180). 

(g) REDEMPTION CERTIFICATES.-Redemption cer tificates are prima facie evidence of payment of proper amounts (181). 

(h) SHERIFF'S TAX CREDITS. Supervisors' tax credits in favor of the sheriff are presumed to be correct (182). 

(i) DISTRICT HAS FUNDS.-In proceedings against drainage district it is presumed that the district has funds (183).

(j) USE FOREIGN LICENSE TAG.—Use of foreign li cense tag for longer than thirty days is prima facie evidence of an intent to evade the tax law (184). 

(k) MOTOR FUEL USE.-Motor fuel brought into the state is presumed to be brought for use therein (185). 

106. Utility rates.-Utility rates are presumed to be reasonable (186). 

107. Treasurers' books.-Treasurers' books are prima facie correct (Instruction given) (187).

V. Presumptions Regarding Land 

108. Patents.-A patent raises a presumption that all prerequisites to its issuance have been complied with (188). 

109. Sixteenth section lease.-Leases of sixteenth section lands raise a presumption that prerequisites have been complied with (189). 

110. Enrollment of Indian.-Enrollment of an Indian by a register under a treaty is presumed to be done after complying with all prerequisites (190). 

111. Plats. References in deeds to a plat raises the presumption that it was filed, where the Court House records have been burned (191).

An ancient plat is presumed to have been regularly filed although not properly certified (192). 

112. Conveyances to several grantees.-Conveyances to several grantees are construed to create estates in common unless a different intention is manifested (193). 

113. Words of inheritance unnecessary.-In conveyances, estates granted are deemed to be in fee simple unless a less estate is clearly shown to be granted, although common-law words of inheritance be not used (194).

Presumptions 

65 

114. Recitals of trustees' deeds.-It is proper to instruct recitals of trustees' deeds are presumed to be true and the deed is prima facie valid (195).

Omission to recite a step where others are recited rebuts the presumption of the correctness of a trustee's deed (196). 

115. Lost grant from long possession.-Twenty years' possession and dominion of land raises a presumption of a lost grant (197). 116. 

Lost records.-Commissioners' restorations of lost records are prima facie true (198). 

117. Partition in kind.—The court presumes that land may be fairly divided (199). 

118. Lien extinguished by cancellation on record.- 

A lien of a deed of trust is presumed to be extinguished by cancellation on the record (200). 

119. Vendor's lien waived by taking independent security.—It is a fair inference from the taking of a deed of trust on part of the property sold by the beneficiary and the making of a large down payment that the vendor's lien did not extend to property not included in the deed of trust (201). 

Taking other security may impliedly waive a vendor's lien (202). 

120. Lien note and limitations. Before the present statute, Section 2290, Code of 1930, the lien of a mortgage was not presumed barred with the note (203). 

121. Ownership from possession.-Possession is presumptive evidence of ownership or of holding in subordination to the owner (Instruction given) (204). 

122. Tenancy.—Prima facie a building on leased land belongs to the lessor and an insured tenant must prove ownership under a policy requiring that the insured be owner (205).

Acceptance of rent prima facie establishes the relation of landlord and tenant (206). 

123. Merger.-When an owner acquires a lien on the owned property it is presumed the two merge, in law; and also in equity, unless the owner will be hurt thereby (207). 

124. Possession with deed.-Possession is presumed to pass with a deed; and to be coextensive with the boundaries named in the deed (Instruction corrected) (208). 

124-a. Overlapping, constructive possession. In reviewing the record, overlapping constructive possession from occupancy of part of a tract described in a deed does not aid either party. Hence, it seems there is no presumption (208-a). 

125. Knowledge of content of deeds.-A purchaser is presumed to know the content of deeds in his chain of title (209). 

126. Complicity of owner in liquor sale.-By statute, the owner of a building is presumed to wink at the sale of liquor therein (210). 

127. Open taking of property.-It is error not to instruct that the open taking of property in the presence of the owner carries with it only evidence of trespass (211). 

128. Abandonment.-Abandonment is shown prima facie where property is deserted for three years (212). 

129. Sentry. It is presumed that a railway consented to the placing of a sentry on its bridge in war-time (213). 

130. Presumption against adverse possession.-Every possession of land is presumed to be in subordination to the legal title and by permission of the owner (Instruction granted) (214).

A co-tenant's possession is not presumed to be adverse to the other tenant's (215).

Presumptions 

67 131. Notice of adverse holding.-The true owner is presumed to be on notice of adverse possession of the entire tract, but not of an inconsiderable part (216). 

132. Assessment of taxes and adverse possession. 

The assessment of taxes as distinguished from payment is no evidence of adverse possession (217). 

133. Adverse holding of sixteenth section land.-Adverse possession of sixteenth section lands for twenty-five years is prima facie evidence of a legally valid lease (218). 

vi. Presumptions in Tort 

134. In general.—The mere proof of a hurt raises no presumption of negligence as the cause (219). 

135. Giving signals at railway crossing.—There is a common-law presumption that a railway company did its duty and gave signals at a crossing. The presumption gives way when all the facts are in evidence (220).

It is negligence not to give a signal three hundred yards or more from the crossing (Instruction given) (221). 

136. Speed. It is negligence to run a train at a speed faster than can be stopped in the distance lighted by the headlight (222). 

137. Obeying company rule.-There is a presumption that a company rule was obeyed requiring a train to wait ten minutes after the departure of an earlier train (223). 

138. Motor vehicles and crossing of railway.-Failure of a motor vehicle to stop before crossing a railway track shall not defeat recovery. In such cases the prima facie statute of this state shall apply (224).

Railway Injuries to Persons or Property Prima Facie Evidence of Negligence 

139. Rule. Section 1580, Code, 1930, provides that proof of injury by railway engines or cars shall be prima facie evidence of negligence (225).

The presumption is of statutory origin (226). 

The statute is constitutional (227). 

140. Scope. This statutory presumption applies only to cases governed by the laws of Mississippi and not to cases governed by the Federal Employers' Liability Act 

(228). 

141. Street railways.-The presumption applies to street railways (229). 

142. Cars moved by lever.-The presumption does not apply to harms received by railway cars being set in motion by another car which was moved by a bar used as a lever. "Lever-power" as used in the statute applies to hand cars (230). 

143. Electric shock.-The statute applies to cases of electric shock while the car was at rest (231). 

144. Crossing gates.-The presumption does not apply to harm inflicted by a descending crossing-gate (232). 

145. Running-board cases. The presumption does not apply to a case in which a person was leaning from a side running-board of an electric trolley car and was hit by a post, before the passage of the present statute expressly including cars run by electric power (233). 

146. Passengers and employees. The presumption extends to passengers and employees (234). 

147. Track of one company, train of another. The presumption applies to harms received where train and track belong to different companies (235). 

148. Livestock. The presumption is inapplicable to cases in which animals are hurt in running from trains, but not struck by the train. It applies where animals are struck (236). 

149. Fire. The presumption applies to cases of fire begun by trains (237).

Presumptions 

69 

150. Content of statute.-The statute requires the plaintiff to prove damage to person or property from the operation of railway trains in order for the presumption to arise (238).

This showing entitles the plaintiff to judgment if not explained (239).

The defendant is required only to disclose the facts, to meet the presumption; and where this disclosure is made liability must be based on the facts without benefit of the presumption (240).

When all the facts are disclosed by either party, the case must be decided on the facts alone (241).

The disclosure required to meet the presumption is a disclosure which the jury is without right to reject. It follows that if the jury deem the disclosure false they may follow the presumption. But the instruction applying the presumption to the facts should not be given merely because there is a conflict in the evidence and from which the jury is unable to determine how the damage was inflicted. The distinction is quite thin (242).

When such disclosure is made the presumption vanishes and no instruction on it should be given (243); the jury are then to decide the case from the facts (244). 

151. Particularity of disclosure required. The disclosure must be sufficient prima facie to rebut the statutory presumption (245).

It seems a prima facie showing is required of how the harm occurred, what the victim was doing, where he was when killed, and what the defendant did to prevent injury, and to disclose by evidence the doing or omission of every act from which prima facie an inference of negligence vel non could be drawn. The evidence of disclosure must be as broad as the presumption itself and must prima facie rebut every negligent act or omission which might under the circumstances reasonably or naturally have caused the fire. Unless this prima facie rebuttal and disclosure is made the plaintiff is entitled to a verdict. It should be noted that the foregoing paragraph is a summary of former decisions as modified by the new rule in the Walden Case, in the effort of

the writer to adapt the old decisions on particularity of rebuttal to the new statement that a prima facie rebuttal only is required. It seems this is the meaning of the 

Court. Decisions since the new rule are not numerous or very helpful (246). 

152. Decisions since the Walden Case (1931).—Al though the Walden Case expressly left open the situation of the applicability of the presumption to situations in which the facts are unascertainable; a more recent decision holds in effect that the presumption does not apply to cases in which all the facts cannot be disclosed, disclosure being made allegedly of all known facts; and hence throws doubt on the earlier decisions requiring a full disclosure, to meet the presumption, and even throwing doubt on the common-law presumption of negligence from harming passengers (247). 

153. Conflicting evidence.-A peremptory instruction for the defendant should not be given where the plaintiff's evidence was such that the jury might disbelieve the defendant's witness and apply the presumption, the presumption not being met by evidence which has been sufficiently discredited (248). 

Exception of Walden Case to the Rule of the Walden Case 

154. Exception. The court ruled, "There may be cases in which the statutory presumption controls, although rebutting evidence has been given by the defendant; for example, when the plaintiff's evidence does not disclose the facts and circumstances of his injury, and the jury has the right to, and does, reject the defendant's rebutting evidence." The court left open this situation and cited a case in which the jury was held to have had the right to reject the defendant's evidence as false (249). 

155. Rule 6 of the Thornhill Case overruled.-Rule 

6 of the Thornhill Case has been overruled. Rule 6 was to the effect that disclosure was not made unless the jury could determine from the evidence what the facts were.

Presumptions 

71 Exactly how much of this jurisdiction to decide whether a disclosure had been made remains is not clear, the 

Sparkman case deciding in effect that the undisclosed situation should not be submitted to the jury, the Walden 

Case in theory leaving the point open (250).

Res Ipsa Loquitur 

156. Theory.-In general, when an injury is caused by an apparatus in the control of the defendant of such character that would not ordinarily have been inflicted if the defendant had exercised due care in the construction, inspection and use of the apparatus, the jury may infer negligence, unless the defendant explains to the jury's satisfaction by showing no negligence on his part caused the harm (251).

Specific decisions applying this doctrine are not harmonious. 

157. Instances.-The doctrine applies to derailment of a train driven at 40 miles per hour on a rough track (252), to the falling of a piece of metal from a merchandise conveyor into a customer's eye (253), to the fall of a beam from a Yale and Towne hoist (254), the bottling of deleterious substances in a bottled drink (255), to the collapse of a railway platform (256), to burns from x-ray treatment (257), to electric shock to a passenger (258), to the runaway of horses unaccompanied by a driver (259), to the fall of live wires into a street (260), to a passenger's fall on a banana peeling which was on a platform after an employee had removed a banana peeling from the train and had gone to the platform to dispose of it (261). 

The presumption is inapplicable to a harm received by the wreck of a trailer which was insecurely fastened to a railway motor car (262), to the fall of a rotten window on the plaintiff (263), to the turning over of a step-box used on a train (264), to the falling of a passenger from a train (265), to the fall of an elevator when a cable broke (266), to the explosion of a coca-cola bottle when the plaintiff took it out of the ice-box (267), to a

fall by a patient in a hospital on a slick, waxed floor (268), though it does apply to a fall on a slick wet floor in an office (269), the presumption is inapplicable to a fall of runaway oxen into an open ditch on private land (270), to harm from unsafe tools unless the defect was reasonably discoverable and the employer had notice of it (271), to the splintering of an anvil (272), to an explosion from pouring oil into a smoking, hot box, when the machinery had stopped and the oil could not circulate (273), or to dumping of a car of dirt on brakeman where valve was open (273-a). 

158. Cities. The presumption of Res Ipsa Loquitur does not apply to cities, but it has been applied to a city in operating an electric power plant (274). 

159. Function of jury.-It is proper to submit to the jury the question whether harm results from specified apparatus if used with due care, and to authorize them to consider the resulting prima facie presumption of negligence along with the other evidence if they find that with due care no harm normally follows the use of the apparatus, in cases within the presumption (275).

Miscellaneous 

160. Knowledge of defect in street. After a reasonable time a city is presumed to know of a defect in a street (276). 

161. Habitual use of railway right of way.-The habit of factory employees in crossing a railway track near a factory is considered to be known to the railway company (277). 

162. Curiosity of children.—The curiosity of children for attractive nuisances is presumed conclusively to be known (278). 

163. Habitual speeding on a bridge.-A trespasser on a railway bridge is not presumed to know that the train will pass over the bridge at an excessive speed, because in the past the custom was so to run (279).

Presumptions 

73 

164. Relation of licensee and invitee. It is inferred by the court on appeal that a person on a railway dump on business which could not be of profit to the railway is a licensee. Where the advantage is mutual the relation is taken to be that of an invitee (280). 

165. Due care toward invitee.-There is a presumption in favor of the exercise of due care toward an invitee, unless the contrary is shown (281). 

166. Incapacity of children to commit an act of contributory negligence.—A child ten or twelve years old is prima facie incapable of contributory negligence (282). 

167. Relation of employer-employee.-A person working on the defendant's premises for the benefit of the defendant is prima facie an employee of the defendant (283). 

167-a. Employee in intrastate commerce.—In reviewing the record, "If pleadings are silent as to character of employment, whether in interstate or intrastate-the presumption is that the employment was within the state and that the employee was engaged in intrastate commerce" (283-a). 

168. Injured employee acted in scope of employment. -It is presumed prima facie that an employee who was injured was acting in the scope of his employment (284). 

169. Safe place to work furnished. It is presumed a master furnished an employee a safe place and tools to work (285). 

170. No assumption of risk by inexperienced employee. An inexperienced employee is not presumed to assume the ordinary risks of his employment (286). 

171. Foreign substances in drink. The presence of a harmful foreign substance in a bottled drink makes a prima facie case of negligence. It will not be presumed that third persons put the substance in the sealed container provided by the manufacturer (287).

172. 

Automobile accidents.-Violation of motor vehicle law resulting in harm makes a prima facie case of negligence (288). This presumption vanishes when all the facts are shown (289). 

The presumption must be pleaded (290).

The presumption does not apply between fellow-employees (291). 

173. Presumption auto driver will obey the law.—

It is presumed that an auto driver will obey the law by stopping at a railway crossing, and a guest is not contributorily negligent in not remonstrating in advance with the driver (292). 

174. Incompetence of habitual drunkard to drive a car. A habitual drunkard is presumed to be incompetent to drive a car, and one who permits him to drive is charged with knowledge of his incompetence (293). 

175. Employer in state having workmen's compensation act is subject to the act.-It is presumed an employer in a state having a workmen's compensation act is subject to the act (294). 

176. Fraud from advantage to a fiduciary at the expense of the beneficiary. It is prima facie a fraud for a fiduciary to receive a benefit from the person dependent on him, as, for a physician to have a will in his favor made by his patient, a deed from one partner to another in whom special confidence was reposed, a will by a ward in favor of the guardian (Instruction given that these factors and influences may be considered) (295). 

Good faith, full knowledge and independent consent and action by the beneficiary on disinterested advice must be shown (Instruction given) (296). 

177. Fraud from voluntary conveyance.-A voluntary conveyance is prima facie fraudulent as against existing creditors. It may be rebutted by proof that the grantor retained enough property to pay his debts. If the grantor is insolvent, the presumption is conclusive (297).

Presumptions 

75 The grantee is presumed prima facie to know of the fraud. Payment of an adequate price by the grantee raises a presumption of good faith on his part (298).

A voluntary conveyance to a wife by her husband is prima facie fraudulent as against a subsequent purchaser from the husband (299).

Notice to a trustee is in law notice to the beneficiary of fraud in conveyance to defeat creditors (300). 

178. Bulk sales.-Bulk sales by a private person without conforming to the statutes governing such sale are presumed to be fraudulent. This does not refer to official sales (301). 

179. Transfers within two years of death.—Transfers without consideration within two years of death are deemed prima facie to have been made in contemplation of death (302). 

180. Checks without funds on deposit. Upon ten days' written notice, failure to pay check drawn by one without funds on deposit is prima facie evidence of obtaining the amount of the check on false pretenses (303). 

181. Fraud in land sales.-Statements regarding material inducements to purchase of land which are recklessly made in conscious ignorance of the truth are prima facie fraudulent (304). 

182. Possession by judgment debtor.-Retention of possession by debtor of property sold at a judicial sale is prima facie evidence of fraud (305). 

183. By mortgagor.-Retention of possession by a mortgagor is a circumstance which may be considered on the issue of fraud on creditors (306). 

184. Sale of personalty.-Sale of seed by name raises an implied warranty that the seed is true to name (307). 

185. Devices to divert gas, water, or electricity around a meter.-Presence of devices to divert gas, water, or electricity around a meter is prima facie evidence of

an intent to defraud the utility company. Devices making meters read too high are evidence of fraud (308). 

186. Pretense of baggage in hotel.-Pretense of baggage or other property in a hotel and nonpayment of the bill or an effort to abscond constitute prima facie evidence of fraudulent intent in obtaining accommodations (309). 

187. Presumptions in defamation.-It is presumed that the plaintiff in defamation has a good character for the trait in question (310). 

188. Malicious prosecution.-Acquittal of a defendant, who later sues for malicious prosecution, is prima facie evidence of want of probable cause for the prosecution (Instructions given) (311). 

189. Want of probable cause and malice.-Want of probable cause to prosecute is not in law evidence of malice; it is a permissible inference of fact (312). 

Malice is not evidence of want of probable cause (313). 

190. Words per se slanderous.-The law implies legal malice from the speaking of words per se slanderous (Instruction given) (314). 

191. Privileged occasions.-On qualifiedly privileged occasions, words are presumed to be spoken in good faith, although ordinarily the words would be slanderous (315). 

vii. Presumptions in Contracts 

192. Legality.-Contracts are presumed to be legal (Instruction given) (316). 

193. Restraint of trade.-Contracts in restraint of trade are prima facie void as against the public interest (317). 

194. Capacity. Every person who attempts to enter a contract is presumed prima facie to have the capacity (318).

Presumptions 

77 

195. Knowledge of content.-Every person is presumed to know the content of his contract (319). 

196. Time and place of acceptance.-An acceptance by telegram is operative when the message is delivered to the telegraph company. Similarly, a mailed acceptance is effective from the time it is mailed (320). 

The place of execution is the place of acceptance (321). 

197. Seal.-A seal to an acknowledgment proves prima facie that the acknowledgment is properly executed (322). 

198. Attestation from place of signature.-A signature in the lower left corner of the last page is taken to be that of a witness (323). 

199. Renewal.-A renewal is presumed to be based on the same terms as the original (324). 

200. Insurance by-laws.-There is no presumption that insurance by-laws were of long standing (325). 

201. Delivery from recording.—Acknowledgment and recordation are prima facie evidence of the delivery of a deed by the grantor (326). 

202. Possession of the deed by the grantee; possession of property.-Possession and production by a grantee of an unrecorded deed are presumptive evidence of delivery by the grantor (327). 

Manual possession alone of a deed by the grantee does not prove acceptance of the deed by him (328). 

Where the delivery of a deed is in issue, in reviewing the record in chancery it will not be presumed that the handing of the deed to a third person without more was a delivery to the named grantee (328-a). 

Execution of a deed is prima facie evidence of the grantee's possession of the property (329). 

203. Date of delivery.—The date of the deed is prima facie the date of its delivery (330).

204. Filing. It cannot be assumed that a deed was filed earlier than it was recorded in absence of proof of the fact (331). 

205. Stamps affixed. It is presumed that officers required to stamp deeds affixed the proper amount (332). 

206. Acknowledgments true.-Prima facie, acknowledgments of deeds are true (333). 

207. Affidavit to account.-Prima facie, an affidavit to an account is true (334). 

208. Account rendered and unobjected to as an account stated between merchants.-Between merchants accounts rendered become accounts stated if unobjected to; but not between merchant and customer (335). 

209. Implied promise from reception of benefits. 

One receiving benefits impliedly agrees to pay the reasonable worth, unless personal relations show otherwise (336). 

210. Goods to infants.-There is no presumption that goods furnished infants are necessaries (337). 

211. Integration.-Antecedent agreements are merged in the written contract (338); and cannot be shown (338-a). 

212. Custom.-Established custom is part of a contract (339).

Construction 

213. Stipulations dependent.-Stipulations are presumed to be dependent (340). 

214. Ejusdem generis.-General words following specific words are limited to the same class (341). 

215. Insurance contracts, bills of lading, deeds. 

Doubts are construed against insurance companies, railway companies, and grantors of deeds (342).

Presumptions 

79 

216. Common meaning of words.-Statutes use words in the common acceptation (343). 

217. Consistent use of words.-Words are taken to have the same meaning throughout a writing (344). 

218. Doubts of law, in favor of the State.-Doubts of law should be resolved in favor of the State (345). 

219. Tax statutes.-Excise-tax statutes are construed in favor of the tax-payer (346). 

220. Recitals.-Recitals of consideration or value or interest paid are prima facie correct (347). 

221. Executory contract, and ownership of property. -An executory contract to deliver bonds is some evidence that the party to deliver did not then own them (348). 

222. Bank accounts.-The named depositor is prima facie the owner of the bank deposit (349). 

223. A deposit is general.-A bank deposit is presumed to be general unless expressly made special (350).

Payment and Performance 

224.

From From possession.-Possession of land for twenty years after the time appointed for payment raises the presumption that payment was made (351). 

225. Receipt.-A receipt is prima facie evidence of payment (Instruction given) (352). Whether the payment is in full depends on the circumstances (353). 

226. Receipt of a note.-Receipt of a note made by a third party is taken to be as a payment of a debt unless the contrary appears (354). 

227. Tenant was paid market price for cotton.-In an action of discovery brought by a landlord it is presumed that a credit for a tenant's cotton was computed at the market price (355).

228. No payment presumed from possession of mortgagor of personalty.-No payment is presumed from possession by the mortgagor of personalty after default (356). 

229. Purchase money notes.-Past due purchase money notes are not presumed to be paid and the purchaser from the maker must inquire (357). 

230. Due payment by receiver. It is presumed a receiver will pay funds recovered on a surety bond as the court directs (358). 

231. Payment of board.—It is presumed that board was paid by one staying in a boarding house, if able to pay (359). 

232. Performance by contractor from fact work was done. There is a presumption of fact that contractor performed his contract from the fact that the required work was done (360). 

233. Place of performance.-The place of making is the place of performance, where the contract is silent (361). 

234. Sales. Retention of bill of lading with draft attached prima facie shows that the seller undertook to deliver at the destination and bore the risk of loss on the railway (362).

Miscellaneous 

235. Proper law.-Prima facie the proper law is the law of the place of performance of a contract unless it is void by that law, in which case the law of the place of formation controls, for the parties are presumed to contract in accordance with the law (363). 

236. Mitigation of damages. It is presumed that a wrongfully discharged employee who took other work got the best wages obtainable unless the contrary is shown by the other side (Instruction given) (364).

Presumptions 

81 Carriers 237. Notice of special importance of prompt delivery, liability of carrier.-Where notice is given a carrier of the special importance of prompt delivery of a shipment, he is conclusively presumed to have contracted with reference to the enlarged liability (Instruction given) (365). 

238. Receipt of goods from bill of lading.-A bill of lading in the hands of a bona fide holder for value is conclusive evidence of the receipt by the carrier of the goods described (366). 239.

Final carrier.-The final carrier is under a duty within thirty days of notice to furnish the consignee of damaged goods copies of complete shipping records, or else be presumed to have caused the damage (367). damage.-Proof 

240. Presumption from loss, damage. Proof that livestock or merchandise was shipped in a good condition but was received in a damaged condition raises a prima facie presumption of negligence by the railway company (368) (Instruction given). The final carrier must meet this presumption (369). Bailments 

241. Fire, loss.-There is no presumption of negligence from the loss of bailed goods by fire (370). 

Nonreturn of bailed goods or return in a damaged condition is prima facie evidence of negligence by the bailee (371). 

242. Telegraph company's compliance with I. C. C's regulations. It is presumed a telegraph company has complied with the regulations of the Interstate Commerce Commission and may stipulate the amount of damage in messages (372). 

243. Rates. It is presumed that a carrier acted in good faith in charging a through rate on part of a shipment unloaded at an intermediate point (373).

Negotiable Instruments 

244. Holder. Every holder is presumed to be a holder in due course, except of demand paper negotiated an unreasonable time after its issue (374). 

245. Value.-An indorsee before maturity is presumed to have given value for the paper (375). 

Every negotiable instrument is deemed prima facie to have been issued for a valuable consideration and every person whose signature appears thereon to have become a party thereto for value (376). 

246. Delivery.-Delivery is presumed prima facie from the introduction of a note in evidence. An instrument in the hands of a holder in due course is conclusively presumed to have been delivered. It is presumed prima facie to have been delivered on that date on which it was signed (377). 

247. Date. The date on an instrument is deemed prima facie to be the true date of the making, drawing, acceptance or indorsement of the instrument as the case may be (378). 

248. Execution by executor.-It is presumed that a note executed by an executor was not executed without authority of law, and the burden is on the one suing the executor personally to show want of authority on the executor's part (379). 

249. Form of execution by agent. The words "agent of H." are prima facie descriptio personae (380). 

250. Indorsement.-Except where an indorsement bears date after maturity; every negotiation is deemed prima facie to have been effected before the instrument was overdue (381).

An indorsement is presumed prima facie to have been made at the place where the instrument is dated (382).

A person signing is presumed to be an indorser if he does not sign as maker, drawer, or acceptor (383).

Presumptions 

83 251. Noting by notary.-It is presumed there was a noting by a notary within a reasonable time of presentment and protest, although the certificate was dated two years after presentment (384). 

252. Foreign deputy's notary.-A foreign deputy authorized by law to act for a notary is presumed to have acted regularly until the contrary is shown (385). 

253. Notice by mailing.-A proper notice, duly mailed in a post office or official box, is deemed to have been received (386). 

254. An acceptance for honor.-An acceptance for honor is deemed to be for the honor of the drawer (387). 

255. Wife as payee.-A wife as payee holds in her own right, the former presumption being repealed (388). 

256. Alteration.-An apparent alteration on a negotiable instrument is presumed to have been made after delivery and to avoid the instrument, and the holder must prove that it was authorized in fact. Apparent alterations in nonnegotiable instruments are presumed to be authorized in fact (Instruction given) (389). 

257. Possession of a forged check.-Possession of a forged check is prima facie evidence that the possessor forged it or procured it to be forged (390). 

258. Fraud.-The indorsee of a check obtained by fraud is presumed to be privy to it (391). 

259. Place of forgery.—It is presumed that an instrument was forged in the place in which it was uttered (392). 

260. Forgery by one in possession. It is not presumed that a person to whom a check is given to be delivered to the payee will commit a forgery, and the forgery will not be imputed to the payee, although he selected the person who committed the forgery (393).

261. Payment.-Payment is presumed from possession of a note by the maker (394).

Nonpayment is presumed from possession by a creditor or his representative (395). 

262. Value of a note.-The value of a note is prima facie its face value in conversion (396).

Agency 263. From acts; authority.-Acting in the interest of another for a considerable time raises a factual presumption of agency (397).

Authority to collect does not imply authority to prosecute a debtor criminally (398).

Authority to sell does not imply authority to exchange (399).

Authority to solicit insurance does not imply authority to vary the terms of policies (400). 

264. Auto wreck by employee.-A car wrecked by an employee is presumed to have been about the employer's business when wrecked (401). 

265. Ownership.-Ownership alone is not evidence that the owner of the car is responsible for the driver's negligence (402). 

266. Kinship of owner and driver.-Kinship of driver to owner does not imply agency of the driver for the owner (403).

Partnership 

267. In general.-All debts contracted in the partnership name are presumed prima facie to be partnership debts. The presumption may be met by proof that the contracting partner acted beyond the scope of the ordinary business of the firm (Instruction given) (404). 

A partner is not presumed to have authority to bind the firm as surety (405).

Presumptions 

85 Acceptance of profits accruing after the termination of a partnership is tacit assent to the contracting of the debts, and makes the party liable therefor (406). 

268. Husband and wife.-In operating a wife's plantation, the husband is held to be her agent, unless the instrument changing the relation is recorded (407). 

269. Louisiana.—Where a husband permits his wife to conduct her own business her contracts are inferred to be her contracts by Louisiana law (408). 

270. Attorneys. It is presumed that an attorney who files suit for a client has authority to do so and to conduct the litigation (409). His authority is presumed to continue until written notice to the contrary is filed (410). The scope of the presumption extends to matters in the due and orderly conduct of the suit. He has no presumptive authority to stay execution and destroy the lien of the judgment (411). 

Principal and Surety 

271. Principal and surety.-A guardian's inventory is competent evidence against the surety to show reception of assets (412).

A judgment against the principal is prima facie evidence against the surety (413).

A judgment discharging a principal discharges the surety (414). 

272. Order of execution. It is presumed execution was omitted against principal debtors by reason of insolvency and that a decree was correct in ordering execution against secondary debtors (415). Garnishment 

273. Rule.—A judgment against a garnishee is prima facie evidence of the debt and may be used by a creditor of the first garnishing creditor (416).

viii. Wills, etc. 

274. Attestation.-Attestation raises a presumption that other formalities of execution had been gone through with (417).

By signing, an attester signifies that he saw the testator sign or acknowledge the signing and that the attesters believed he had testamentary capacity (418).

Proof of testator's capacity and of the execution of the will makes a prima facie case of the validity of the will (419). 

Probate. Probate of a will is prima facie evidence of its validity (420). 

275. Destruction from Disappearance. That a will traced to the testator's possession cannot be found after his death raises a presumption that the testator revoked and destroyed it (421). 

276. Testator's sanity.-A testator is presumed to be sane (422). Adjudication of a person's insanity is not conclusive evidence of testamentary incapacity, but is presumptive evidence of incapacity (423). 

277. Absence of undue influence.-Proof of testamentary capacity and of the mechanical execution of a will raises a prima facie presumption of the absence of undue influence (424). This presumption is met when evidence tending to show undue influence is introduced (425). 

278. Intestacy.-In construing a will it is presumed that the testator intended all his property to be disposed of by the will (426).

In partition by an heir intestacy is presumed (427). Administration 279. (a) Appointment.-When a court appoints an administrator de bonis non, it is presumed that a vacancy existed in the office of administrator (428).

Presumptions 

87 (b) Bond.-A new guardian's bond is presumed to have been regularly approved although the record is silent as to the summons, appearance, and orders of court, and the circumstances mentioned in the statute, Sections 

1211, 1212, Code of 1871 (429). (c) Publication. Where an administrator's own claim is presented eleven months after the estate was opened, it is presumed that publication for creditors was made promptly and that the time for probate was past (430). (d) Noncollection of claim.-After a reasonable time for the collection of a debt is passed, it is presumed noncollection is negligence on the part of the personal representative (431). (e) Deed.-An administrator's deed and long occupancy of the land conveyed by the deed implies that the administrator sold the land with due authority (Instruction given) (432). (f) No assets.-There is no presumption that an administrator has assets, from the fact a judgment has been recovered against him (433). Gifts 

280. In general.—A gift is presumed when a parent delivers personal property to a child soon after marriage (Instruction granted) (434). 

281. Advancement.-Money or other property turned over by a father to a son or a husband to his wife is presumed to be an advancement (435). 

282. Chose in action.-Delivery of an insurance policy prima facie is a gift (436). 

283. Legacy to creditor.-A legacy to a creditor is presumed to be meant to be applied on the debt (437). 

284. Board.-Board by a step-father to step-child is presumed to be a gift where the step-father went into

possession of the estate of the father of the step-children, for twenty years, and made no accounting (438). 

285. Personal services in family.-Personal services to a sick member of a family are presumed to be gratis (439). 

286. Resulting trusts.-A resulting trust is implied from the use of another's money in the purchase of property. If the title holder be a dependent or natural object of bounty, a gift is presumed. Payment of consideration by a purchaser is prima facie evidence of no notice of the resulting trust (440). 

ix. Physical Facts 

287. Knowledge that wet wood in the open rots is presumed (441).

It is presumed an indelible pencil mark cannot be erased without leaving a trace (442). 

288. Common use of tools.-Common use of a tool makes it reasonably certain that it is reasonably safe (443).

X. Status and Condition 

289. Building.-A year after a fire a building is presumed to be in the same condition as just after the fire (444).
