290. Personal disability.-Under provision of policy that disability should be deemed permanent when it lasted six months, this presumption is conclusive when six-months' disability is shown, although later the insured person recovered (445). 

291. Scope of acts of invitee.-An invitee in a store is presumed to have continued in this status (446). 

292. Continuance of office of administrator.-An administrator is presumed to continue in office until the estate is closed (447).

Presumptions 

89 

293. Continued possession.-Possession is presumed to continue until the contrary is shown. A merchant who sells cotton the day a writ of injunction was served on him must prove that he sold it before service, where it had been shown he was in possession of it that day (448). 

294. Abandonment.-Absence less than ten years does not imply abandonment of property (449). 

295. Illicit sex relations.-A meretricious relation is presumed to continue until the relation is shown matrimonial (Instruction given) (450). 

296. Insanity.-Insanity of the complete and permanent type is presumed to be continuous (451). The rule does not apply to temporary or intermittent types (452). 

297. Identity and name.-Identity of name is prima facie evidence of identity of person (453). 

298. Married name. There is no presumption that 

Mrs. J. E. Sabin is the wife of J. E. Sabin (454). 

299. Family name.-It does not follow that J. B. 

Jones is father to J. W. Jones (455). 

300. Death from absence.-A person absent from the

State or concealed within the State for seven years without being heard of is presumed to be dead, unless proof that he was alive within that time be offered (Instruction given) (456). 

The presumption does not apply to children aged three to seven years (457).

There is no presumption as to the exact time of death within the seven-year period (458). 

301. Death in common disaster.-There is no presumption of survivorship where all die in a common disaster (459), a concessive decision. Facts must be shown indicating which one died first (460).

302. Death from administration.-Death is pre sumed prima facie from the grant of letters of administration (461). 

303. Presumption against suicide.-There is a presumption against suicide (462). 

304. Validity of marriage.-Proof of holding out as married is proof prima facie of a legal marriage, except in cases of bigamy and criminal conversation (Instruction given) (463). 

A marriage is presumed to be valid (464). 

305. Presumption against bigamy. The presumption of the validity of a second marriage prevails over the presumption of the continuation of life of the first spouse of one of the marrying parties. This presumption is rebutted by proof that the parties to the first marriage are living and are not divorced (465).

Where a ceremonial marriage has been celebrated between a man and a woman who had at the time a living husband undivorced and whose whereabouts for 2 or 3 years afterwards was unknown, the law presumes in favor of the second marriage that the former husband has either obtained a divorce or is dead, notwithstanding that his absence has been for a period less than seven years (465-a). 

306. Husband and wife live together. It is presumed that husband and wife live together, unless the contrary is shown (466). 

307. Capacity to marry.-Every man is presumed to have mental capacity to marry (467). 

308. Legitimacy of issue.-A child begotten or born during wedlock is presumed to be legitimate. The presumption may be overcome by proof of nonintercourse of the husband and wife (468).

A child conceived before but born after wedlock is presumed to be legitimate (469).

A child conceived in wedlock but born after divorce is prima facie legitimate (470).

Presumptions 

91 

309. Domicile.-Domicile is presumed to continue 

310. Good faith of parent in alienating child's affections from husband or wife.-A parent is presumed to act from proper motives in inducing a child to leave a spouse (472). 

311. Possession of a husband or a wife.-Liquor in the joint physical possession of husband and wife is presumed to be in the control of the husband (473). The husband is presumed to own property found in the joint possession of husband and wife (474). 

312. Former rule on separate acknowledgments.—

Formerly a husband was presumed to control a wife who acknowledged an instrument in his presence, hence the former rule on separate acknowledgments (475). 

xi. Corporations 

313. Town acts under charter.-An incorporated town is presumed to act under the charter until shown to have come under a statute (476).

A charter power to sue implies a power to make bond in attachment (477). 

314. By-Laws.-By-laws are presumed to be in harmony with the charter (478). 

315. Foreign dissolution.-By statute a dissolved for eign corporation is presumed to be in existence three years in this state, to permit its creditors to reach its assets (479). 

316. Copies of constitution and laws.-Printed copies of constitution and laws, certified by the secretary or corresponding officer, are prima facie evidence of the legal adoption thereof (480). 

317. Authority of officers.-The president is presumed to have authority to bind the corporation by entering any contract the corporation has power to make (481).

A banker cashier has power to transact the active business of a bank, in this case, to pledge bonds to secure a deposit of public funds (482). 

A corporate secretary has no presumptive authority to pay his personal debts with corporate assets (483). 

318. No notice of fraud of fiduciary using assets for personal advantage.-By statute, a bank dealing with a fiduciary is not deemed to have notice of want of authority of the fiduciary to use trust funds for his personal advantage by reason of the fact of such application (484). 

319. Knowledge of corporate power. The public dealing with a corporation is presumed to have knowledge of its corporate power (485). 

320. Value in taxation. In the franchise tax, book value is prima facie the correct value of the corporate capital (486).

C. CIRCUMSTANTIAL EVIDENCE 

i. Criminal Law; Fraud 

1. Threats.-A deceased person's communicated and uncommunicated threats to kill the defendant are admissible to show the state of the decedent's mind and the character of his act in the fatal encounter, where the evidence conflicts as to which party committed the overt act of aggression (487).

An overt act of the decedent endangering the defendant must be shown before his threats are admissible (488).

The defendant's communicated and uncommunicated threats to kill the decedent are admissible to show the state of mind, wilfulness, and intent to murder, and aggression, where the evidence conflicts as to which party committed the overt act of aggression (489). 

2. Details. The details which accompanied threats are inadmissible, except insofar as necessary to make the threats intelligible. This means that the occasion and

Circumstantial Evidence 

93 facts may be shown in general outline without the development of detail (490). 

3. Conditional threats.-Conditional threats are admissible in cases in which the conditions have been fulfilled. The court leaves open the question whether such threats would be admissible where the conditions have not been fulfilled (491). 

4. Indefinite threats.-Threats not referring to ascertainable persons or not declaring that the speaker would do the harm spoken of are too indefinite to be admissible (492). 

5. Statement amounting to a threat.-The deceased's statement that he would carry a controverted point with the defendant or go to hell before sundown is a threat (493); defendant's statement 3 or 4 hours before the killing that he was going to kill somebody is admissible to show malice (493-a). 6. 

Remoteness.-Remote threats are inadmissible. 

Seven months is too remote (494). 

7. Communication to defendant.-Evidence is admissible showing that the defendant was informed of the decedent's threats (495). 

8. Explanation of deceased's threats.-An explanation of the cause of the deceased's threat against the defendant is inadmissible (496). 

9. Reconciliation.-The defendant's reconciliation with the deceased is admissible after evidence of the defendant's threats has been introduced (497). 

10. Threats and defensive acts.-Threats are admissible as showing due cause for the person threatened to go armed (498). 

11. Overtures for peace. The defendant's overtures for peace several months before the homicide are inadmissible because too remote (499).

12. Defendant's request for protection.-Neither the defendant's nor the deceased's requests for protection prior to the fatal fight are admissible because self-serving (500).

Attendant Circumstances 

13. In general.-Attendant circumstances are admissible.

Evidence is admissible to show that the deceased was armed when he made threats prior to the fatal fight (501), that a defendant was armed and lay in wait (502), that a defendant was armed with a concealed weapon when he began a fight (503), that a snapped shell was in the deceased's gun (504), that a defendant habitually went armed (505), that a deceased person was unarmed when killed, as bearing on an alleged hip-pocket gesture (506), that a decedent's rifle was on safety when the killing occurred (507), that the deceased made preparations (508), the overt act being shown, the size and strength of the deceased who attacked with hands and feet when in striking distance (509), that the defendant or decedent was intoxicated just prior to the shooting (510), the effect of cocaine on one of the parties (511), circumstances of execution of plot to kill motivated by adultery (512), etc.

The decisions ruling on the admissibility of attendant circumstances are not harmonious (513). Traces 

14. In general.-Traces of crime are admissible. 

Blood and bones of cremated victim (514), the condition of the victim's body (515), blood on a companion of the defendant may be shown (516).

That a body was undisturbed may be shown (517). 

The condition of the defendant as cut or bruised may be shown (518).

Poison may not be identified by color of a mixture of medicine and orange juice (519). 

Marks on a shell may be shown (520).

Circumstantial Evidence 

95 

Before bullet holes may be shown in the defendant's car it must be shown first that the car was not tampered with (521).

Foot tracks may be compared with the defendant's shoe (522), if detailed comparison is made (523).

Before traces at a place may be described, at which a rape was alleged to have been committed, the place must be identified as the place of the crime (524). 

The physical condition of a prosecutrix in rape may be shown (525).

Traces of liquor in a container in a defendant's possession may be shown as tending to show that he had sold liquor (526). 

Blowing up a safe may be shown in a case of larceny, although the breaking of the safe was not alleged (527). 

Acts in Same Quarrel; Transaction; Prior Related Quarrel; Prior Crime of the Deceased 

As Showing State of Mind; Prior Similar

Crimes; Previous Misconduct 

15. Transaction.-Quarrels of defendant and victim so closely related in time and so connected by the law of cause and effect as to form one transaction are admissible, to show mens rea, intent, motive, consciousness of guilt, significance of language and acts, or concerted action or conspiracy. The specific decisions are not harmonious (528). 

A quarrel forty minutes before the killing, which grew out of the same cause and difficulty as the killing, is admissible (529). 

15(a). Details. The state is not permitted to show the details, but the defendant, in explanation, may show the details of prior difficulties. 

Also, the defendant may show reconciliation (530). 

16. Specific Instances. Other unrelated acts, as of violence or homicide, or rape, are not admissible (531). 

A series of rapes on the same person while kidnapped is not admissible (532).

Prostitution cannot be shown in connection with the use of a house for the sale of liquor (536). 

Prior robberies may not be put in evidence (537). 

17. Exceptions.—In bastardy, former illicit intimacy may be shown between the parties (533).

In seduction, prior acts of unchastity of the prosecutrix are admissible (534). Subsequent intimacy may not be shown (535).

Prior rape of the defendant's wife by the deceased is admissible to show the deceased's animus in threatening and attempting to kill the defendant, whom he hated as his successful rival in courtship (538).

On the issue of the identity of the defendant in a liquor case, evidence is admissible that the defendant was seen at the still on prior occasions (539). 

18. Fraud.-Prior fraudulent misrepresentations by the same agent are admissible to show fraud (540). 

Fraud in prior cases in changing speedometers of used cars is admissible (541). 

A confidence game followed by purse-snatching is admissible in an action for purse-snatching (542).

A series of embezzlements may be shown, each being treated as part of a general and continuous offense, within the period laid (543).

Prior false entries and forced balances may be shown (544).

A subsequent transfer to defeat creditors is admissible (545).

The facts of a person's financial condition and his several liabilities may be shown on the issue of fraud in misrepresenting these facts (547).

A preference to sons is not evidence of fraud on unpaid creditors (548). 

19. Remoteness.-Forgery three years previously is inadmissible (546). Fraud 

20. Inadequacy of price.-Gross inadequacy of price is sufficient evidence to prove fraud. Of course it fol-

Circumstantial Evidence 

97 lows that this circumstance is admissible (549). As to creditors, pro tanto inadequacy may be shown (549-a). 

The following circumstances prove fraud: "Inadequacy of consideration, transaction not in usual course or mode of doing business, absolute conveyance as security, secrecy, insolvency of grantor, transfer of all his property, attempt to give evidence of fairness by conscripting sister-in-law as a conduit for passing title to the wife, retention of possession, failure to take a list of the property covered by the conveyance which was commingled with some furniture and fixtures of his father's estate, relationship of the parties and transfer to person having no apparent use for the property" (549-b). 

21. Imbecility of party.-Evidence is admissible to show imbecility of mind of an owner from whom by fraud a bill of sale had been gotten as a circumstance tending to show criminal intent by the defrauder (550).

Dependence and personal trust by the defrauded person may be shown in such cases (551).

Poverty of an assignee is no evidence of fraud in the assignment, against creditors (552).

Continuous Offenses; Status 

22. Specific instances. In prosecution for fornication, illicit intimacy prior to and after the date of the indictment may be shown (553).

In bigamy, the first marriage may be evidenced by cohabitation with the first wife, prior to the attemptive second marriage (554).

Married status may be proved by long-continued conduct, declarations and repute, and holding out (555).

Circumstantial evidence is sufficient to prove prostitution, viz., no occupation by day, solicitation at night, entering house with men, and on several occasions getting in bed with men (556). 

Seduction may be shown by proof of promises of marriage followed by betrayal and attempts at abortion (557).

Nuisances 

23. Reputation or conviction.-General reputation of house or conviction of inmate of prostitution is prima facie evidence of such nuisance and of knowledge of the fact by persons charged with maintaining it (558). 

23-a. Reputation of place. --Reputation of place where liquor is sold is admissible in prosecution for sale (558-a). 

24. Liquor cases. By statute, in trials for sale or giving away of liquor, offenses may be shown prior to the date laid in the indictment and not barred by the statute of limitation (559). The statute does not apply to purchases by the defendant (560).

The statute does not apply to the manufacture of liquor or the keeping of liquor (561). 

The statute does not render admissible evidence of an offense for which the defendant had been acquitted or convicted (562).

The state must elect which offense the defendant is to be tried on (563).

Under the statute, evidence of a prior offense is not admissible where the affidavit charges a sale at a specific date. Neither is such evidence admissible where no date is mentioned, for this prevents the defendant from having the benefit of the immunity of the statute, as to offenses anterior to the date laid (564). 

The date of the prior sale must be shown (565). 

Sale after the date laid may not be shown (566).

Prior lawful possession of liquor may be shown in an action for the sale as tending to show the fact of sale (567). 

25. Gambling and dope cases. Statutes similar to the foregoing statute on liquor are in force in regard to gambling and selling of cocaine, opium, morphine or their derivatives (568). 

26. Circumstantial evidence of philandering; specific instance.-Invitations of a married woman to a man

Circumstantial Evidence 

99 to visit her in the absence of her husband and clandestine visits are admissible as tending to show adulterous disposition of the wife in an action for divorce for adultery (579). 

Character and reputation 27. Defendant's good character.-The defendant's good character for the trait involved in a criminal charge is admissible and is to be considered by the jury along with other circumstances in the case (Instruction given) (569).

After the defendant has thus put his character in issue the state may show it is bad, but not unless the defendant has put his character in issue (570).

It is error to comment on the defendant's failure to call character witnesses (571). 

28. Insanity.-The defendant's insanity may not be proved in a criminal case by evidence of his reputation (572). 

29. Deceased's bad character.-The deceased's bad character and reputation for the trait involved may be shown in a criminal case where the evidence shows he committed the overt act of aggression (573). Character may be shown by reputation, but not by a witness' opinion (574). 

30. Deceased's good reputation.-Except to rebut proof of his bad reputation, the deceased's good reputation and character are inadmissible (575). 

31. Reputation of prosecutrix in rape.-In rape the reputation of the prosecutrix is admissible upon the issue of consent (576).

In statutory rape, the reputation of the prosecutrix is admissible as tending to show unchaste character (577). 

Good character of the prosecutrix in statutory rape is admissible only in rebuttal (578).

Reputation in Civil Actions 

32. Generally. In civil actions generally character is not in issue and reputation is inadmissible-as in assault and battery; trial of title to property in attachment; in tort cases for reckless driving of car, the defendant's reputation for recklessness and the deceased's for sobriety, are inadmissible (580). 

33. Collateral issue.-Reputation for prudence of third person who stored cotton in the place in which the plaintiff's cotton was stored when burned is inadmissible (582). 

Character in Issue 

34. Defamation. In a case of false imprisonment on a charge of drunkenness, alleging damage to the reputation, the plaintiff may show his good character for sobriety (584).

In slander the plaintiff's general bad reputation may be shown under a plea of truth. The plaintiff may prove his good character on the issue of punitive damages, although not attacked by the defendant in the pleading (585). Under a plea of the general issue in a damage suit for unlawful search of a hotel, injuring the reputation of the hotel, the general bad reputation of the hotel may be shown (586).

Rumor of arson is admissible as showing reasonable grounds and good faith of the defendant in publishing an alleged libel (588). 

35. Negligence. In a tort action against a physician for negligence in treating the plaintiff, his character is in issue and he may prove by other physicians that he is a careful physician and surgeon (587). 

36. Incompetent driver.-In a tort action for permitting an incompetent driver to drive a car, incompetence may be proved by the reputation of the driver (581).

Circumstantial Evidence 

101 

37. Solvency in issue. On the issue of a broker's obtaining a solvent purchaser, the purchaser's reputation is admissible (583). 

38. Marriage.-General reputation of the nonexis tence of a marriage is incompetent (589). 

39. Partnership.-Rumor of existence of a partnership is inadmissible (590). 

40. What is reputation?-The prevalent reputation of the party for the trait in question in the neighborhood in which he lives among those conversant with his general reputation is competent. Personal knowledge by the reputation-witness, particular acts, or majority opinion are not competent (591).

That a witness never heard anything against the person is the best evidence of his good character. The witness need not have heard the character of the person discussed (592).

The general question, are you aware of a person's reputation in his community for a specified trait, is proper as the basis for asking whether it is good or bad (593). 

Acquaintance too brief to hear reputation commented on is insufficient to enable a witness to testify as to reputation. Acquaintance six or eight years is sufficient for a witness to testify to reputation (594). 

Reputation of a person in a place from which he removed two years before the trial is competent (595). 

41. Scope.-Reputation is restricted to the nature of the charge or issue in the case (596). 

42. Ante litem motam.-The defendant's reputation is confined to proof thereof before the commission of the crime charged (597). 

A witness' reputation for veracity is not in litigation and is not cut off as of the beginning of the suit (598). 

43. Specific acts and reputation.-Generally particular acts are inadmissible to show character; only general reputation is admissible for this purpose (599).

In statutory rape unchastity of the prosecutrix may be shown by specific acts (600). Conduct 

44. Use of bloodhounds; trailing. The fact that trained, pure-blooded hounds trailed the defendant when put on the trail by an experienced trainer where the crime was committed is admissible as a circumstance to Ishow that the defendant was at the scene of the crime. 

The hounds must be pure bred and must have been trained to trail humans and must have been tested and found reliable, and must be put on the tracks at the scene of the crime, which tracks are sufficiently identified as those of the criminal (601). 

45. Flight in general.-Flight by the defendant or by a negligent agent is admissible and may be considered by the jury with the other evidence (602). 

The defendant's disappearance is admissible as tending to show flight (603). 

A killing in making a get-away may be shown (604). 

46. Refusal to touch the corpse.-In 1852 evidence was admitted showing that the defendant fled to avoid being required to touch the victim's corpse, the belief being that the wounds would gape and bleed afresh when the murderer touched the body (605). 

47. Flight of third person.-Flight of third person is not admissible where nothing else connected the third person with the crime in question aside from the flight. 

The flight of third persons who were associates of the decedent, it seems at or about the time of his death, is admissible (606). 

48. Refusal to flee-The defendant's refusal to flee is inadmissible (607).

In a case in which the defendant went to his home after the killing for which he was tried, his voluntary surrender later to the sheriff is admissible and the jury should be instructed that they may consider it, together

Circumstantial Evidence 

103 with the other facts in evidence (608). There is a dictum that such evidence is admissible only in rebuttal (609). 

49. Explaining flight.-Evidence explaining a flight which has been shown is admissible (610). 

50. Conspiracy acts in series.-Concerted action or conspiracy is admissible. Where a series of crimes is committed by a band of armed men in close time sequence, the defendant's presence at a crime prior to the one in issue may be shown as tending to show his presence (611).

In showing a killing by an assassin hired by conspirators, the conspiracy may be shown and the conspirators' motive may be shown. Acts in furtherance of a conspiracy are admissible, even though the defendant be not present (612).

Conspiracy must be evidenced in some way before acts of others may be shown against the defendant (613). 

Motive and State of Mind 

51. Motive of the deceased.—Adultery between the deceased and the defendant's wife may be proved as showing the motive for and the seriousness of the deceased's threats against the defendant (614).

The cause of the deceased's hate for the defendant may be shown, where the evidence conflicted as to who was the aggressor (615). 

52. Motive of the Defendant.-The defendant's motive to kill his victim is admissible, as adultery with the victim's wife, other adulterous relations and jealousy, blood-feuds, robbery, desire to kill a talkative accomplice, litigation over seduction, sale of liquor, theft (616). 

53. Third persons.-Remote acts possibly showing a motive of a third person to commit a crime in issue are inadmissible (617). 

54. Animus and state of mind of defendant; ill will. -The defendant's ill will for the family of the decedent,

growing out of a seduction by the defendant and a feud is admissible, as showing mens rea. Similarly, an animus arising from other difficulties is admissible, as anger over domestic chores, and other assaults (618). 

55. Recklessness. In an action for manslaughter by motor car recklessness on the same trip prior to the death is admissible as showing the state of mind of the driver (619). 

56. Hate.-Threats against officers made after cutting a man are admissible (620). 

57. Furtive conduct.-Secretive conduct in selling a stolen mule is admissible to show quo animo defendant acted (621). 

58. Hiring assassin.-Attempts to hire an assassin are admissible, in murder (622). 

59. Outlaw attitude.-A burglary by the defendant may be proved in order to show that the defendant had a motive to resist arrest by an officer whom the defendant then killed, and for whose murder he was tried in the present case (623). 

60. Knowledge of commercialized vice.-Knowledge of the keeper of a house of ill-fame may be shown by proof of prior acts of prostitution, the state electing the offense to be submitted, however (624). 

Good Motive 

61. Good motive.-Open taking of property in the presence of the owner and others carries the inference that it is only trespass, in an action for larceny; and the jury should be so instructed (625). 

62. In lying in wait.-Evidence is admissible tending to show that a defendant who lay in wait and killed a man did so with the plan and intent to rescue his son who had been kidnapped by the man and his associates (626).

Circumstantial Evidence 

105 

Recent Possession of Stolen Goods 

63. Recent possession.-Evidence of recent possession of stolen goods is admissible. Four or five months after the stealing is too long. How recent the possession must be to warrant the inference is a question of fact for the jury (627). 

64. Other goods.-Possession of goods stolen at the same time as the goods in issue may be shown (628). 

65. Explanation.-The defendant's explanation of his possession is admissible. An extra-judicial explanation before the defendant's arrest is admissible (629). 

66. Open possession.-Open and unconcealed possession is a circumstance to be considered by the jury in the defendant's favor (630).

Possession and Other Circumstances As Evi dence of Fraud 67. Retention of possession.-Retention of possession, by vendor or grantor, especially where he is embarrassed financially, is evidence of fraud as against his creditors (631). 

68. Personal support reserved.-A conveyance to one creditor, reserving personal support for the grantor, and in which the grantor retains possession, is a fraud on the other creditors (632). 

69. Voluntary conveyance.-A voluntary conveyance to a relative by an insolvent is a fraud on creditors. Hence, these stated circumstances may be shown (633). 

70. Circumstances of release.-All circumstances of a release of a cause of action for personal injuries may be shown (634). 

71. Unusual recitals. Unusual recitals detracting from the value of property are viewed with suspicion (635).

72. Sudden wealth.wealth. The sudden acquisition of wealth by a poor man is a suspicious circumstance (636). 

73. Mortgage in form of a deed.-A mortgage in the form of an absolute conveyance is not per se fraudulent, but is a circumstance to be considered (637). 

74. Partnership of grantee with grantor.—It is competent to show the relation of partnership of an immediate grantee of the partnership as a circumstance showing a scheme to defeat creditors, where a second grantee contests with creditors of the partnership (638). 

75. Explanation, agency.-The debtor may explain his dealing with property sought to be reached by creditors by showing he acted as an agent of his wife, the true owner (639). 

ii. Tort 

76. Prior quarrel.-Generally, a prior quarrel between a motorman and a relative of the plaintiff is inadmissible (640). 

77. Prior or subsequent acts.-Prior or subsequent acts of negligence are inadmissible (641). 

78. Fire.-Negligent handling of engines at or near the place and time of a railway fire, and negligent habit in handling engines are admissible as tending to show the cause of the fire in issue (642). 

79. Prior electric shocks.-Prior electric shocks on the same street-car or track are admissible to show the dangerous nature of the place (643). 

80. Prior x-ray burns.-Prior x-ray burns by the defendant with a different machine are inadmissible (644).

The court distinguished the ruling that the unsafeness of a place may be shown by prior hurts at the place (645). 

81. Prior acts of negligence in handling explosives. 

-Prior acts of negligence in handling percussion caps are inadmissible (646).

Circumstantial Evidence 

107 

82. Safe use by others.-Safe use of a place by a majority of people under similar conditions does not show that the place is reasonably safe, and it is error so to instruct. Hence it seems that the fact is inadmissible (647). 

But it is error to exclude evidence that no prior accidents happened at an H-type switch ballasted with slag (648).

Prior near accidents at a place are admissible (649). 

Similar construction of street drains under different traffic conditions is inadmissible (650).

Use of slag as ballast by other railroads is admissible (651). 

83. Subsequent repairs. Subsequent repairs of a place are admissible upon the issue of negligence, not as an admission. Repairs five months after the accident are too remote (652).

An agreement to build a crossing gate is inadmissible in an action for killing a mule on a railroad (653). 

84. Negligent habit.-Negligent habit in leaving an elevator pit unguarded is admissible (654).

Reckless habit of a railway engineer is admissible (655). 

Reckless habit of a plaintiff in "hopping" trains is admissible (655-a). 

Habitual drunkenness of a telegraph employee is admissible to show his incompetence (656). 

85. Specific acts. Specific acts not amounting to habit are generally inadmissible (657). 

86. Liquor cure.-Treatment for the drink habit is admissible (658). Custom 

87. In bridge construction.-Custom in lowering a bridge timber is admissible, as bearing on negligence in a fatality resulting from the fall of the timber (659).

88. Railway signals.-The custom of other engineers not to give signals is inadmissible, because irrelevant (660). 

89. Violation of Jim-Crow law.-Customary violation of the Jim-Crow law is admissible on the issue of improper ejection of a passenger (661).

Violation of company rule.-Evidence is admissible that the public generally occupied cupolas of cabooses with the knowledge of the crews and conductors, in violation of a company rule (661-a). 

90. In contract.-In contract, a custom to ascertain damage by appraisement is admissible as an implied part of the contract (662).

A custom to charge firms with debts of individuals in the firm is inadmissible, because unreasonable (663). 

91. Company regulations.-Company regulations are admissible as showing custom and usage (664). The rule may be a test of due care (665).

There is a duty to adopt a rule for complex work, not for simple work (666).

Disregard of.-Habitual disregard of a rule is admissible to show abandonment of it (667). 

92. Law of the road.-Prima facie violation of the law of the road is negligence and hence such an act is admissibble, viz., driving on the left, but this may be overcome by showing a necessity so to do to avoid a collision (668). 

State of Mind 

93. Anger or malice in tort.-Anger and recklessness of a train crew are admissible to show the state of mind of the crew in an action for willful negligence (669). 

94. Defamation. Similar defamatory statements are admissible to show malice (670).

Circumstantial Evidence 

109 

95. Domestic affection.-Love of a wife for her husband as shown by letters before marriage is admissible in an action for alienation of affection (671). 

96. Parental affection. The purpose of a meeting between a father and a daughter may be shown to negative an intention of alienating affections of the daughter for her husband (672). 

97. Knowledge of cant hooks.-Plaintiff's familiarity with cant hooks may be shown, on the issue of negligence in using a cant hook while in a defective condition (672-a). 

Attendant Circumstances 98. Generally.-Attendant circumstances, physical surroundings, conditions and related acts, are admissible in all cases. On the issue of disorderly conduct in false arrest, the plaintiff's drunkenness three hours before the arrest may be shown (673). In a case involving a car wreck, the defendant's speeding a mile from the scene of the wreck may be shown (674). The beginning of a fire on land near a railway shortly after the defendant's train passed and the spreading of this fire to the plaintiff's land may be shown as circumstantial evidence that the defendant's engine caused the fire (675). The defendant's drunken appearance after a tort, i. e., a car wreck, may be explained by showing that he took whiskey to kill pain (676). circumstances are 

99. Miscellaneous.-Inconclusive inadmissible, as, in a case involving disability from diabetes, the fact that third person with this disorder could work (677); that a patient sent a Christmas card to a physician and later sued him for negligence (678); process of sanitary bottling in an action of damages for injuries caused by broken glass in the beverage (679). 

A deed of land is admissible to show good faith of the grantee in cutting trees on the land, although later the deed was invalidated (680).

100. Limitation on gruesomeness.-Shocking physical details of injuries of a party's companion should be excluded. But evidence arousing tragic pity is admissible despite the appeal to sympathy if the evidence is otherwise competent and is not collateral (681). iii. Ownership 

101. Street line.-Recognition of a street line and building with reference thereto are taken to establish the true line, although probably 2 1/2 feet off the true line (682). 

102. Dominion.-Occupancy and dominion are admissible to show ownership and to establish a boundary (683). 

103. Tax return.-Failure of an alleged owner to return land for taxes is admissible. Payment of taxes evidences a claim but does not show possession (684). 

104. Request for permissive user.-A letter requesting permission to stay on land is admissible to show possession was permissive (685). 

105. Dedication.-Laying out streets and selling lots with reference thereto amounts to dedication of the streets although actual user does not follow at once (686). 

User may prove dedication (687). 

106. Long continued user.-User for twenty years shows prima facie a lost grant (688). 107. 

Abandonment.-Abandonment of a homestead is shown by separation of a husband and wife and a conveyance by the husband accompanied by declarations that the homestead had been abandoned, and absence for seven years (689). 

108. Reputation; boundary.-A private boundary may be proved by reputation if definite (690). 

109. Value. The value of an animal may be proved by reputation of its pedigree (691).

Circumstantial Evidence 

111 Value 

110. Factors.-Circumstances showing value are ad missible, viz., proximity to navigation, town, road, and other factors showing value (692). 

111. Forced sales.-Value of land in condemnation proceedings is parallel to forced sales and is inadmissible on the issue of condemnation (693).

It is reversible error to admit evidence of the price owners of similar property had accepted in settlement of condemnation proceedings. ""The price paid in settlement of condemnation proceedings or the sum paid by the condemnor for similar land, even if proceedings had not been begun, is not admissible. Such payments are in the nature of compromise, to avoid the expense and uncertainty of litigation, and are not fair indications of market value'" (693-a). 

112. Value of adjoining land.-Actual sale value of adjoining land of like quality may be shown (694). 

But recitals of consideration in deeds to adjoining land are inadmissible (695). 

113. Offers to sell.-Offers of taxpayer to sell property are admissible upon the issue of taxable value (696). 

But the value of land or marketable personalty cannot be shown by offers of would-be purchasers (697). 

114. Sales. Where there is no market the price of articles or similar articles at a fair sale is admissible to show value (698). 

115. Breach of contract.—On the issue of breach of contract, the contract price is admissible on the issue of value (699). 

116. Market value.-The market value of cotton at a nearby market or at an agreed market is admissible (700). The market value of labor is admissible (701).

In fraud cases, the highest market value during the period involved is taken as the measure (702).

Market price is inadmissible on the issue of the values of valued insured property (703).

In condemnation of land for use as a highway, evidence of specific damage is competent only insofar as it relates to the market value of the land. In proving market value, all facts as to the condition of the property and its surroundings, improvements, and capabilities may be shown. But specific damage may not be shown as separate elements, but only as bearing on market value of the land involved (703-a). 

117. Pedigree.—Pedigree is admissible on the issue of the value of an animal (704). 

118. Insolvency of obligor.-The insolvency of the obligor of bonds is admissible to show no-value of bonds and fraud in selling them (705). 

119. Artistic value.-The cost of replacement of family portraits having no market value is admissible, but personal associations are not competent on the issue of value (706). 120. 

iv. Domicile and Status 

Residence.-Long residence is admissible to show domicile (707).

Authority of Minister to Celebrate Marriage 

121. De facto status. The open claim of being a minister authorized to perform the marriage ceremony is sufficient, although he be not ordained properly by his church (708). Insanity 

122. History of.-Upon the issue of insanity all facts and circumstances prior to or soon after the party's alleged crime are admissible. Every act of the party's life is relevant to this issue. Evidence is admissible showing that insanity is in the party's family (709).

Circumstantial Evidence 

113 

123. Testator.-A testator's acts and declarations both before and after the execution of his will are admissible upon the issue of his testamentary capacity. A grantor's previous declarations followed by execution of a deed in accordance with them are admissible to show mental capacity (710). 

But evidence is inadmissible, because not showing insanity, that the defendant was eccentric, irritable and passionate (711). 

Undue Influence 

124. Surrounding circumstances.-On the issue of undue influence, the relation of the parties and the influence of one over the other may be shown. A wide range of inquiry is permitted in such cases (712). 

V. Contracts 125. Course of dealing.-Prior contracts for medical services for servants do not prove later contracts to have been made or authorized. But a course of dealing under similar circumstances may be shown upon the issue of an implied understanding. Willingness to contract may be shown upon the issue of having contracted. A series of payments on a note by a party is sufficient to prove the execution of the note (713). 

126. Handwriting samples.-Genuine handwritings samples are admissible for comparison by the jury with disputed writings, although no expert is introduced (714). 

127. Delivery.-A bill of lading naming the buyer as consignee and delivered is prima facie evidence of unconditional delivery to the consignee; retention of the bill of lading is evidence of retention of the title by the consignor (715).

Delivery of cotton to a carrier in the customary way, acquiesced in by the carrier, is a good delivery (716). 

128. Acceptance of merchandise. A purchaser's holding and using a machine is admissible to show acceptance of it (717).

129. Tacit acceptance.-Previous dealings of tacit acceptance of an offer are admissible to show another offer was accepted in the same way (718). 

130. Consideration.-Correspondence of a stock cer tificate and a note in dates and amounts is admissible as tending to show the stock to be the consideration for the note (719). 

131. Payment; waiver; etc.-An offer to pay after the time of alleged payment is admissible as evidence of nonpayment (720).

Possession by the insurer of the insured's premium check a day after his death is evidence of payment by mail before his death (721).

A memorandum of payment and cancellation on the margin of a deed of trust is admissible as some evidence of payment (722).

Payment by mail is shown by facts of the deceased's being seen to mail a letter and of finding the letter postmarked one day after the death of the deceased (723). 

132. Waiver.-Waiver of strict payment in the grace period for insurance is shown by a request after grace that payment be made (724). Nonobjection is waiver of strict proof of the death of the insured (725). 

Efforts to collect from an assuming vendee a year after the assumption of the debt and no further efforts to collect from the first purchaser are sufficient to show a release of the first purchaser (726). 

133. Practical construction.-Dealings of parties un der a contract are admissible as evidence of a contemporary construction (727).

Miscellaneous 

134. De facto existence.-Payment under a contract is admissible as evidence of its execution by the party (728). 

135. Priority and guaranty.-No priority is implied by guaranty of some of assigned notes and not of others

Circumstantial Evidence 

115 assigned, the notes being secured by the same deed of trust and maturing a year apart (729). 

136. Notary's records, past misconduct.—Particular acts of a notary in filling out blanks in his book without reference to particular cases and swearing to the record are admissible to show his notarial record is unreliable (730).

Account Books 

137. Collateral errors.-Non credit of third person's payments in an account book is inadmissible to impeach an account book (731).

Agency 

138. Retainer of attorney. The fact that a suit is brought for a litigant is evidence tending to prove employment. The court papers are admissible to show retainer and the nature of the services (732). 

138-a. Agency, from course of dealing.—Where a tenant rented land for an annual money rent and with the knowledge and consent of the landlord for two years sold the crops, this course of dealing constituted him the agent of the lessor; and an innocent purchaser, who pays the tenant, takes clear of the landlord's lien (732-a). 

139. Scope of.-Evidence is admissible showing that an agent or employee was about his master's business at the time he committed the tort complained of, or at the time he was himself hurt (733). 140. 

Control.-Circumstantial evidence showing joint control of an employee is admissible on the issue of joint liability (734).

Evidence that the servants of X were under the control of a company is admissible to show that X himself was under the control of the company (735). 

141. Scope identifying principal. The scope of a per son's activity is admissible as tending to show for whom he works (736).

142. Insurance as evidence of agency.-A company's carrying insurance on workmen or agents is admissible as evidence of the employment of such persons by the company (737). 

143. Dual agency as basis of imputation of knowledge. -Where parties dealing with one another are represented by the same person, knowledge of the facts of one person's business by the common agent will be imputed to the other party, except where the agent had a personal interest which would prevent disclosure (738). 1. 

D. JUDICIAL NOTICE Generally.-Judicial notice notice is taken of facts which are so well and commonly known to all persons of average intelligence as to be certain and indisputable (739). Matters of common knowledge, reactions, sentiments, and feelings of average, normal persons are noticed by the courts (740). i. Matters Cognizable, or Not 

Specifically, the following things are known to the courts: 

2. Political subdivisions.-Boundary of state and counties, but not of supervisors' or justices' of the peace districts, for venue; location of towns in counties of state, for purposes of venue, is judicially known (741). 

3. General law.-Emancipation proclamation, state statutes, result of local option election; but not operation of a law dependent on a contingency (742). 

4. Foreign law. Statutes and general law, known in the same manner as Mississippi State Law (743). 

5. Private acts of legislature.-Printed acts are ad missible without being pleaded (744). 

6. Historic facts in public law. Civil War, public relief in great depression, general requirements of public land sale (745).
