11. Rulings of Attorney General (750). 12. 

Undervaluation in tax assessment (751). 

13. Judiciary. That a named person is a justice of the peace of a certain district, a named person is circuit judge of a certain district. A court knows judicially its own clerk, the Supreme Court knows that in Smith County the clerk and not deputies usually do the work, that a justice of the peace is not required to have a seal (752). 

It is common knowledge that prior to 1941 appeals by drainage districts have been had without appeal bonds (752-a). 

14. Matters in same case or transaction (753). 

15. Not of records in other cases (754). 

16. Coordinate branch of government (755). 

17. Records, but not agency of Insurance Commissioner to accept process; Senate Journal confirming appointment; seal of Soil Conservation Committee (756); corporate charter in office of Secretary of State (756-a). 

18. Not of town ordinances (757). 

19. Not of regulations of State Board of Health (758). 

20. Not of penitentiary record of admission and discharge of prisoners (759). 

21. Parchman as a penal farm, but not of other farms (760). 

22. Time of state elections (761).

23. Physical facts in nature.-Debris is in streams, wet timber decays in the open, fruit may be shipped to Mississippi from Georgia without perishing, cypress requires standing water for growth and life, not that cutting and boxing trees for turpentine destroys value as timber, banana peelings are slick, tobacco and cotton are farm products requiring cultivation (762). 

24. Habits of animals.-Dogs kill sheep, not all halftamed foxes will return to the cage (763), a gentle horse may become frightened and violent when struck by another horse in falling (763-a). 

25. Bacteria.—Bacteria may be on knives, ticks transmit cattle fever, syphilis is contagious and is transmitted by contact (764). 

26. Chemicals, medicines, beverages.-Quinine is a medicine for malaria, gasoline is explosive, whiskey is intoxicating, methyl alcohol diluted and in small quantities is not harmful externally (765). 

27. Geographic facts.-There is no range 5 in a specific official survey; section 2 adjoins section 11 on the north in T-9 range 20; the distance from Hammond, Louisiana, to the county seat of Scott County is only a few hours' drive; less than 31 hours is required for a shipment from Concord, Georgia, to Grenada, Mississippi; navigable channel of the Mississippi River, location of towns, of scil conservation districts, by statute (766); overflow of Mississippi river (766-a). is hereditary, 28. Physiological traits.-Insanity hardening of the arteries shortens life, different people do not require the sarne amount of sleep and it is not negligence to drive a car after the driver has had 3 1/2 hours of sleep; appearance in middle life is no index of exact age (767). 

29. Human traits.-An undertaking establishment is depressing in a residential district, children are attracted by unusual objects, the way of brides are inexplicable to bachelors, judge's opinions have great influence with jurors, some jurors are skeptical of circumstantial evi-

Judicial Notice 

119 dence, it is the usual thing in damage suits for harms sustained at railway crossings for all the train crew to swear signals were given and for the plaintiff's witnesses to deny it, extravagant testimony is usual when trials are had out of the community in which the cause of action arose, it is not the usual practice to record wills in counties other than that of probate, that opinion is divided on questions of public finance (768). 

30. Calendar.-Dates of week days, seasons, crop year (769). 

31. Meaning of words.-"Miss." stands for Mississippi; “O. N." in a bill of lading stands for order notify; “mixed” train; “disease”; “lever-power"; epithet, "bitch"; "heirs," in vernacular; "yearling"; "medical" (770); and “agricultural products" (770-a). 

32. General matters pertaining to traffic.-Limits of reasonable speed, habits of pedestrians, practice of parking cars, density of peak-traffic in town, mode of steering car and trailer, safety of car-door latches, general life of auto tires (771). 

33. General conditions of towns, markets, and industry. No trolly cars in small towns, boarding houses have rates, hospital facilities are in large cities, the appearance of used cars does not indicate their value, substantial uniformity of market price on a commercial basis, cotton is the staple crop of Mississippi and is valuable for lint and oil, cotton in a compress is on its way to market, in mid-summer labor is not in great demand, there is a market for blue-blooded dogs, the time of the depression and when it is over, general facts of railway construction and operation, mortality tables, increased revenue from gasoline tax (772). 

ii. Miscellaneous. 

34. Miscellaneous-Fire hazard of vacant property, safe use of street cars, control of schools by principals, no public time schedule for freight, etc. (773).

35. Generally, particular and specific facts are not cognizable by the courts without proof (774).

Sources of Information 

36. Books, records and treatises may be resorted to for facts judicially cognizable (775).

III. DOCUMENTARY EVIDENCE A. PAROL EVIDENCE 

1. In general. The term "parol evidence" denotes oral as distinguished from written evidence (776).

Insofar as the legal consequences of words are affected by rules requiring writing, the rules are substantive in nature. But rules of statutes requiring certain transactions to be evidenced by writings and rules of written contracts by which all conversations are presumed to be merged in the formal writing and excluding all save the form selected by the contracting parties, deal with manifestations of intent or the appropriate form of acts and hence embrace matters evidentiary in nature, in this, that such rules recognize a connection between form, or manifestation, and intent, or a mental state. In this view, the parol evidence rule and the Statute of Frauds and other Statutes regulating the permissible form by which contractual intent or other intent may be manifested are a part of the general rules treating the admissible manifestations of states, conditions, intents and acts, i. e., of the law of evidence (777). 

2. The proper law. It seems the lex fori controls parol evidence (778). 

3. Persons subject to the rule. The parol evidence rule applies to parties and privies to the contract but not to strangers to it (779).

Parol Evidence 

121 

i. Subject Matter Within the Rule 

4. Judicial acts.-Judicial records may not be varied or supplemented by parol evidence. Want of jurisdiction may be shown by parol; courts may not recite themselves into power over persons (780).

The jurisdiction of courts of special powers may not be shown by parol evidence (781).

Parol evidence is admissible to show fraud in judgment, or fraudulent and false entry of court minutes after adjournment (782). 

5. Correction of court records. By statute, judgments or decrees may be amended by use of other parts of record or writing before the court, but not by parol evidence, except unauthorized changes of the record, on not less than three days' notice to the other side (783). 

Apart from this statutory proceeding court records may not be varied by parol evidence, except for fraud (784).

The court clerk is not permitted to impeach the court records (785). 

6. Process. The return of process may be contradicted by parol (786). 

7. Execution. The return on execution may be amended (787). 

8. Sheriff's receipt.-A sheriff's receipt may be varied by extrinsic proof (788). 

9. Real parties to a judgment.-Real parties in interest in a judgment may be shown by parol evidence (789). 

10. Tax receipt. The description of property on which taxes were paid may be shown, although the tax receipt misdescribes the property (790). 

11. Tender of tax.-Tender and refusal of tax money may be shown by parol (791).

12. Falsity in removal of disability.-Falsity of petition in proceedings to remove disability of minority may be shown by parol evidence (792). 

13. Judgment a bar.-Parol evidence is admissible to show whether a judgment was rendered on the merits, and so is a bar to further prosecution (793). Similarly, whether a dismissal of a case was on the merits (793). 

14. Judgment of non-liability and oral reservations. -Nonpayment of a judgment against a person insured against loss from tort liability may be shown by parol evidence by the insurance company, but reservation of liability of an insurance company cannot be shown by parol evidence by the person who had insurance against damage to his own car, where a judgment had been entered in favor of the tort feasor, against whom the insurer would have a cause of action of subrogation but for the judgment of non-liability for the tort (794). 

15. Materiality of issue in perjury.-In perjury, an attorney may testify a given issue on which a party was alleged to have perjured himself was not contested as material to a judgment, although the evidence in the former case was before the court in the perjury trial (795). 

16. Ground of judgment.-Parol evidence is admissible to show the ground on which a judgment was based, viz., whether a judgment for the maker of a note was based on failure of consideration, where a guarantor had guaranteed the validity of the note (796). 

17. Minutes and acts of Boards of Supervisors; minutes after adjournment.-Parol evidence is admissible to show that purported minutes and orders of a Board of Supervisors were entered after adjournment and without having been docketed and presented (797). 

17-a. Notice of tax sale.-Parol evidence is inadmissible to show that notice of tax sale was sent to lienor by registered mail where the notation made by the clerk

Parol Evidence 

123 merely said "notice required by law" was sent, under 

Sec. 3261, Code, 1930 (797-a). 

18. Claims. Although the minutes of a Board of 

Supervisors do not show rejection of a claim, parol evidence is admissible to show that the claim was rejected (798). 

19. Jurisdictional facts.-Parol evidence is not admissible to dispute the fact of action recorded in the minutes or to show that jurisdictional steps were taken when the record does not so show (799), or to vary a lease or a contract made by the Board of Supervisors (800). 

20. Acts of Board of Pharmacy.-Records and find ings of Board of Pharmacy may not be attacked by showing findings of good character were not based on a full investigation (801). 

21. Tax deed. Description in tax deeds may not be varied by parol evidence, but parol evidence is admissible to vary the date (802). 

22. Election return.-An election return cannot be contradicted by an election officer (803). 

23. Land contracts and deeds and records.-Parol evidence is inadmissible to add stipulations to contracts of sale of land or to deeds of land (804), or to change the tract of land conveyed (805). Parol evidence is admissible to show when a lease began and when payment was due (806).

Parol evidence is admissible to show land which was included in a trustee's deed was not included in the trustee's sale (807). 

24. Land records.-Where a patent and tract book conflict, then warrants locating the land, patents and tract books may be looked to, to decide which is correct (808). 25. 

Omissions.-Parol evidence is admissible where other clue identifies the land to supply county

and state in a tax deed, section number of land located by a named creek; township and range omitted in deed may be supplied from the title bond (809). 

26. Tax sale for named fiscal year.-Parol evidence is inadmissible to vary the recital of a tax deed that the sale was for a named fiscal year (810). 

27. Tax sale from day to day.-Parol evidence is admissible to show a tax sale was made after being continued from day to day although the deed does not so recite (811). 

28. Identifying land described in tax assessment by tract and owner.-Under statute, parol evidence is admissible to identify land separately owned where a tax description of the tract and owner is given. Where the description leads certainly to the land intended, oral and documentary proof in aid of the roll are admissible (812). The clue and course must be in the tax roll (813). 

The initials of the maker of a subdivision may be supplied (814). 

A sub-division of a nonexistent section not otherwise located is incurable (815). 

29. In aid of description of tax deeds.—Parol evidence is inadmissible to show what parts of designated lots or sections were conveyed by a tax deed which did not specify the part conveyed (816). 

But parol evidence is admissible to show which 40 acres in a half of a quarter section was conveyed (817). 

Parol evidence is inadmissible to locate a given number of acres in a section (818).

Tax deed. Parol evidence is inadmissible to supply lot number omitted in tax deed (821). 

30. Contract referring only to tracts of named owner. -Parol evidence is inadmissible to identify four tracts of land of a named owner, being all the land of said owner, which were made subject to an option (819).

Parol Evidence 

125 31. Bounds of named owners.-Parol evidence is admissible to identify land bounded by land of named owners (820). 

32. Description ascertainable in future.-Parol evi dence is admissible to apply to the res a general description in a contract, viz., lands upon which a school was to be erected in a town to be selected-after the town had been selected (822). Similarly, of a deed or a will, except where it refers to another instrument in aid as the source of information, but which does not contain the information (823). 

33. Boundaries.-Parol evidence is admissible to locate, identify, and prove boundaries (824).

A dotted line on a government map may be explained by parol evidence as a line that was never run. Unintelligible letters, marks, and figures on a government land office map may not be explained by parol evidence. 

A public record must be clear and certain and must be tried by inspection (825).

Parol evidence is inadmissible to show that boundaries different from those of the deed were pointed out (826).

A boundary given in a deed cannot be varied by parol evidence of a different understanding (827).

Where egress and ingress are granted, reasonable roadways may be located by parol (828).

Adverse possession may be shown by parol evidence although a deed only gave a life estate to the one in possession (829). 

34. Inaccurate and incomplete descriptions.—Parol evidence is admissible to show what property was conveyed by a deed in which the boundaries overlapped bounds of adjacent property deeded (830). 

The description of property which does not give a clue which would lead to the property is not enough to admit parol evidence in aid (831). 

Totally erroneous descriptions may not be corrected by parol evidence (832). Parol evidence is admissible to explain terms and abbreviations, a description in a tax

deed and tax roll, referring to an official plat, but not to amend the official plat by showing an unofficial revised plat was used in describing the lot (832-a). 

35. Insurance policy description.-A general de scription of property in an insurance policy though in part erroneous may be applied to the res where the correct county and distance from a town of the insured house were given (833). 

36. Contracts not dealing with land.-Parol evidence is admissible to identify personal property, as churns, notes, company guarantees, orders, claims, etc., when described in general language in contracts or correspondence without great particularity (834). 

37. Attendant circumstances.-Parol evidence is admissible to remedy a latent ambiguity in a deed by explaining a party's entire interest in a named estate consisted of an interest in a plantation (835). 

The circumstances of the parties may be shown in order to apply the language used to the subject matter and to show what was meant, in construing instruments whose meaning is not clear. But the instrument may not be varied by resort to such evidence (836). 

38. Meaning of words.-The surrounding circum stances, the position and intelligence of the parties may be shown in order to throw light on the meaning of doubtful words (837).

Short and incomplete terms may be explained by parol evidence, and words having an established special local signification may be defined by the proof of such local usage (838). For example-the words "timber" (838) "car" (840) "Artesian" (841) "Bal. a/c to date" (842).

But expressions of an established general meaning cannot be varied unless an established local meaning is shown (843). Where a death certificate stated that a deceased woman had been pregnant "about" six months at the time of her death, upon the issue as to what the reporting physician meant by the word "about" the rule of the State Board of Health is admissible to the effect that a physi-

Parol Evidence 

127 cian is not required to report the birth of a child who has not advanced to the fifth month in gestation (843-c). 

39. Practical construction.-Conduct of the parties after execution of a contract is admissible, only with reference to ambiguous language (843-a). 

Where an oral contract was made and performed for hauling sand it was held that "what the parties to a contract consistently do thereunder throughout is evidence, is indeed often the best evidence of what the contract between them required that they should do" (843-b). 

40. Custom.-Custom is inadmissible to vary a contract (844).

Usage is not a part of a contract unless it is shown to be general, uniform, certain, and reasonable. When such showing is made usage may be shown (845), for, a uniform and established custom raises a presumption that the parties contracted with reference to it (846). 

41. Deed as a mortgage.-A deed or conveyance absolute in form under which possession is given may not be shown by parol evidence to be a mortgage (847). 

A deed or conveyance absolute in form under which possession is not given may be shown by parol evidence to be a mortgage (848). 42.

Mortgage.-A mortgage may not be varied by oral evidence adding other beneficiaries (849). 

43. Trust relations.-Parol evidence is admissible to show trust relations imposed on a deed, but the declaration of trust must be clear and unambiguous (850). A resulting trust may be shown by parol evidence (851). 

44. Husband & wife, farming and transfers.—Oral evidence is insufficient to show growing crops on the wife's land belong to the husband (852). 

Oral evidence is inadmissible to show transfer between husband and wife required by statute to be in writing (853). 

45. Testamentary intent.-Parol evidence is admissible to show that a letter was written with testamentary

intent (854); but not to change the res disposed of or the part given the devisees (855). Similarly an instrument appearing to be a will may be shown by parol evidence to be a deed (856). Equity cannot reform a will.

Where a will disposes of property by a definite description, parol evidence is not admissible to contradict or vary the description (856-a). 

46. Preliminary negotiations.—Preliminary negoti ations are merged in the executed instrument and parol evidence is inadmissible to show the preliminary or contemporaneous negotiation (857). 

47. Integration.-Parol evidence is admissible to identify a paper referred to by a contract (858). 

48. Tickets. Where a ticket does not stipulate the route, evidence of what the agent said as to the route is admissible (859). relating to per- 

49. Performance.-Questions formance may be developed by parol evidence (860). 

50. Conditions anterior, precedent, subsequent. 

Parol evidence is admissible to invalidate tax sale by proof of tender of tax (861). 

Conditions precedent may be shown by parol evidence (862).

Parol evidence is admissible to show an act of performance as an acceptance of an offer (863).

Conditions subsequent may not be proved by parol evidence (864). 

51. Date.-Parol evidence is admissible to vary the date of execution of an instrument (865). Parol evidence is inadmissible to vary the date expressly given on which the instrument is to be effective (865-a). 

52. Consideration.-The true consideration of sealed and unsealed instruments may be shown despite recitals to the contrary, except where contractual provisions as to consideration is shown (866).

Want, or failure of consideration may be shown by parol evidence (867).

Parol Evidence 

129 A release of all claims against an insurance company may be shown by parol evidence to extend only to sick indemnity (868). 

53. Contractual receipts.-Parol evidence is inadmissible to bring in consideration in addition to contractual consideration. Where the minds of the parties are directed to the precise matter of mutual covenants setting forth the consideration on which the agreement rests, then the stipulation of consideration is contractual and cannot be varied by parol evidence any more than any other contract (869).

Stipulations in checks that payment is in full operate as contractual receipts although a greater debt is thereby settled (870).

An ambiguous recital on a check "for total and permanent disability payment" does not operate as a release in full, although subsequently a letter so reciting was received by the payee. The offer and acceptance must be clear and unequivocal (870-a). 

Negotiable instruments in the hands of a holder in due course cannot be shown to be based on no consideration. The promise runs directly to the holder, and its provision may not be varied (871). 

54. Variation of terms of contracts. Variation of the terms of contracts by parol evidence is not permitted (872). The following topics are within this rule; contemporaneous oral agreements to make refunds on conditions (873); not to hold indorser personally liable (874); for acceptance of negotiable instrument to be conditional (875); to qualify a blank indorsement (876); that primary parties are sureties (877); to ingraft conditions on a bond (878); to change liability to pay interest (879); contradiction of a stipulation reserving a vendor's lien (880); to add covenant to erect a fence (881); or a party wall (882); to add a restrictive covenant (883); to show real parties in interest using a firm name (884); to add or to omit a clause by which a debt was assumed (885); to change a person contracted with (886); to supply essential terms to take a case out of the 3-year statute of limita-

tions (887); to change a lease into a sales contract (888); to add a provision requiring payment of rent (889); to add a promise to repair (890); to restrict the size of trees sold (891); to add a warranty (892); to add a promise of a lifetime job (893), or to teach a trade (894); to change a sales contract into one of agency (895); to add a provision requiring advertisement of goods sold, or payment of a bonus (896); to show variation of writing from oral agreement where fraud in factum is not alleged (897); to change specifications (898); to change the character of a letter of credit (899); to show as against a creditor fraudulent representations made by principal to sureties (900).

Parol evidence is inadmissible to show fraudulent inducements by an agent of the other party where the contract stipulates that it contains all representations and warranties (901). 

55. Conditional sale.-Parol evidence is inadmissible to show that a sale is a conditional sale (902). 

56. Bailment.-Loss of bailed goods may be shown by testimony and receipts independently of the books of the bailor, cotton-warehouseman (903).

Prior to modern statutes, parol evidence was admissible to show nonreceipt by carrier of goods listed in a bill of lading, as against an innocent purchaser for value (904). 

Nonreceipt of a package by an employee of a carrier may be shown, although the employee signed a receipt for it (905). 

57. Nuns.-Parol evidence is inadmissible to show nuns received property contrary to rules of their order (906). 

58. Application for credit.-Parol evidence is inadmissible to show that a schedule of liabilities in an application for credit was not meant to be complete (907). 

59. Bond. The court term named in a bond may not be varied by parol evidence (908).

Parol Evidence 

131 The time of payment of a written contract, a note, deed of trust, may not be varied by parol (909). 

60. Performance.-A substituted mode of performance may be shown by parol evidence (910).

Specifically, the medium of payment, place of sale under a deed of trust, the quantity of merchandise to be delivered under a sales contract may be changed by parol (911). 

Where a contract is silent as to the exact mode of performance, parol evidence is admissible to show a specific agreement, as, the place of storage of cotton (912). 

Parol evidence is inadmissible to change the res of a release of dower, or to show that a cancellation was to be partially ineffective (913).

A passenger's statement to flagman that he would get off the train before it reached his destination does not waive his right to be carried to the destination (914). 

61. Contracts required to be in writing.-It seems that contracts required to be in writing cannot be subsequently altered or modified except by writing (915). 

Memoranda for sale of land may not be aided by oral evidence describing the land or showing part performance (916). 62. 

Payment. Payment may be shown by parol evidence (917).

The amount of payment, the medium of payment [contra (918)] may not be varied by parol evidence (919).

By an early statute, parol evidence was admissible to show the kind of money a contract was intended to call for (920). 

63. Value of commodity credit.-Parol evidence is admissible to show the true value of a commodity credit (921). 

64. Application of payment.-Mode of application of payments may not be varied by parol evidence (922).

But an oral agreement not varying a contract, which was ambiguous in this respect, is competent (923).

A parol agreement by creditor, payee of a note, with the maker to apply the real debtor's cotton on the note is valid (924). 

65. Waiver of liens.-Parol evidence is admissible to show waiver of vendor's lien, lien of a conditional sales contract (925), and waiver of priority of lien (925-a). 

66. Of notice to indorser.-An oral agreement at the time of indorsement to waive notice to the indorser is incompetent (926). 

67. Waiver of provisions of insurance policy.-Parol evidence of waiver of terms of an insurance policy is competent, viz., provision against storing oil, requiring proof of loss (927). 

68. Place of delivery.-Parol evidence is admissible to show the place of delivery of goods sold if the written contract is silent (928). 

69. Arbitration.-It seems parol evidence is admissible to show that arbitrators have gone beyond their authority and beyond the issues submitted, where the arbitration agreement is not specific in this (929). 

70. Liability of sureties inter se.-The liability of sureties inter se may be shown by parol-if, it seems, the contract is silent as to the proportion to be borne by each (930). 

71. Lost records.-Parol evidence is inadmissible to show a lost drainage tax roll had been restored (931).

Lost citations to court and a lost receipt may be shown by parol evidence (932). 

72. Mistake.-Mistake in giving a tax receipt by one officer to another may be shown by parol evidence (933).

In equity, in subjecting property to a debt, mistake in describing parties may be shown by parol. At law it is inadmissible to show mistake (934).

In reformation, mistake or fraudulent omission may be shown by parol evidence (935).

Parol Evidence 

133 

73. Fraud; in factum.-Fraud in factum in a deed or contract may be shown by parol evidence (937). 

74. Fraud in inducement.-Fraud in the inducement may be shown by parol evidence (938). 

75. Fraud; patent.-A collateral attack cannot be made on a patent for fraud (936). 

76. Blue sky law. Under Blue Sky Law fraudu lent representations of agents may be shown, although the written contract stipulates that all representations are written in it (939). 

77. Usury, nuisance, liquor offenses.-Usury, immoral purpose of a lease of a house, for prostitution or sale of liquor, orders for sale of liquor may be shown by parol evidence (940). 

78. Contracting as agent.-Where a person signs a contract as an agent, parol evidence is admissible to show his acts bind the principal (941). 

79. Subsequent agreements.—Subsequent agreements may be shown by parol, as agreements to carry insurance on cars (942); extras, in building project (943); waiver of tenant's right to cut timber (944). 

80. New promise to avoid statute of limitation.— 

Parol evidence is incompetent to show the new promise to take a case out of the statute of limitation (945). 

81. Indefinite contract.-A contract too indefinite to show any obligation cannot be aided by contemporary oral agreements (946). 

82. Stipulations.-Stipulations between counsel must be in writing. The rule does not apply to stipulations made by a litigant not an attorney (947). 

83. Rescission.-Rescission of a contract may be shown by letters of a party (948). no

B. BEST EVIDENCE 

1. Rule. The best evidence of writings to be proved must be produced. If the original is not available after due efforts to get it, secondary evidence may be introduced (949). The foregoing rule is inapplicable to writings which are collateral to the issues (949-a). 

2. Code. The enrolled draft of the Mississippi Code and statutes, approved by the Governor and deposited with the Secretary of State, is the best evidence of the code of statute law, and it controls where the printed code conflicts (950). Enrolled acts are signed by the President of the Senate, the Governor of the State, and the Speaker of the House (951).

The printed code is evidence of what the law is (952). 

Ordinances.-Where the ordinance book cannot be found, the minute book is competent to show ordinances (953). 

4. Confirmation of appointments.-The Senate jour nal is the best evidence of confirmation of appointment by the Senate (954). 

5. Election results. The report of commissioners of elections is the best evidence of the result (955). 

6. Registration books.-Registration books of voters are competent evidence as to the number of voters; death and disqualification may be shown aliunde (956). 

7. Tax rolls and receipts.-Tax rolls are the best evidence of the charges against the collector, and minutes of the Board of Supervisors are the best evidence of allowance of insolvencies (957).

Tax receipts and duplicates are the best evidence of taxes collected (958). A certified copy of a tax collector's bond is competent (959). 

8. Privilege license.-A privilege license or record thereof is the best evidence of the license (960).

Best Evidence 

135 9. Land records.-A Spanish patent and certificate of confirmation by Mississippi commissioners is the best evidence of a Spanish grant (961).

An Indian treaty certificate of location is competent evidence of location of land under the treaty (962). 

Certified copies of plats on file in the General Land Office and of records of entry, and reports of sales of swamp lands by supervisors, and plats are competent (963). 

The list of land sold to the State for taxes is the best evidence of the sale (964).

Where a deed of land to the State is lost, official lists of land held by the State are competent (965). 

10. Judicial records.-Judicial records are the best evidence of their contents—as records of final judgments to show a judgment, and not the minute book, or a transcript (966); the file of court proceedings, pleadings, exhibits, writs, process and returns, and records, or an authorized substitute (967); judgment rolls, awards (968). 

11. Impeachment of witnesses.-A witness may be asked whether he has been convicted of any crime without producing the record, but if any other means of proof is sought, the record must be produced (969). 

12. Negative fact.-Persons having knowledge of judicial records may testify that a named record does not show a given fact, i. e., that no divorce had been decreed of named persons in a named court (970). 

13. Disputed foreign records.-Disputed instruments recorded in foreign states must be presented or loss accounted for before certified copies may come in (971). 

14. Board of Supervisors; School Board.-The minutes of the Board of Supervisors of counties or of school boards are the best evidence of their acts (972).

15. 

Search warrant.-A search warrant and affidavit are the best evidence of their issuance and content (973). 

16. Corporate acts.-Corporate books are the best evidence of corporate acts (974). 

17. Contracts, deeds, etc.-The original instrument is the best evidence of contracts, deeds, notes (975). 

If executed in duplicate or triplicate, etc., each is an original (976). 

18. Market value.-Newspaper reports are the best evidence of the market value of cotton on a given day (977). 

19. Telegrams. In suits against telegraph companies for nondelivery or failure to deliver a message promptly, the telegram received and transcribed at the final destination shall be conclusive evidence in the hands of the addressee of the filing of the original and may be introduced as the best evidence of the filing of the original by the sender (978). 

20. License to sell liquor.-The best evidence of a license to sell liquor is the license (979). 

21. Record of testimony.-The statutory record of testimony before a justice of the peace, duly filed, is the best evidence of such testimony, and secondary evidence is not admissible, unless the loss of the record is shown, or unless no record was made (980).

Stenographers' notes are not the best evidence of former testimony. Any person, otherwise competent, may testify to what a witness testified (981). 

Oral evidence of an admission or other fact is admissible, although reduced to writing or memorandum by the witness (981-a).

Best Evidence 

137 

22. Cumulative evidence. Where the best evidence has been presented, testimony to the same effect may be presented, and then it is not secondary evidence (982). 

23. Books of account.--Books of account are not the exclusive evidence of the transactions recorded therein (983). But copies of the books are incompetent unless the originals are duly accounted for (984). 

In taking depositions, original accounts may be copied (985). 

24. Loss. Loss of the best evidence may be shown by a witness who is disqualified by interest (986). 

25. Secondary evidence.-Secondary evidence of writings is inadmissible unless loss or inaccessibility is shown after diligent search (987). 

26. Notice to Produce.-Generally, a notice to produce the best evidence in possession of the opposing party must be given a reasonable time before the trial (988). 

The notice must give a reasonably definite description of the writing (989).

Notice is unnecessary where the writing is the basis of the action (990).

Where a note is sued on and a copy is made an exhibit, but its execution is not denied, then where a part of the record is lost, no notice is required to the other side before proving a copy of the note, under Section. 

2337, Code, 1930 (990-a). 

27. Subpoena duces tecum.-A witness served with subpoena duces tecum must bring the papers called for to court and leave it to the court to decide whether they are privileged (991).

D. STATUTE OF FRAUDS 1. Statutes.-Section 3343, Code, 1930 (Section Four, 

Statute of Frauds); Sections 3344-6, 3348-50, 3347, Code, 1930 (Section Seventeen, Statute of Frauds). 

2. Persons subject.-Parties to the contract may invoke the statute, but not strangers (1114). 

3. Gifts.-A verbal gift of land is void (1115).

As against heirs of a husband, a parol gift by the wife is valid (1116). i. Land 

3-a. Land sale.-An oral contract to convey land is unenforceable (1116-a). 

4. Land partition.-A parol partition followed by possession is not within the statute (1117). 

5. Boundary dispute.-An oral agreement to settle a boundary dispute followed by possession is competent (1117-a). 

6. Exchange.-—A parol exchange of lands followed by possession is within the statute of frauds (1118). 

7. Purchase and convey.-A parol agreement by one to purchase land in his name and to convey half of it is not within the statute of frauds (1119).

8. Priority of lien.-A parol agreement by a lienor for a lender of new money to have a priority over him is not within the statute (1120). But an equitable mortgage cannot lawfully be created by the mere deposit of the title papers (1121). 

9. License.-A parol license to enter land and construct a railway is not within the statute, but a parol easement is within it (1122). 

10. Remove building.-An oral reservation of the right to remove a building is within the statute, but an oral agreement to build a dividing-line fence is not within it (1123). 

11-13. To erect a building; sell trees; commission. 

Oral permission to build a school on land gives an irrevocable license. An oral sale of standing trees is void (1124). An oral agreement to pay a commission for selling land is valid (1125). 

14. Resulting trusts.-Resulting trusts are not within the statute (1126). 

15. Partnership.-Partnership agreements are not within the statute (1127). 

16. Promises in consideration of marriage.-Agreements in consideration of marriage are within the statute. An oral agreement by parties to a marriage prior to marriage but after the engagement is not based on the marriage, and is valid (1128). ii. Personalty 

17. Cut timber.-A contract for the sale of cut timber is valid where part delivery is made, for cut timber is personalty (1129). 

18. Swap horses.-An oral contract to exchange horses is within the 17th section of the statute of frauds (1130).

Statute of Frauds 

153 

19. Stock. Corporate stock is personalty, within the statute (1131). 

20-21. Insurance; wills.-Oral contracts of insurance or to make will are valid (1132). 

22. Leases and contracts performable over a period of time.-A three year oral lease is void (1133).

An oral lease for a crop season is valid (1134). The year-period for leases begins with the term of the lease, not with the execution of the contract (1135). An oral lease from year to year is valid for one year (1136). 

An oral assignment of a lease is valid (1137). 

An oral agreement to renew an insurance policy at its expiration, which was three years from the time of the agreement, is invalid (1138).

An oral agreement to perform personal services not within 15 months is void (1139). Various instances of contracts within the 15 months clause occur (1140). An oral promise of a life-time job is valid (1141). 

23. Direct promises.-A purchaser who bought in an associate's name which was used as a trade name, and who promised to pay for the goods is bound by the promise if he bought for his own use (1142).

An oral promise to pay for goods in a joint account made by one of the parties is binding (1143). 

Where a credit is extended to a person, his oral promise to pay is binding although he ordered delivery to be made to another (1144).

A vendor's agent's oral promise to apply part of the purchase money to pay taxes due on the land is valid (1145). 

A vendee's oral promise to pay the vendor's debt with the purchase money is valid (1146).

An oral promise of one creditor to divide a payment with another creditor is valid (1147). 

An agent's agreement to bear 1/2 of losses from purchasers' defaults is not within the clause as to promises to pay the debts of another (1148).

A party's oral promise to pay a carrier for loss of cars by fire is valid (1149).

A houseowner's oral promise to pay a materialman the amount a contractor owed for materials furnished the contractor for use in his house is valid, for it is an application of the contract price to material and not a promise to pay the debt of another (1150). 

An oral promise based on consideration to pay the promisee's creditor is valid (1151). 

A novation may be oral (1152).

An oral promise to pay another's debts, which promise is based on performance of a previously existing obligation is invalid (1153). 24.

Executed agreements.-The statute of frauds does not apply to executed agreements (1154). 

25. Waiver.-An oral waiver, acted on, which excused performance of a written contract for sale of personalty or to cut trees is valid (1155). 

But an oral contract for the sale of land, though followed by possession and part performance is invalid (1156). 

26. Acts by agents.-Agent's authorization to contract to sell land need not be in writing (1157).

Authorization of an agent to convey must be in writing (1158).

At law authorization to lease land must be in writing, but equity will enforce a lease of an agent orally appointed (1159).

A memorandum of the sale of land may be signed by a sheriff at a sheriff's sale,- or by an auctioneer at an auctioneer's sale (1160). 

27. Delivery of writing.-The writing required by the statute must be delivered (1161). 

28. Signature by party to be charged. The signature by the party to be charged is sufficient; the filing of suit by the other party satisfies the rule of mutuality (1162). 

29. Estoppel.-The nonsigning vendee of land who records the deeds and enters into possession is estopped

Statute of Frauds 

155 to assert he did not sign the deed and the vendor may recover the purchase price (1162-a). 

30. Description and terms.-The description and terms must be definite (1163). Parol evidence cannot be used in aid of an incomplete memorandum (1164).

The price is an essential term and must be written, but the place of delivery need not be in writing (1165). 

The relation of the parties and the thing agreed on must be shown in writing, location of land, or quantity of personalty, terms of payment, and the disposition of the res (1166). 

31. Mechanical form.-Correspondence containing the terms of an agreement satisfies the statute; the writing need not be formally drawn on a single page or pages attached together as a formal document (1167). 

An unsigned document may be incorporated by reference (1168). 

32. Part delivery.-Delivery must be accepted to satisfy the statute (1169). 

A buyer's directing the banking of sugar cane on the seller's land does not amount to accepting dominion or part delivery (1170).

A seller's agreement to hold cattle for the buyer does not constitute delivery and render the seller a bailee (1171). 

Tagging cotton by a buyer is not delivery where the seller kept the warehouse receipts (1172).

Delivery of a pitcher is a good delivery in part, of a soda water fountain and equipment (1173).

Part delivery of a shipment of ice, cotton, etc., satisfies the statute (1174). 

33. Part performance.-Part performance of a contract for sale of personal property satisfies the statute, as by surrendering a stipulated position (1175), giving a book credit on a debt owed by the seller (1176), but part performance of a sale of land cannot be substituted for the writing required by law (1177).

IV. TESTIMONIAL EVIDENCE A. WITNESSES 

1. Qualifications. In general, the character of the witness, his opportunity to observe correctly, his powers of recollection, the reasonableness of his testimony in the light of the undisputed facts and of reason and experience are the factors to be considered by the jury in weighing testimony (1178). 

2. Age.-A witness six years of age is not incompetent because too young; all that is required is capacity to observe, recall, and communicate in answer to questions (1179).

The child need not know the penalty for perjury. 

Formerly he was required to understand the wrong of lying and the divine sanctions imposed; but it is probable that the abolition of religious belief as a qualification changes this rule (1180).

But the child must show some sense of duty to speak the truth, and not merely what he has been told to say (1181). 

3. Impaired ability.-Although a witness has had a serious brain injury, if he is rational, recalls facts, and can talk, he is competent (1182).

In impeaching an eye-witness, defective vision may be shown (1183).

A witness may state whether he has ability to hear (1184). 

4. Deaf mutes.-Deaf mutes are competent witnessess if intelligent and able to express themselves by signs. Interpreters who can read the signs may be used.

The oath is administered both to the deaf-mute and to the interpreter (1185). 

5. Duress prior to trial.-Duress imposed on a witness prior to trial does not disqualify him (1186).

Testimonial Evidence 

157 

6. Inability to understand oath.-Although a witness has testified, his inability to understand the nature of an oath may be shown, as affecting the weight of his testimony (1187). 

7. Present drunkness.-A witness may be asked whether he is intoxicated at the time he testifies and other proof may be resorted to, the court states (1188). 

8. Opportunity to observe.-An admission or testimony clearly not based on observed facts should be excluded (1191).

Where a witness has heard a person speak, he may identify him from his voice (1189).

A witness who has seen persons write may identify their handwriting (1190).

A person may testify to his own state of mind in uttering an alleged libel (1192). 

9. Impression.-An impression is admissible where denoting recollection (1193). 

10. Oath.-An affirmation may be substituted for an oath on the part of conscientious objectors (1194). 

11. By whom administered.-The oath may be administered by judges, clerks, masters in chancery. members of boards of supervisors, unemployment commission, board of disability for relief appeals, justices of the peace, notarys public, mayors, police justices, officers authorized by law of other states, the Federal Government or foreign states, or by de facto administrators of oaths (1195). 

12. Essential.-The oath is essential (1196). Chain bearers in surveys are treated as other witnesses and need not be sworn in advance (1196-a).

In estates in chancery, attorney's statements to the court are covered by the attorney's oath (1197). 

13. Religious belief.-Religious belief is not essential (1198).

14. Witnesses in chancery.-In chancery, witnesses are examined as at law, viz., orally (1199).

Opinions of witnesses 

15. In general.-A witness must testify to facts. 

Where facts are complex or elusive and practically indescribable, a witness may give his opinion (1200). 

i. Instances in Which Opinion Is Inadmissible Criminal Cases 

16. Age. Opinion evidence is inadmissible to show age when the exact age of a person is in issue (1201). 

17. Position of criminals.-Opinion evidence is inadmissible to show the position of actors at the scene of crime. The facts observed must be given (1202). 

18. Tracks. Tracks may not be identified by opinion evidence (1203). 

19. Nature of weapon.-Opinion evidence that a stick is a deadly weapon is incompetent (1204). 

20. Breed of hounds.-That hounds are pure bred may not be shown by opinion evidence (1205). 

Civil cases 21. Torts. The proper construction of cattle guards may not be shown by opinion evidence (1206).

An opinion is incompetent that a cold-chisel is defective (1207).

It is error to permit a deputy sheriff to testify that a railway crossing is dangerous, instead of requiring him to state the facts merely on which his opinion rests (1207-a).

The amount of damage from depredations of cattle cannot be shown by the mere opinion of a witness; the basis of the estimate must be shown (1208). 

22. Medical cases. Nonexpert's opinion as to the cause of physical disorders is incompetent (1209).

Testimonial Evidence 

159 

23. Number of workmen required to lift a load. —Opinion of the inability of six workmen safely to lift a log 15' x 12" is not sufficient to support a verdict (1210). 

Contracts 

24. Damages.-Damages from breach of contract may not be shown by opinion evidence. Factors of cost may be shown by opinion (1211). 

25. Legal effect.-Opinion is incompetent as to legal effect of a contract (1212). 

26. Solvency.-Solvency may not be shown by opinion. An auditor's opinion based on an audit is competent on this issue (1213). 

27. Ownership and boundary.-Opinion is incompetent to show ownership or the location of a boundary (1214). 

28. Undue influence.-A witness' opinion that undue influence was exerted on a testator is incompetent (1215). 29. 

Proper storage of cotton.-Opinion is incompetent to show a place was safe for storing cotton (1216). 

30. Meritorious defense.-Opinion is incompetent to show a meritorious defense existed (1217). 

31. Construction of writing.-Opinion is incompe tent to construe writing, terms of art not being used (1218). 

ii. Instances in Which Opinion Is Admissible 

32. Race. Opinion as to the race of a person is competent (1219). 

33. Opinion as to mode of electric shock.-Opinion as to the mode of electric shock of a passenger on a car is competent (1220). 

34. Handwriting.-Handwriting may be shown genuine by nonexpert opinion (1221).

35. Value of land.-Land value may be shown by opinion (1222). 

36. Damage of buildings.-Extent of damage to a building may be shown by opinion (1223). 

37. Gems. The value of gems may be shown by the opinion of one experienced in the diamond market (1224). 

38. Corporate stock.-The value of corporate stock may be shown by testimony as to the facts forming the basis of the witness' opinion and by the opinion of the witness (1225). 

39. Merchandise.-Merchants may testify to their opinion of the value of a general line of merchandise and each is not restricted to his own special line (1226). 

40. Oats. An oats dealer may give his opinion of the value of oats (1227). 

41. Car damage.-A nonexpert may give his opinion. of the value of a car after a wreck (1228). 

42. Dog.-A dog-owner may give an opinion as to a dog's value (1229). 

43. Gun.-Nonexperts may give opinions of the value of a gun (1230). 

Medical Cases 

44. Sanity.-Nonexperts may give opinions as to the sanity of a person if first the observed basis of the opinion is elicited (1231). 

45. Cancer. A physician's opinion as a nonexpert is competent as to the development of cancer after a blow (1232). 

46. Blood.-Nonexpert opinion is competent that stains resemble blood and were taken for blood (1233).

Testimonial Evidence 

161 

47. Pneumonia.-Nonexpert opinion based on experience that pneumonia of cattle was caused by “dipping" is competent (1234). 

48. Drunkenness.-The fact and extent of drunkenness may be shown by opinion (1235).

Expert Opinion 

49. In general. A general medical practitioner is not qualified to speak as an expert on insanity. A physician who has made special study of medicine although not a graduate and not licensed may testify as an expert on matters of general medicine, for example, as to whether a substance he tested was strychnine (1236).

Trained nurses are not qualified to testify as experts (1237). 

50. Cause of death.-The cause of death may be shown by the opinion of physicians (1238). 

51. Suicide.-Whether fatal wounds in the head were suicidal may be shown by a physician's opinion (1239). 

52. Wounds and course of bullet.—The course of bullet.-The place of entrance and exit of a bullet in a body may be shown by a physician but he may not give an opinion as to the course of the bullet in the body (1240).

He may testify as to what organs would be cut if the bullet took a specified course (1241).

X-ray interpretation.-A general practitioner or xray technician who has had experience in examining x-ray pictures is competent to interpret an x-ray picture (1242). 

53. Paranoia.-A physician may testify that paranoiacs are dangerous (1243). 

Experts may not be asked whether the insane kill for money (1244). 

54. Rape.-Experts may testify to the effect on a child's genitalia of rape by an adult; but not whether

the condition in a given case was caused by intercourse (1245), or voluntary act (1245-a). 

A physician may express an opinion on virginity, the opinion being based on physical examination (1246). 

54-a. Effect of high blood pressure, etc.-A physician may be asked whether a patient suffering from hardening of the arteries and high blood pressure is able to work, for a layman cannot decide this question without the assistance of expert medical testimony; and the physician's opinion is admissible (1246-a). 

55. Distillery mash.-An officer experienced in enforcing the liquor laws may give an opinion as to whether certain mash could be used in making whiskey (1247). 

56. Visibility on railway and stopping trains.-A railwayman may give an opinion on the range of visibility at a certain place on a track and the distance in which the train could be stopped (1248). 

57. Handwriting.-Handwriting experts may express opinions on the genuineness of handwriting (1249). 

58. Audit.-Auditors, as experts, are competent to explain complicated audits (1250). 

59. Weights.-Estimates of weights by persons ex perienced in weighing are competent (1251). 

60. Quantity of timber cut.—An experienced lumberman is competent to express an opinion as to the quantity of timber cut on observed tracts (1252). 

61. Reasonable worth of attorney's fees.-Attorneys may give opinions as to the value of legal services (1253). 

62. Opinion in argument of cases.-An attorney may give his opinion of the merits of a case in arguing it before the jury (1254). 

63. Appraisers.-Appraisers may give opinions as to the value of damaged insured goods (1255).

Testimonial Evidence 

163 64. Surveyors.-Surveyors may testify as to their surveys if their instruments have been corrected within the year (1256). 

Hypothetical Questions to Experts 65. Factual basis.-Generally each fact stated hypothetically in a question must have some support in evidence (1257) 

66. Conflicting evidence.-Hypothetical questions may frame only one version of conflicting evidence (1258). 

67. Form. The form should be, if given facts are true, and are proved to the satisfaction of the jury, then what is your opinion as to the issue specified (1259).

It is in the discretion of the court to permit a question merely referring to all the testimony and not stating the facts hypothetically, where the witness heard all the testimony and there is no conflict in it (1260). 

C. TESTIMONIAL USE OF MEMORANDA 

68. Present recollection.-A witness may use a memorandum to refresh his present recollection. The other side has a right to inspect the memorandum and to crossquestion the witness regarding it (1261). 

69. Past recollection.-Contemporaneous memoranda of facts known at the time by the witness are admissible in evidence. The witness need not have done the writing, provided he adopted it. Present recollection of the facts is not required. A memorandum of facts reported in part by helpers is admissible (1262).

A memorandum made two years after the event is not admissible as a past memorandum because not contemporaneous (1263). 

70. Surveys and maps.-Private surveys made after filing of suit are admissible (1264).

A map is admissible which a witness testified was a fair representation of the grounds and scene of the homi-

cide, the said witness having measured the place (1265). 

A map should be made part of the record on appeal, to render testimony intelligible (1266).

Diagrams illustrating testimony may be exhibited to the jury (1267). 

71. Photographs.-Photographs are not evidence themselves apart from the witness who made it and who knows it is accurate. It is evidence of what he knows or has recorded, and is similar in legal effect to maps and memoranda (1270). The place must be substantially the same as at the time the cause of action occurred (1271). 

Photographs made after the crime which show persons standing in the positions which were testified to have been the positions of the defendant and the victim at the time of the crime are not admissible because too vivid (1268). But this placing of persons has been permitted in a tort case (1269).

Moving pictures.-Where a competent motion picture photographer with a motion picture camera of standard make in good condition which would reproduce accurately a scene photographed including the speed at which persons were moving, and where the film was made by an expert and was in the same condition at the trial as when made, and when it is offered to project the picture on a screen with a projector of standard make and in good condition operated by a competent operator, then such evidence by means of motion pictures is admissible, and it is reversible error to exclude it (1269-a). 

72. X-ray pictures.-X-ray pictures are admissible which are identified from memoranda on them and on the envelope, and are shown to have been taken by a skilled person on a proper machine (1272).

D. HEARSAY 

73. Generally.-Hearsay evidence is inadmissible, i. e., extra-judicial statements used to show the truth of the thing stated (1273).

Testimonial Evidence 

165 

i. Law, Records, Documents, etc. 

Hearsay Exceptions 

74. Law.-Printed statutes, published by authority of the legislature, are admissible (1274). 

Copies of ordinances certified by the clerk are admissible (1275). 

75. Marriage certificate.-Certified copies of marriage certificates are admissible (1276). 

76. Habeas corpus affidavit.-Affidavits of witnesses unable to attend may be admitted in habeas corpus proceedings (1277). 

77. Injunction proceedings.-On motion to dissolve, affidavits may be introduced. Certified copies of the bond may be used as basis of execution when the injunction is dissolved (1278). 

78. Agency Insurance Commissioner to receive process.-Copies of his appointment to receive process certified by the insurance commissioner are admissible (1279). 

79. Judgments.-Certified copies of judgments are admissible (1280). 

80. Copies of writings filed in court.-Certified copies of writings filed in another court are admissible (1281). 

81. Judgment of justice of the peace on land.-Certified copies of judgments of justices of the peace conveying land are admissible (1282). 

82. Public records generally.-Certified copies of public records are admissible, viz., of counties, Federal 

Government, etc. (1283). 

83. Maps and plats.-The record or copy of any writing, map, plat or other instrument required or permitted to be recorded is admissible when certified to by the clerk in custody of the record (1284).

84. Acknowledged writings.-Acknowledged writ- ings are admissible without further proof (1285). A statutory certificate may be given in lieu of an acknowledgment (1286). 

85. Land certificates and field notes.-Land-office certificates are admissible. Certified copies of field notes are admissible. A certificate of a survey by a county surveyor is admissible, as to land in his county (1287). 

86. Book of entries.-Certified copies from books of entries of land in official state books are admissible (1288). 

87. Ancient deeds.-Ancient deeds over 30 years old are admissible (1289). 

88. Wills.-An affidavit of an attester to a will is admissible to prove the will. Certified copies of wills of record are admissible (1290). 

89. Appointment of foreign administrator.-A certified copy of the record of appointment of an executor, administrator, or guardian in another state or county is admissible (1291). 

90. Foreign records.-Certified copies of foreign public records are admissible (1292). 

91. Lost records.-An officer's certificate that a record of his office is lost is admissible (1293). 

92. No divorce.-A clerk's certificate that no divorce has been granted in his court to named persons is admissible (1294). 

93. Proof of publication.-A publisher's affidavit is competent to prove publication (1295).

An affidavit of the posting of a notice is competent (1296). 

94. Bonds.-Certified copies of bonds of officers may be used in suits on the bonds (1297). 

95. Liquor shipments.-Certified copies of official records of liquors used for medicine are admissible (1298).

Testimonial Evidence 

167 96. Protest.-Certified copies of records of dishonor and protest and notice of protest of negotiable instruments are admissible (1299). 

97. Constitution and by-laws of fraternal insurance societies. Printed copies of constitution and laws of fraternal insurance societies are admissible when certified by the secretary of the society (1300) 98. Cotton receipts and weights of compress company. -Cotton compress weights and receipts are competent (1301). 

99. Vital statistics. Certified copies of records of vital statistics of this and other states are competent (1302).

The certificate is limited to physical facts as distinguished from legal acts, as suicide (1303). Age may not be shown by death certificate where birth certificate is not produced (1304). 

100. State chemist.-By statute, certificates of the state chemist showing analysis of food, feed, fertilizer, motor fuels, paints, soil are admissible, but in tort action his analysis of a bottled drink or of blood is inadmissible (1305). 

101. Records of State Insane Hospital.-Certified copies of records of the State Insane Hospital are admissible (1306). 

102. Certificates of Clerk of Board of Supervisors. -Certificates of the Clerk of the Board of Supervisors of defaults in transmitting lists of executions or sheriff's returns are admissible. The clerk's certificate of the appointment of a road overseer is admissible (1307). Commissioner.-The 

103. Certificate of Insurance certificate of an Insurance Commissioner that a fraternal benefit insurance company has complied with the law of organizing as a corporation is competent (1308). Other records may be certified by him (1309).

104. Register of engineers.—The register of proceed ings of the State Board of Engineers and registrations of engineers are competent (1310). 

105. Notice of drainage proceedings. The affidavit of the chancery clerk or other credible person of the notice of the formation of drainage districts and of related proceedings is competent (1311). 

106. Testimony before Superintendent of Banks.—

The transcript of testimony before the Superintendent of 

Banks is admissible (1312). 

107. Certificates of officer, evidence of official character. Certificates of persons as officers of any state or of the United States are prima facie evidence of the official character of such persons (1313). ii. Confessions 

108. Rule.-Voluntary confessions are admissible. 

It is reversible error to admit involuntary confessions (1314).

The problem is to distinguish between voluntary and involuntary confessions. Confessions, to be admissible, must not proceed from a mind influenced to make them by hope of reward or fear of punishment (1315). 

109. What amounts to improper influence.-Con fessions gotten after the defendant had been whipped in jail are inadmissible (1316).

Confessions gotten by promises by a private person not to prosecute are inadmissible (1317).

Personal violence accompanied by statements that the defendant had better tell the truth constitute improper influence in getting a confession (1318).

Statements by a private person that he would assist the defendant to get a lighter sentence if he confessed render a confession secured thereby involuntary and inadmissible (1319). 

The gathering of an armed crowd and their taking the defendant into the woods and a promise by one of

Testimonial Evidence 

169 the crowd that if the defendant confessed he would release the defendant render a confession secured under these circumstances involuntary (1320). 110. 

What does not amount to improper influence. -It has been held that a statement by a private person that it would go lighter with the defendant if he confessed did not render the confession which followed involuntary. 

It seems each such case must be decided on its peculiar facts (1321).

Inquiries by private persons unaccompanied by threats or offers of aid do not render confessions given in response inadmissible although at the time the defendant was under custody (1322).

An ulterior motive, viz., the saving of the reputation of a hotal by confessing a liquor offense, does not render a confession involuntary (1323).

A confession gotten by a spiritualist by exciting the superstitions of a sick and nervous person is inadmissible (1324). 

111. Statement to officers.-Confessions which are ccerced by use of the sweat-box by officers are inadmissible (1325).

An officer's promise to save the defendant from a mob if he confessed constitutes undue influence and vitiates the confession (1326). 

An officer's promise of immunity from prosecution or aid of his influence vitiates a resulting confession (1327). 

An officer's statement to the defendant while a prisoner that it would be better to confess render inadmissible a confession secured thereby (1328). 

An officer's statement that he would turn the prisoner over to the federal officers vitiates a confession induced thereby (1329).

Where the accused was taken into custody by officers and was questioned by them, and an officer said "You might as well tell the truth so we can get the facts of it, and we will make it as light as we can," a confession given after the officer's statement, apparently in response thereto, is involuntary within the legal sense, and is inadmissible (1329-a).

112. What does not amount to improper influence. -A questioning marathon by officers, lasting from 9 A. M. to 5 A. M. the next day does not constitute undue influence (1330).

The fact the confessor is under arrest does not vitiate the confession (1331). 

113. Repetition of involuntary confession.-The repetition of a forced confession shortly thereafter in court in the presence of those who influenced the confessor to make it is presumed to be under the same influence and it is reversible error to exclude the evidence showing the former coercion outside court (1332).

A repetition of a forced confession the next day is taken to be under the same influence, but a repetition several months later where it is shown no improper influence was present is not presumed to be under improper influence (1333).

It must clearly appear that the repeated confession was not made under the same improper influences as the first (1334). The influence of fear must be removed where great pressure is put on a prisoner and a mere verbal assurance that the prisoner did not have to confess is insufficient where the influence of fear has not been removed (1335).

Confessions must be shown to be uninfluenced by fear of punishment or hope of reward (1336).

A repetition in court must appear to be free from the influence of the former improper influence (1337). Warning 

114. Statements to officers.-No warning is required outside court where a person confesses to officers or private persons. In court the prisoner should be warned that any statement he may make may be used against him. (1338).

Where a prior confession has been improperly obtained, a second confession is not admissible unless the confessor is warned that what he said may be used against him and is advised that he is free to remain silent (1339).

Testimonial Evidence 

171 114-a. Withdrawal of plea of guilty.-In a criminal action, the trial court has discretion to permit or refuse to permit withdrawal of a plea of guilty alleged to have been induced by duress and where the court examined a defendant-witness in open court before pronouncing sentence as to the facts charged and the defendant-witness admitted the facts and where the court found as a fact that the admission of guilt by plea was free and voluntary, then the court's refusal to permit the plea to be withdrawn is not error (1339-a). 

115. Scope. Where a confession reveals the locality of stolen property and the property is found in the place indicated, then so much of the confession as has been corroborated thus is admissible. An earlier decision holds that the state may not use evidence discovered by using a forced confession (1340). 

116. Function of court and jury.-Before being admitted a confession should be shown to be voluntary and the jury should be excluded if the defendant so requests. 

The court decides whether it is admissible (1341). 

The appellate court will not reverse the ruling of the trial judge unless it appears clearly the confession was improperly obtained (1342).

The jury should be instructed that if they believe the confession was induced by fear and was untrue, they should disregard it (1343).

It is reversible error to exclude from the jury testimony tending to show the confession was improperly obtained. The jury are entitled to all the circumstances (1344). 

117. Completeness of statement.-The witness should be required to give the substance of all the confessor said (1345). 

118. Other crimes.-A confession is incompetent if it contains confessions of other and distinct crimes (1346). 

119. Degree of proof of voluntariness of confessions. -For a confession to be admissible, every reasonable doubt

must be excluded which would show that the confession was procured by the hope of reward or the fear of punishment (1347). 

120. Time for objection.-The objection may be made for the first time in the Supreme Court, where in the course of the trial the trial court was informed of the coercion of the confessor. The competency of a confession need not be gone into prior to the trial (1348). 

121. Harmful error.-It is harmful error to admit an involuntary confession in a close case, although the jury were told later to disregard it (1349). 

iii. Admissions 

122. In pleadings.-All matters of fact averred in a bill and not denied other than by a general traverse may be taken as admitted at the hearing (1350). 

123. Signature and status.-In suits founded on written instruments set forth in the pleadings it shall be unnecessary to prove the execution or the signature unless the same be specially denied by plea, verified by oath (1351).

The existence of a corporation or partnership is admitted unless denied by sworn plea (1352). 

Stock subscriptions.-Stock subscriptions, calls and publication therefor are admitted unless denied by sworn plea (1353).

Sworn accounts.-Sworn accounts are admitted unless an affidavit is filed particularizing wherein it is untrue (1354). 124. 

Continuances.-Admissions of facts sought to be proved by absent witnesses shall have the same effect as if the absent witness were present in court and testified and no more, where a continuance is denied (1355). 

125. Former plea of guilty.-A former plea of guilty is an admission in another trial for the offense and also in a civil action (1356).

Testimonial Evidence 

173 

126. Nolo contendere.—But a plea of nolo contendere is not an admission (1357). 

127. Pleas in abatement.-Pleas in abatement setting up nonjoinder admit jurisdiction and later pleas setting up nonresidence and no jurisdiction should be rejected. A plea in abatement does not admit venue (1358). 

128. Pleadings in other actions. Sworn pleadings between the same parties in other actions are admissible to show the facts. Unsworn pleadings are not admissions (1359). 

129. Separate pleas in same action.-Allegations in a distinct plea in the same action are not admissions on trial of another distinct plea, (1360), except that the plea of justification in defamation may be considered in aggravation as evidence of intent, quo animo, under the general issue, and it is proper so to instruct (1360-a). 

130. Conflicting exhibits.—Where a party's exhibits conflict it is error not to permit him to show which is correct, and make an explanation, and to exclude the one most favorable to him (1361). 

131. Admissions into record in open court.-Admissions dictated into the record in open court are taken as true, as solemn admissions, unless something is done to correct the record (1362). 

132. By leading questions.—Where the plaintiff assumed in a line of leading questions that a barge had been destroyed the defendant may rest on such assumption and need not prove it as a fact in his defense for not returning it (1363). 

133. Admissions by demurrer.-The demurrer admits every material allegation of fact of the declaration, bill, or pleading demurred to, which allegation is well pleaded (1364).

A demurrer does not admit the conclusions of the pleader (1365).

A demurrer does not admit facts possibly inferable from those alleged (1366).

Extra-judicial Admissions 

134. Rule.-Admissions against a party's interest are admissible (1367). 

135. By lease.-A lease is an admission that the lessor had title when he made the lease (1368). 

By Parties Interested in the Same Transaction 

136. Conspirators.—Acts and declarations of conspirators are admissible against one another if made in furtherance of the conspiracy, but not after the accomplishment of the crime (1369). A prima facie showing of conspiracy is prerequisite (1370).

To make admissible the declaration of one of the joint actors in a killing made when the other actor was not present there must be proof of a conspiracy between the two to commit the unlawful act, and it is reversible error to admit a declaration by one of the actors to the effect that he was going to kill the victim where no evidence of conspiracy was offered, except the presence of both actors at the scene of the killing (1370-a). 

137. By next friend.—A statement by a next friend in regard to getting a ticket for the minor is inadmissible where the minor was not present at the time the statement was made (1371). 

138. By a tenant in common.-An admission by one tenant in common is not admissible against the other tenants in common (1372). 

139. By beneficiaries of an estate. An admission by one of several beneficiaries of an estate is inadmissible against the other beneficiaries (1373). 

140. Principal and surety.-Admissions of a principal are admissible against the surety (1374).

Testimonial Evidence 

175 

141. Ancestor; grantor: lessor.-Admissions of a landowner in possession are admissible against his heirs (1375). Similarly, of a grantor in possession, against his grantee (1376), and of a lessee in possession, against his sublessee. The admissions are not admissible if made after the interest of the one making the admission has been conveyed (1377). 

142. Holder of a note.-Admissions by the payee of a note made while a holder are admissible against a subsequent holder (1378). 

143. Real party in interest.—Admissions by assignee of insurance as the real party in interest are admissible although the wife of the insured is made a party (1379). 

144. Ancestor's admissions of marriage.—Ancestor's admissions of marriage are admissible in an action involving kinship of descendants (1380). 

145. Admissions by agents.-A witness may testify in court as to the extent of his powers, but his extrajudicial statements as to the fact or scope of his agency are not admissible unless first it is shown that he was an agent (1381). Even when this is shown, an agent must be acting in the scope of his agency, if his admissions out of court are to be received (1382). 

146. By principal.—An extra-judicial statement of the principal admitting another's agency for him is competent (1383). 

147. By administrator.-Admissions of an administrator made while acting in the scope of his authority are admissible against a later administrator de bonis non. 

But a later case holds there is no privity between the administrators here (1384). 

148. By an attorney.-An attorney's admissions made pending a suit that payments had been made on the debt in suit are competent (1385).

149. By a partner. The admissions of one partner about partnership business are admissible against another partner (1386). 

150. Admissions after agency ended.-—Statements made by agents referring to acts in the scope of their agency but after the event are inadmissible (1387). Such statements fifteen minutes after the event are incompetent (1388). 

Statements after the event are competent to show knowledge, where pertinent (1389). 

151. Admissions by agent's silence.—An agent's tacit admissions in relation to acts within his agency of which he has knowledge are competent (1390). 

152. Tacit admissions.-Tacit admissions are competent in all cases, civil and criminal, and consist of undenied charges or damaging statements made in the presence of the party charged under circumstances in which it would be natural for him to deny the charge if untrue (1391).

A charge of defendant's wife undenied by him is not admissible, because an indirect violation of the rule forbidding a wife to testify against him. Here the offense charged was rape of the defendant's daughter. This rule should be abolished (1392). 

153. Where denial is not required. The defendant is not expected to deny a charge made while he is wounded and lying on the ground unable to stand and suffering from great shock, or to deny statements made by his wife when she was hysterical, or a statement made by a relative of the slain man a few moments after the shot was fired (1393). 

154. Silence as to a contractual condition.-Failure to repeat a condition of a payment in a conversation relating to the payment does not signify that the condition does not exist (1394).

Testimonial Evidence 

177 

155. Denial.-Statements denied by the defendant at the time made are not admissions (1395). 

156. Statements in court.-Statements in defendant's presence in court are not admissions, although not denied by him (1396). 

157. Statements not in defendant's presence.-Statements against a defendant not made in his presence are not admissions (1397). 

158. Statements not amounting to a charge.—Undenied statements not making charges against the party are not admissions (1398). 

159. No admission by offer to compromise.-An offer to compromise a civil case is not an admission and is not admissible. But a voluntary offer to compromise a criminal case is admissible as an admission (1399). 

160. Admission of fact in offer to compromise.—A statement of fact made as fact in an offer to compromise is admissible as an admission, being against interest (1400). 

161. Recitals. Recitals in instruments are some evidence of the facts recited in such transactions and are admissible in actions on such instruments (1401). In checks, recital of payment in full are conclusive (1401-a). 

162. In divorce.-A wife's admissions shortly after separation, as to its cause, are admissible where nothing indicated collusion. The statute excluding admissions by answer or otherwise applies to collusive admissions (1402). 

163. Completeness.-All that was said in making a confession or admission should come in (1403). 

164. Foundation.-No foundation is required before admitting a party's admissions as in impeaching a witness who is not a party (1404). 

165. What constitutes an admission.-A woman's letters disavowing any intent to sue her lover do not con-

titute admissions that the man made no promise to marry her (1405). 

166. Distinguish opinion.-Opinions on the effect of a contract do not alter the effect of the contract (1406). 

Opinions as to what a party would have to do for a relative do not amount to admissions of legal liability (1407).

A patient's opinion diagnosing his own case does not preclude showing the true diagnosis (1408). 

167. Self-serving statements.-Statements of a CO
