maker that he had bought the note are self serving and are inadmissible against the holder (1409). 

168. Probative effect.-An admission of liability is sufficient to establish the debt (1410). 

Admissions should be received with caution because statements are easily misunderstood and are easily fabricated (1411). 

iv. Dying Declarations 

169. Rule.—Dying declarations are admissible provided: the declarant has abandoned hope of life and is convinced death is imminent and impending immediately, or, in a case of mercurial poisoning, within a few days, and provided the declarant has a fixed, hopeless and settled expectation of death as certain and impending at once; the declarant's mind is sane and rational; the declarant was in extremis when the statement was made and death followed; and that the declarations are limited to the sense of impending death, the homicide and attendant circumstances which are known to the declarant; and are without malice; and are limited to paternity, in bastardy proceedings (1412).

The sense of impending death must be shown beyond a reasonable doubt (1413); and this is not shown where the declarant was weak from loss of blood and was drowsy from a hypodermic injection, although he said several times that he was going to die (1414).

Testimonial Evidence 

179 170. Scope. The declaration is limited to the statements showing rationality, consciousness of impending death, the identity of the killer, the fact and causes and circumstances of the fatal act (1415).

The statement is competent that the defendant shot without cause (1416).

Why the defendant shot may not be included in the declaration (1417). The usual location of the bottle of poison is not competent in a dying declaration; but a statement that the defendant poisoned the declarant is competent (1418). 

Expressions of self-pity and opinion are incompetent (1419). 

171. Statements in favor of the defendant.-Dying declarations not made under a sense of impending death are not rendered admissible by the fact that they are favorable to the defendant (1420). 

172. Admissibility not affected by existence of other evidence. Although other evidence of the facts be admissible, dying declarations still are admissible (1421). 

173. Bastardy.-Declarations of paternity of a bastard made by a woman in travail are admissible if the declarant was under a sense of impending death and did die (1422). 

174. Completeness.-The complete declaration must be given, and the witness must not assume to omit parts he thinks immaterial or favorable to the defendant. If written, a copy may be used where the original has been destroyed (1423). 

175. Impeaching the declaration. Declarant's pression of desire that his murderer be punished renders his declaration inadmissible (1424). 

Religious disbelief may be shown as affecting the weight; it does not render the declaration inadmissible (1425).

Prior inconsistent statements of declarant are admissible (1426).

Evidence showing unconsciousness of the declarant after being shot and shortly before making the statement is admissible (1427). 

General statements throwing light on rationality of the declarant are admissible when it is contended he was irrational (1428). 

176. Constitutionality.-The rule admitting dying declarations does not violate the constitutional requirement of confrontation (1429). 

177. Cautionary instruction.-A cautionary instruction should be given to the effect that dying declarations are to be considered carefully by reason of the source and should be weighed with the circumstances, and the fact that it is hearsay should be considered, and they are weaker than testimony in court (1430).

It is proper to instruct for the state that a dying declaration is presumed to be made under a solemn and religious sense of approaching death and the jury should so consider until the contrary is shown (1431).

V. Other Declarations 178. Family tradition.-Common reputation and be lief in the family as to death, kinship, pedigree and marriage in the family are admissible (1432). Extrajudicial statements of a deceased person, a testator, as to who were his heirs are competent (1432-a). 

179. Reputation of marriage.-General reputation is not admissible to prove the existence or nonexistence of marriage in an action for distribution of an estate (1433). 

Fornication may not be shown by reputation (1434). 

180. Age. Declarations of age in age in naturalization papers are competent (1435). 

181. Domicile.-Declarations of an intent to change domicile are competent, when made ante litem motam or in absence of inducement to falsify (1436).

Testimonial Evidence 

181 182. Statements when stolen goods found in possession.-Defendant's statement made when stolen goods were found in his possession are competent, viz., that he said he bought the hogs but would not name the seller (1437). 

183. Declaration characterizing possession.-Contemporary declarations of a person in possession of personalty that he owned it are competent (1438).

Explanations of one while in possession of stolen property are admissible, but statements after he is caught are inadmissible (1439). 

184. Declarations regarding boundaries.-Declarations regarding location of a boundary accompanied by acts of forbearance beyond the declared line are admissible (1440).

An agreement in writing to divide land in accordance with a plat and report which had been held void is competent when followed by occupancy as agreed (1441). 

185. Declaration of a testator.-Testator's declara tions of his purpose and of the execution and content of a lost will are admissible to corroborate other testimony as to execution and content, but alone are insufficent (1443). 

Despoiled or lost will.-Upon the issue of revocation of a will the testator's declaration after executing a will of his intent not to revoke the will are admissible on the issue of revocation of a will found despoiled among the testator's papers (1442). 

186. Testamentary capacity.-On the issue of testamentary capacity and undue influence, a testator's plans and declarations of disposition made are admissible, which were made before and after the execution of it (1444). 

187. Res gestae.-The leading case in this jurisdiction treating "res gestae" gives the following rules: "Where declarations are admitted as a part of the res gestae, there is hardly any distinct rule as to what will

constitute the res gestae which will support such declarations."

Circumstances and statements accompanying and illustrating acts and showing their character are admissible. 

If the declarations are merely narrative, they are inadmissible. 

If declarations are not the natural and concomitant accompaniment of the principal fact in controversy and so connected as to be the result and consequence of coexisting motives, they are inadmissible.

Whatever expressions are naturally suggested by the coexisting motive and are referable only to that motive and conduct are admissible.

Declarations part of the res gestae must be made when the act is done which they characterize and must so unfold and characterize the act as to be part of the same transaction (1445).

Comment: Modern writers analyse the situations and deal with them separately under verbal acts, spontaneous exclamations, statements of present pain, recent complaints of prosecutrix in rape, etc.; and it is believed that this treatment tends to simplify the problem of the scope of the res gestae. 

In the leading case, declarations of a guardian were made to a scrivener whom he went to get to come and write his sick ward's will; and the guardian said to the scrivener that he did not know what the ward intended and had not asked her, for reasons of delicacy. The declarations were held incompetent because self-serving. The guardian was guilty of undue influence and knew all about the ward's intentions. He had contrived to have the will made in his favor, and to have the ward disinherit closer relatives in great need, whereas the guardian was rich (1446). 

vi. Verbal Acts 188. Upon delivery of an instrument. Declarations of a party to an instrument made at delivery as to whether it was a will or a deed are competent (1447).

Testimonial Evidence 

183 

189. Abuse of a passenger.-Personal abuse and threats to a passenger by an agent of a carrier, and refusal to let the passenger off a train are competent. Demand of fare is admissible as part of wrongful return of a passenger to the point of embarkation (1448). 

190. Declaration of authority.-Oral recognition of authority of an agent by a principal is competent where the parties agreed to abide by the answer (1449). 

191. Agent's statement during fire.-An agent's statement that he started a fire is admissible as part of the res gestae where the statement was made during the fire. 

It seems the statement might be classed as an admission or as an expression of volition concurrently with the deed, according to the intent with which the statement was made (1450). 

192. Declarations while arming.-Pacific declarations accompanying declarant's act of arming are competent (1451). 

193. Statements during negotiations.-Statements during negotiations are competent, as statements in employing a surveyor to run a boundary, statements made in purchasing property, and in executing notes (1452). 

194. At sale. Statements made in persuading prospective purchasers not to buy at a judicial sale are competent. Statements by the real purchaser at a judicial sale that he was purchasing through another for his own son are competent. It seems this is an admission, but may be considered as an expression of present volition accompanying an act (1455). 

195. Statements during stabbing.-Statements made while two men stabbed another to death are admissible (1453). 

196. Statements prior to negotiations.-Statements prior to negotiating purchases are inadmissible (1454).

197. Spontaneous exclamations.-"Verbal acts are admitted in evidence as facts, such as the exclamations accompanying pain or injury, the expressions of joy or grief, or surprise or emotion, naturally attending and emanating from these exciting causes, and referable to it alone; or to the casual, unpremeditated utterance of the intentions, motives or feelings, which give rise to or accompany an act” (1456). 

198. By-stander.-A by-stander's exclamation upon witnessing a murder is admissible. A by-stander's expostulation as a fight began is admissible (1457). 

199. Victim.-The victim's outcry and statement that his wife had murdered him are admissible (1458).

The deceased's statement immediately after the fight that he did not recognize the one he hit or he would have not hit him is admissible, where relevant to the identity of the person who hit the deceased in self-defense (1459). 

200. Outcries.-A witness may testify to outcries (1460). 

201. Threats.-A threat accompanying the act of taking a weapon is admissible (1461). 

202. Appeal for help.—The appeal of a victim of robbery for help stating at the time that he had been robbed is competent (1462). 

203. Narratives. Statements narrative of past events are incompetent, although made only 5 minutes after a stabbing which opened the abdominal cavity and prostrated the victim with shock. This and other decisions ignore the modern rulings on shock and spontaneous exclamations (1463). 

204. Self-serving statements afterward. Self-serving explanations half an hour after the one speaking had committed a crime are inadmissible (1464). 

205. Declarations by possessor of authority to sell. -An occupant of land declared to a purchaser that he

Testimonial Evidence 

185 had authority to sell timber on the land. His declarations are admissible upon the issue of the purchaser's good faith (1465). 

206. Statement bearing on mental states. Various statements bearing on mental states are admissible, as, those showing information acted upon by a husband in suing for a divorce, spite of a killer, knowledge of threats by the one threatened, as explaining his actions, and parent's motive for urging separation of a child from her husband (1466). 

207. Statements of present pain.-Statements out side court of present pain and symptoms of the one speaking are admissible (1467). 

208. Orbit. An insured person's statement of his past case-history is incompetent, the insured having no interest in the policy (1468). 

A patient's statement that she had drunk coffee an hour before being seized with pains of arsenic poisoning is inadmissible (1469).

A deceased person's statements of what medicines he had taken and which produced death are inadmissible (1470). 

Statements as to the cause of pain or abrasions are inadmissible as showing the cause, but are competent and admissible to show the basis of the physician's opinion and diagnosis (1471). 

209. Complaints of prosecutrix in rape. It is competent to show that the prosecutrix in rape made complaint of the fact of being raped soon after the act, but the details, time, and place, and the identity of the person charged are not admissible (1472). A complaint 5 days after the alleged crime is not recent within the rule (1473). 

210. Upon rigid questioning by parent.-A complaint given in answer to rigid questioning by a parent demanding answers disclosing the facts is inadmissible because not spontaneous (1474).

211. Telephone conversations.-A person may testify to a business conversation on the telephone with an employee of the defendant, at defendant's place of business, although he could not identify the employee (1475). 

A by-stander may testify to one end of a telephone conversation, regarding renewal of insurance (1476). 

Hearsay statements transmitted by telephone are inadmissible, however (1477). 

212. Testimony at former trials.-Testimony of a witness at a former trial is admissible in a later trial between the same parties where the witness has died. The rule applies to committing trials in criminal cases (1478). 

But the rule has been held inapplicable where the first trial was for a robbery and the second for murder of the victim of the robbery, who had died since the first trial.

The transaction-test was not adopted, although the facts of the transaction had been brought out at the first trial (1479).

The general rule applies to proof of wills, and bastardy proceedings (1480). 

213. Mere absence.-Mere absence of the witness from the state is not enough to let his former testimony in (1481). 

214. Incapacity.-Mental incapacity or physical disability of a witness arising since the first trial is sufficient to authorize the introduction of his testimony on retrial (1482). 

215. Change of parties.-Where the parties at a later trial are not the same, the rule does not apply (1483). 

216. Mode of proof. The stenographer's notes are competent to show the former testimony (1884). A bill of exceptions is not competent for this purpose (1485).

The certificate of testimony at the committing trial must be sealed and filed in the circuit court and must recite it is complete in substance (1486).

Private stenographic notes may be obtained by a subpoena duces tecum (1487).

Testimonial Evidence 

187 

217. Credibility.—It is error to instruct that reported testimony is entitled to the same consideration or credence as if the witness had testified in court (1488)). 

218. Books of regular entries.-Books of regular entries of accounts are admissible provided that they have been fairly and honestly kept, they are the books of the party engaged in the business to which they refer, that the entries were made in the usual course of the business at or about the time the facts transpired, that the entries are original and were made by a party having knowledge of the facts entered, or that information thereof was communicated to the party by whom the entries were made by some person engaged in the business whose duty it was to transact the particular business and make report thereof for entry on the books and such report and entry must be made at the time of the occurrence or before the facts can be supposed to have passed from recollection (1489). 

219. Ownership from fair or express company books. -Ownership cannot be shown by entries of exhibitors in state-fair or express company books, unless the entries were authorized in some way by the person whose name is entered as owner (1490). 

220. Alterations and additions.-Alterations and additions to the account must be satisfactorily explained (1491). 

221. Loose leaf books. It seems that loose leaf books are permissible (1492). 

222. Pencil writing.—It has been held that a pencilwriting entry in a ledger showing the amount paid, the balance due, is incompetent (1493). 

223. Audits.-Audits of extensive accounts are admissible and are required (1494).

Joint audits are admissible and one of the auditors who participated in making the audit may testify as an expert regarding the whole audit (1495).

224. Mortality table.-Standard mortality tables are admissible to show the life expectancy of persons of the class treated in the tables, and not otherwise (1496). 

225. Commercial rating.-Commercial ratings are hearsay and are not competent evidence of the facts recited, but may be competent evidence of knowledge by a person of facts therein alleged, as membership in a firm of partners (1497). 

226. Treatises.-Medical treatises are inadmissible, medicine being an inexact science (1498).

A treatise on duelling may be read to the jury in a case involving a duel. The treatise is admissible as argumentation on the immorality of duelling. The court has discretion to limit counsel so that argumentation may bear some relation to the facts of the case (1499). 

227. A judgment as evidence.-A judgment is only conclusively binding on parties; as to others, the facts on which a judgment is based must be proved (1500). 

In a criminal case in which the state got an instruction that if the defendant cut the victim thinking he was another, L., still the defendant was guilty, the defendant may introduce the record of his trial for cutting L. and show by it justification for cutting L. It would seeem the parties were the same formally, though the victims were different persons (1501).

The effect of a judgment as a fact may be shown, although between third persons, as the fact that a judgment debt existed; prima facie, the fact of insanity of a person adjudged insane; the fact that a suit had been instituted for damages for injuries, as tending to show that an injury of some kind had been received (1502).

E. IMPEACHMENT OF WITNESSES 

228. Animosity.-The animosity of a witness toward a party is admissible, to show his bias, in civil and criminal cases (1503).

The animosity of a witness toward the deceased in a criminal trial is admissible (1504).

Testimonial Evidence 

189 The criminal affinities of a witness may be shown in criminal cases, as, that the witness is a criminal solicitor, or the mistress of the defendant (1505). 

229. Conviction of crime.-Conviction of crime is admissible to impeach a witness' credibility (1506). 

The witness must first be asked if he had been convicted of any crime (1507). Crime includes a misdemeanor, except traffic offenses less than felony (1508). 

The crime may be identified but the details and the person offended may not be shown (1509). The witness' explanation of why he was convicted is incompetent (1510). 

The punishment may not be shown (1511). It is incompetent to show that a witness had been charged with crime (1512). 

230. Unchastity.-Unchastity may not be shown to impeach the credibility of a witness (1513). In rape cases, the good or ill fame of the victim is competent upon the issue of consent; but not to impeach the testimony of the victim (1514). 

231. Admitted statements.-Where statements of a person were written by a witness and were admitted to have been written correctly, it is error to admit evidence in impeachment of the witness who wrote the statements (1515). 

232. Miscellaneous.-Enmity of a witness toward a third person is collateral and inadmissible (1516). That a witness claimed a reward for arresting the defendant is inadmissible (1517). That a bribe has been offered to a witness is not admissible to impeach him (1518). 

That a witness is of nomadic habits does not impeach him (1519). It is improper to ask defendant-witness whether he was the hangman and had hanged 43 men (1520). That a witness had testified for the state in other cases is not competent impeaching evidence (1521). 

233. Intoxication.-That a witness was intoxicated at the time of observing events is admissible as bearing on his power to observe and remember and on his credibility,

but drunkenness sometime, several hours, before the observed events is incompetent (1522). 

234. Defendant's former testimony.-The use by the state of the defendant's former testimony does not render him the state's witness so as to preclude impeachment (1523). 

235. Bias from interest.-Any witness may be examined concerning his interest, his answers may be contradicted, and the jury may consider, and counsel may argue from the interest of the witness, including the defendant-witness (1524). Promised immunity to a state's witness may be shown (1525). The court has discretion to refuse an instruction to the jury to view with caution the testimony of interested witnesses (1525-a). 

236. Monetary, property, and economic interest. 

Witnesses may be shown to have monetary, property or economic interests in the litigation, as, from agreement with a party to split the sum recovered, as beneficiary of insurance, as one who would benefit by establishing a certain boundary, by being paid as a detective to work up cases, as an employee of a party, etc. (1526). 

237. Duress prior to trial.-Duress prior to trial is admissible to affect the credibility of a witness, but it does not disqualify him (1527). 

238. Foundation. It is said that a witness should first be questioned as to his alleged bias before introducing independent evidence of it (1528). 

239. Prior inconsistent statements. Before prior inconsistent statements are admissible to impeach a witness, the witness must be asked questions calling his attention to the time, place, persons present and subject matter (1529). Similarly, before a witness may be impeached by proving prior contradictory statements at a former trial, he must be shown the written report of the testimony. Before documentary evidence may be introduced to contradict a witness, the document must be shown to the witness (1530).

Testimonial Evidence 

191 No foundation need be laid in impeaching a partywitness (1531). 

240. Immaterial error in laying foundation.—In calling a witness' attention to circumstances, error in naming a person as present is immaterial where the other details are sufficiently accurate to call to mind the occasion (1532). 

241. Disgrace of witness. That contradictions degrade the witness is no objection to the use of impeaching evidence (1533). 

242. Disclosure to facilitate impeachment.-A chief witness may be compelled to name persons with him when he observed alleged facts, in order to permit resort to these persons to impeach the chief witness (1534). 

243. Persons who may be impeached.—The party opponent may be called and impeached as an adverse witness (1535).

In criminal cases, when the defendant testifies, his testimony is subject to cross-examination, impeachment and all other incidents as an ordinary witness (1536). The jury may consider the interest of the defendant-witness as bearing on his credibility as witness, and the attorney for the state may argue from it (1537). 

244. A party's own witness.-A party may not impeach his own witness except in case of fraud or surprise.

A party may contradict his own witness on a material issue to develop the case, not in impeachment (1538). 

245. Warning.-Before impeaching one's own witness, for surprise, on a material issue, the witness' attention must be called to his previous statements (1539). 

246. Instruction.-It is reversible error to instruct that a party indorses his witness; it is enough that the party cannot impeach his witness (1540). 

247. The law's witness.-Where a person is required by law to call a witness, he may deny his testimony and impeach him, as when the proponent of a will is required to call the attester (1541).

248. Issues subject of impeaching evidence.-Generally, on material issues evidence of prior inconsistent statements by witnesses may be shown to impeach the credibility of the witnesses, but not testimonially. It is error in argumentation to make testimonial use of impeaching evidence (1542). 

249. Immaterial issues.-On issues not material, impeachment by prior inconsistent statements is not permissible (1543). 

If a witness on the stand is asked, Did you say so and so out of court?, and in answer denies saying so and so, then it is error to prove that the witness did say so and so, for the fact of making the statement is imterial although the content of the statement is material. 

The proper course is to ask the witness a question as to the existence of the fact contained in the purported statement out of court, and then a denial of the fact may be impeached by proof of the prior contradictory statement (1544).

Other collateral issues are the deceased's praying for the defendant, the flight of a witness, a witness' having made whisky, threats in a civil suit, whether defendant in a criminal case was armed when arrested (1545), an offer to compromise, whether the prosecuting witness in assault and battery had flirted with the defendant's wife (1546), physical facts which induced a witness to change his former testimony (1547). 

250. Material issues.-Instances of material issues are as follows: weapon used in attack (1548), defendant's visit to deceased's house after an alleged difficulty which was alleged to have culminated in aggression by deceased at the fatal encounter (1549), defendant's acts immediately after the killing (1550), alibi (1551), commission of rape (1552), and other crimes as charged (1553), motive of witness to falsify (1554), in wills cases the writing of the will by a witness at testator's request (1555), in tort action kicking a passenger off a train (1556), engineer's not seeing child on track (1557).

Testimonial Evidence 

193 

251. Where no testimony is given.-Where a witness testifies that he knows nothing of the case, prior inconsistent statements to impeach him are inadmissible (1558). 

252. Prior statements under duress.-Prior inconsistent statements under duress are inadmissible to impeach a witness (1559). 

253. Completeness of reading.-Where former testimony is read in part and the opponent is given opportunity to read any other part, this is sufficient (1560). 

254. Reputation for want of veracity.-A witness may be impeached by evidence of his bad character for veracity in the community in which he has lived for many years but from which he had removed two years before the trial. Bad reputation of a witness may be shown by a witness who heard it discussed only after the trial began (1561).

Reputation is confined to veracity under this rule (1562). 

255. Rehabilitation of witnesses.-A witness' good character for veracity can not be shown where it has not been impeached (1563). Contradiction of a witness' testimony substantively does not authorize proof of his good character for truthfulness (1564). After a state's witness had testified that no immunity had been offered him it is error to permit officers to bolster his testimony by testifying that no immunity had been promised (1565). 

Prior consistent statements are inadmissible to support a witness who had been impeached generally (1566). 

F. PERSONS WHO MAY TESTIFY 

256. The following persons may testify.—Indians (1567), trial judges (1568), jurors (1569), grand jurors (1570), executors to establish a will (1571), interested persons (1572), parties accomplices (1573), convicts except perjurers and suborners of perjury (1574), co-defend-

ants (1575), attester-devisees (1576), the devise being voided.

Officers may not impeach their own acts (1577). Executors may not contradict their own accounts (1578). 

Jurors may not impeach their own verdict by revealing their own misconduct but may testify to outside influence (1579). The court clerk is not permitted to impeach the court records (785).

Officials may testify in favor of their own acts (1580). i. Preferred Witnesses 

257. Attester. Attesters of wills are preferred witnesses and may give their opinion of the testator's capacity to make a will (1581). 

Attesters to deeds are preferred witnesses (1582). 

258. In bigamy and criminal conversation.—In bigamy and criminal conversation marriage may not be proved by holding out or repute (1583). But see Circ. Ev. 22. 

259. Eye-witness.-In criminal cases the state is under a duty to call at least one eye-witness unless he be hostile or unless the defendant calls an eye-witness (1584).

G. PRIVILEGES 

i. Privilege of the Criminal 

260. Rule. Any circumstantial evidence of crime which an officer of the state or Federal government obtains while trespassing on the defendant's property is inadmissible no matter whether the trespass be slight and the crime discovered enormous (1585).

Knowledge of crime secured by an officer while committing a trespass against the defendant is inadmissible (1586). 

Nontrespassing by-stander. Where a murder had been committed by hitting an aged woman on the head with an axe and an officer made a search of premises under a defective search warrant which was invalid be-

Testimonial Evidence 

195 cause it used the word "suspected" rather than "believed," and the officer found the axe in the well, it was reversible error to permit a neighbor to testify that she stood on her own lot at the time the axe was found and saw the officers get it out of the well. 

A by-stander off the premises cannot testify to a discovery made by an officer conducting an illegal search (1586-a). 

Provided no trespass is committed, the officer may eavesdrop, and if he discover anything thereby he may be permitted to testify as to it (1587). 

Voluntary admissions made during officers' illegal search are admissible (1588).

Circumstantial evidence or knowledge of crime gotten by a private citizen by or while trespassing is admissible (1589).

The validity of the warrant and the legality of the search are in issue and are to be tried by the court before admitting evidence thus obtained on objection by the defendant (1590).

On objection the affidavit and search warrant must be produced or the loss accounted for (1591).

Property that is occupied by the defendant alone or with others is not subject to search without a warrant, generally, and evidence thus obtained is inadmissible; but if the place is not owned or occupied by the defendant, evidence obtained by an officer searching without a warrant is admissible, as where the defendant concealed a still in woods not owned by him or in his possession (1592). 

261. Reasonable searches.-Reasonable searches and seizures by officers without warrants are permissible and evidence thus gotten is admissible. Reasonable cause to believe liquor is being transported in violation of law authorizes such search and seizure (1593). 

Such circumstances as would lead a reasonable man to believe a crime is being committed constitute probable cause for and authorize a search without a warrant (1594). 

The reported decisions on what is and what is not probable cause are irreconcilable, and no attempt will

be made here to reconcile them by resort to the court records, which may not be fully summarized in the written opinions of the court (1595).

A statute authorizing search of any house or place in which an officer has reason to suspect gambling is going on is unconstitutional (1596). 

262. Property and interests protected. To invoke the rule, the defendant must have owned or have had a right to possess the property searched. Leased property is within the rule (1597). Squatters may not object to the search of the place squatted on (1598).

Employees are not entitled to object to illegal search of the employer's property; nor may adult children, of their father's property; but a husband may object to evidence gotten by unlawful search of the home owned by the wife and occupied by both; guests may not complain of the unlawful search of the property of their host (1599). 

263. Lawful search.-Probable cause to believe a felony has been committed authorized an arrest and a search of the person arrested and of the place in which he is arrested (1600). 

264. Issue. The existence of probable cause is in issue when evidence obtained by arrest on probable cause is objected to and a witness must disclose on cross-examination the identity of his informer, but the state may not first offer the information acted on by the officer (1601). 

265. Comment on the rule. The present rule reversed a former ruling in this state which followed the common-law rule, but in reversing the former ruling it was not noticed, and possibly was overlooked, and the Federal rule was followed by an evenly divided court. 

The present rule adopts the reasoning of the Federal Court. The reasoning is inconclusive, arbitrary, and quixotic. Cf. Miss. L. J. VIII, No. 3, p. 248.

Testimonial Evidence 

197 

ii. Privilege Against Self-Incrimination 

266. Rule. In all crirninal prosecutions the accused shall not be compelled to give evidence against himself. 

His failure to testify shall not operate to his prejudice or be commented on (1602). 

267. Privilege of witness.-A witness may decline to answer questions if the answer will expose him to criminal prosecution or penalty (1603). 

268. Summons as compulsion.—When a defendant is called by the state and is examined before the grand jury or in the trial as a witness in regard to any transaction in which he is later tried his testimony against himself may not be used in his own trial. The oath is compulsion. But where he voluntarily appears, his testimony may be used. In such case the oath is not compulsion (1604). 

269. Presumption.-It is presumed that the defendant's testimony was voluntary and he must show the contrary (1605). 

270. Subpoena duces tecum.-The securing of the defendant's books by subpoena duces tecum is compulsion and documentary evidence thus obtained is inadmissible under Section 26 of the Constitution, in a criminal case (1606). The writ may not be used against defendant's secretary to secure the defendant's papers (1606-a). 

What Is Incriminatory 

271. Variance from previous testimony.-A witness may not decline to answer because a true answer would be at variance with previous testimony and would subject him to punishment for perjury (1607).

A witness who seeks reinstatement as an attorney after disbarment for blackmail need not answer a question that would incriminate him by showing what became of the black-mail money, having previously given an account which the court had found to be false (1608).

272. Co-defendant.-A co-defendant tried jointly cannot be compelled to testify for his co-defendant; but if a severance is granted, he may be compelled so to testify (1609). 

273. Illegally obtained evidence.-Testimony of what an officer saw while making an illegal search is incompetent because "tantamount" to compelling the defendant to give testimony against himself (1610). 

274. Subpoena duces tecum.-Defendant's books obtained by subpoena duces tecum are inadmissible in criminal cases because the constitution forbids the use of evidence extorted from the defendant by force and evidence contained in his books and papers alike (1611). 

275. Exhibition of foot, etc.-The defendant may be compelled to exhibit his foot, or to put on his cap and coat, to facilitate identification (1612). 

276. Distinguish acts accompanying confession.- 

Acts of defendant accompanying a forced confession are admissible, as by getting and returning stolen money (1613). 

277. Actions.-The privilege applies in contempt ac tions, hearings for continuances in criminal actions, but not to civil actions where no criminal liability or penalty is involved in the matter sought to be elicited from the witness (1614). 

278. Incidents of exercise of the privilege.—The failure of the accused to testify in a criminal case shall not operate to his prejudice and the court should instruct the jury that they have no right to draw an unfavorable inference against the defendant from the fact that he did not testify in the case (1615). 

279. Comment or reference to defendant's not testifying. In argument any reference to the non-contradiction of the evidence for the state which expressly refers to defendant's not testifying or makes such references as necessarily to call attention to the fact that he did not

Testimonial Evidence 

199 testify is error. It is proper to say that a given fact is undisputed where witnesses other than the defendant could have denied it (1616). 

280. Form. It is permissible to say that the evidence of the state is undisputed (1617). 

281. Emphasis.-The use of the passive voice, viz., the evidence for the state is undisputed, is good form; the use of the active voice, viz., no one, or not a living soul has denied the facts, is objectionable where no one but the defendant could dispute or deny the facts (1618). 

The distinction seems to be one of emphasis rather than of logical content of the two forms. 

282. Harmless error.-Where guilt is overwhelmingly shown, comment on the defendant's not testifying is harmless (1619). 

283. Procedure in invoking the privilege.-The privilege may not be exercised until a specific question is put (1620).

The witness should be permitted to decide whether the question calls for an incriminating answer (1621).

The witness' attorney cannot exercise the privilege for him; the witness must assert it. The court should advise the witness of his privilege (1622). 

284. Abolition of privilege where immunity from prosecution is conferred.-The privilege against self-incrimination has been abolished at the price of immunity from prosecution, where the witness has been called, in the following cases: 

Legislative investigations 

Trials for duelling 

Trials for liquor offenses

Trials for violation of anti-trust laws Trials for violation of anti-gambling laws 

Trials for violation of game and fish law (1623) (1624) (1625) (1626) (1627) (1628)

284-a. Privilege of Juvenile Delinquents.—“Any evi dence given in such case (in proceedings against a delinquent child under juvenile court act) shall not in any civil, criminal or other case or proceeding whatever in any court, be lawful or proper evidence against such child for any purpose whatever, except in subsequent cases against the same child under this act." Section 9, Ch. 300, Laws of 1940. 

iii. Privilege in Highway-Accident Reports 

285. Rule. By statute a privilege is given in highway-accident reports which requires that the reports be held in confidence except that the identity or presence of a person at an accident may be shown, and whether or not a report has been filed may be shown (1629). 

iv. Trade Secrets 

286. Rule. A witness has a qualified privilege not to disclose trade secrets when disclosure would depreciate their value. When the attainment of justice requires, however, the secret must be revealed, as, in a case in which the witness had contracted to sell a formula, he may be compelled to disclose it (1630). 

v. Privileged Communications Between Husband and Wife 

287. Introduced by one another.-Husband and wife may be introduced by each other in all cases (1631). 

288. Controversies inter se.-Husband and wife are competent witnesses in their own behalf as against each other in all controversies between them (1632). 

A wife may be compelled against her will to testify to show assault and battery on her by her husband (1633). 289. 

Irregular ruling.-A husband is incompetent to testify to his wife's admissions in a case in which she was charged with burning a house at night in which her husband usually slept at night (1634).

A wife is incompetent to testify that her husband raped her before marriage and then compelled her to go through a marriage ceremony by threats to kill her (1635).

Testimonial Evidence 

201 290. Nonsupport of wife.-A wife may testify to nonsupport of her by her husband (1636). 

291. Family.-A wife is incompetent to testify to nonsupport of her child by her husband (1637). 

292. Nonaccess.-A husband may testify to nonaccess to his wife in a case of a bastardy involving a child begotten during wedlock and born after divorce (1638). 

293. Bigamy.-In bigamy the first wife cannot testify to the first marriage, for this is not a controversy between herself and her husband (1639). 

294. Crime against family.-A wife cannot testify to show that her husband raped her daughter. The sanctity of the home forbids (1640). 

295. Crime against paramour.-A husband cannot testify that his wife shot her paramour (1641).

Comment: Such homes as those in which monstrous crimes occur have lost their sanctity, and forfeit any right to privacy. Homes that have any sanctity do not need protection of the rule. Hence there is nothing to prevent a wider and more reasonable definition of a controversy between husband and wife. 

296. Divorce.-In divorce, spouses may testify for or against one another (1642). 

297. Against one another.-But in all other instances wherein either of them is a party litigant the other shall not be competent as a witness (1643). 

298. Acts and words when alone.-Acts and words performed and uttered when the spouses are alone are privileged, for reasons of public policy (1644). 

299. After interest in subject matter ceases.-After the husband's interest in the subject matter has ceased, the wife may testify, as where the husband had deeded property, the wife may testify as to the consideration although the husband was a party to the suit (1645).

300. After death.-After the death of the husband the wife may not testify to his threats to kill another, even though he be his own son who is on trial for his life and shows self-defense (1646). The sanctity of their home forbids the revelation of the truth. 

300-a.-Under Sec. 1528, Code, 1930, the wife of a decedent is competent to testify to what occurred at the scene of the killing and as to who was present, and the statements made by others, or statements made in the presence of others bearing on the material issue in controversy, viz., the striking of the fatal blow by defendant, and the credibility of a witness thereto, who said it was too dark to see. The deceased husband is not a party to the trial for his murder (1646-a). 

301. After divorce.-After divorce, confidential communications between married persons may not be disclosed (1647). 

302. Waiver. The privilege may be waived (1648). 

303. Joint actions.-The wife cannot make admissions which would affect the joint interest of husband and wife. This ruling was made before the removal of the wife's legal disabilities (1649). 

Where husband and wife are joint defendants, the opponent cannot require one to give evidence against the interest of the other. This rule has been applied where husband and wife were intermediate grantees in fraudulent conveyances (1650). 

304. Incidents of the privilege. It is error for the state to make profert of the wife of the defendant as a witness, to ask the defendant on the stand whether he would waive the privilege, to instruct or comment on the fact that the state cannot call defendant's wife as a witness, or to comment on the fact that she did not testify (1651). 

vi. Privileged Communications Between Attorney and Client 

305. Rule. At every peril to themselves, attorneys are under a duty to preserve the confidence and secrets

Testimonial Evidence 

203 of clients. All matters of fact and all professional intercourse, papers, and communications are within the privilege. Hence, attorneys may not testify as to such matters, without the client's consent (1652). 

306. Scrivener. The privilege does not apply where the attorney is employed as a scrivener. Earlier cases are contra (1653). 

307. Presence of third parties. Where third parties adversely interested are present when statements are made to attorneys the statements are not privileged (1654). 

308. Waiver of accomplice.—An accomplice, by turning state's evidence, waives his privilege to withhold incriminating statements made to his counsel. Prior inconsistent statements to counsel may be elicited from the state's witness, and (obiter) from his attorney (1655). 

vii. Physician-Patient Privilege 

309. Rule. Communications to a physician or surgeon by a patient under his charge are privileged, and shall not be communicated in any legal proceedings except at the instance of the patient (1656). 

310. Observed facts.-Facts learned by observation of the physician as well as oral communications are within th privilege (1657). 

311. Partner. The attending physician's partner who saw the patient in a hospital but who did not personally attend the patient is incompetent (1658). 

312. Consultant.-Consulting physicians are incompetent (1659). 

313. Company physician.—A company physician who attends an employee is incompetent, even though the patient sues the company for neglect of the physician in treating him (1660). 

314. Nontreatment.-A physician may testify that he did not treat the defendant for wounds on the head, for this discloses nothing (1661).

314-a. No relation.-Where defendant, a seducer, asked a physician to give the victim something to produce abortion, and the physician declined, the relation of physician-patient did not exist between the physician and either defendant or the victim; and the physician may testify to the fact of defendant's request, in an action for statutory rape (1661-a). 

315. Dentist.-A dentist is not within the privilege.

Chiropractors are not within the privilege (1662). 

316. Nurse.-A nurse is not within the privilege, except when she is present with and acts as an assistant under the physician in charge (1663). A student nurse in a hospital may testify to cases observed by her (1664). 

317. Nonprofessional observation.-Facts learned by a physician from nonprofessional observation are admissible. If he can separate scientific and privileged information, the physician may testify as an expert (1665). 

318. Asylum records, vital statistics, court physicians. The privilege does not apply to asylum records, vital statistics, or court physicians appointed on issue of the defendant's insanity (1666). 

319. Waiver; by heirs.-Heirs and personal representatives may not waive the privilege. A parent or grandparent in charge may waive the privilege for a child under 12 who does not object (1667). 

320. Mode.-An informal statement that he would waive the rule, made by a party on the witness stand who was ignorant of his privilege, does not preclude the assertion of the privilege when the physician is offered (1668). 

321. By contract.-A waiver is valid where made in a contract before or at the trial (1669). An agreement to furnish a medical report by the attending physician is not a waiver (1670). 

322. Disclosure of patient no waiver.-Disclosure of communications by a patient while on the witness-stand is not a waiver (1671).

Testimonial Evidence 

205 323. By voluntary act.-Voluntary submission to a physician employed by the other side for examination and report does not create the relation of physician and patient within the statute. Similarly, when a prisoner submits voluntarily to a court physician, the privilege does not apply (1672). 

324. Introduction of one of several physicians.-The introduction of one of several attending physicians does not waive the privilege as to the rest (1673). 

325. In absence of jury. The privilege applies in the absence of the jury and disclosures may not be made to the court (1674). 

326. Patients's murderer or assailant.-The murderer or assailant of the patient may not invoke the rule (1675). 

327. The privilege a rule of evidence.-The privilege is a rule of evidence and applies to patients treated outside the state, even though the case is tried under the 

Federal Employers Liability Act (1676). 

Incidents of Exercise 

328. Instruction.-The court may instruct that if the plaintiff does not call his physician who examined him and is available this may create a presumption that his testimony would be unfavorable (1677). An earlier case, contra (1678), has been overruled (See n. 1677).

Reports to Insurance Commissioner 

328-a. Statement to insurance commissioner.-Under Section 5208, Code of 1930, the statement of facts by an insurance company to the commissioner of insurance relative to cause of termination of employment of an agent shall be "absolutely privileged." 

viii. Either-Neither Rule 

329. Rule. A person shall not testify as a witness to establish his own claim or defense against the estate of a deceased person which originated in the life-

time of such deceased person or any claim he has transferred since the death of such decedent. Claims or defenses arising since the death are not within the rule (1679). 

The same rule applies in estates of persons non compos mentis (1680).

Persons Subject to the Rule 330. Corporations.-Corporations as claimants or de fendants are not within the rule, although owned and conducted by a single proprietor (1681). 

331. Administrator.-An administrator is competent to prove a claim of his estate against another estate which arose in the life-time of the other decedent. Although the administrator of the first estate is also a distributee of the first estate (1682). 

332. Partnership.-In general it seems that a partnership is treated as a separate entity and is not within the rule, but the estate of a deceased partner is within the protection of the rule (1683).

A person who dealt with a partner who subsequently died may testify against the partnership to establish his defense (1684).

A surviving partner may testify to establish a claim in favor of the partnership against the estate of a deceased member of the firm which the administrator of the deceased partner had paid, the action being the final accounting of the said administrator (1685).

In an action against an executor of a deceased partner, the plaintiff is incompetent to testify to prove he contracted with the decedent as a partner (1686). 333. 

Remaindermen.-Remaindermen are competent witnesses to prove claims to inherit based on oral agreement between life tenant and deceased spouse (1687). 

334. Heirs in favor of the estate.-Heirs are competent to testify in resisting a claim against the decedent's estate (1688).

Testimonial Evidence 

207 

335. Heirs, against the estate.-An alleged widow may not testify to prove her marriage to the dededent where she had conveyed as heir of the deceased, and where property rights of her grantees and of heirs of the decedent were involved (1689). 

336. Fact of death.-Heirs may testify to the fact of the death of the ancestor (1690). 

337. Spouse.-The husband or wife of the claimant may testify to establish a claim of his or her spouse against the estate of a decedent which arose during the life of the deceased person (1691).

In reviewing rulings on competency of a witness, the wife of a mortgagor may testify that he did not sign the mortgage but may not testify the res was a homestead, where the mortgagee had died, for the wife has an interest in the homestead, but not in nonhomestead property (1691-a). 

338. Next friend. The next friend of a minor may testify to transactions with the decedent in order to establish the minor's claim against the estate (1692). 

339. Constructive trustee.-The constructive trustee of an equitable interest in land, as against the estate of the constructive trustee of the legal title is incompetent to testify to transactions with the trustee of the legal title (1693). 

Claimants of a constructive trust are incompetent to testify that the deceased constructive trustee recognized the trust in her lifetime (1700).

A purchaser of land is competent to testify that a decedent did not advance the price and to deny an alleged oral agreement of sale by himself to the decedent, in an action between an heir of the decedent's vendee and the purchaser (1694). 

340. After interest ended, before death of other party. 

Grantees who transferred land prior to the grantor's death may testify to the transaction with the grantor (1695).

A transferor of a negotiable instrument may testify that the transferee, now deceased, assumed the transferor's debt to another person, in an action by such person against the estate of the assuming grantee (1696). 

The grantor in a deed of trust is competent to testify to payment of the secured debt after the death of the beneficiary in an action between a grantee of the said grantor in the deed of trust and the trustee under the deed of trust. It is not clear whether the conveyance was before or after the beneficiary's death, but presumably it was before (1697). 

341. Assignee, and 3rd parties.—An assignee of life insurance may testify to the transaction as against 3rd parties, the insured's estate not being a party (1698). 

342. Satisfied creditors.-Creditors whose unprobated claims had been paid are competent to testify for the executor that the claims were due, owing, and unpaid (1699).

Transactions 

343. Gifts; claim of.—A party contesting an estate's right to property is incompetent to testify that the decedent gave the property to him (1701). 

344. Denial of.-A distributee of an estate is competent to testify that the decedent did not give away bonds owned by him, in contest with the claimant of a gift (1702). 

345. Property of the estate; rule.-In contest between heirs and a widow, the heirs are incompetent to testify to show admissions of permissive user by the ancestor.

The rule extends to every assertion of right to any part of the estate left by the deceased as a result of a transaction in the life of the decedent (1703).

A person claiming land by parol gift from a decedent followed by ten years adverse possession is incompetent to testify to the alleged fact of a parol gift, although the heirs to the land had conveyed the land to another. 

All property of every kind left by a decedent is protected

Testimonial Evidence 

209 against testimony of persons claiming by virtue of alleged transactions between the claimant and the decedent, un der Section 1529 (1703-a). 

346. Equities.-A claimant who seeks to take by replevin or other remedy property held by a beneficiary of a deed of trust given by a person now deceased is within the either-neither rule and cannot testify to contracts and negotiations with the decedent, for the estate owns an equity in the property, although not a party to the suit (1705). 

347. Undisputed transaction.-Where it was undisputed that a decedent had sold property, a contest between competing purchasers from the decedent is not within the rule and both purchasers may testify (1704). 

348-9. Contractual and related transactions; rule. 

A party to a contract with or an alleged gift by a decedent is incompetent to testify as to the contract or gift in litigation with the personal representative (1706). 

An executor is incompetent to testify to an agreement between the testator and a debtor which excused the executor from collecting the note evidencing the debt (1707). 

A person dealing with a member of a partnership is incompetent to prove his contract against the estate of the deceased firm-member with whom he had dealt (1708).

A widow is incompetent to establish a claim against her husband's estate arising in his life-time (1709). 

350. Legatee in favor of will.-A legatee is incompetent to testify in favor of a will, although he has assigned his claim (1714). The proponent, a legatee, may testify in support of a will on issue of devisavit vel non (1710) (Overruled). 

A legatee is incompetent to prove the testator was incapacitated when attempting to cut down the legatee's share by a codicil (1712).

351. Heir, on intestacy.-The heir cannot testify to prove the incapacity of the testator and thereby establish the right to take by intestacy (1711). 

352. Legatee, against will.-But the legatee may testify that the testator was of unsound mind for this is against his interest (1713). 

353. Writings.-Witnesses within the either-neither rule may authenticate books of account or letters which show sums due by the decedent (1715). Similarly, the grantee of a deed may testify to delivery of his deed by the decedent, grantor, as against another grantee; but later decisions are contra (1716). In contests between heirs and parties who contracted with the decedent the heirs are incompetent to show the deceased's contractual disability, or forgery, for this is in their interest (1717).

An insured person in an action against the company is competent to testify that a transfer to S. of insurance is a forgery although S. was dead. One seeking to recover stock pledged to a decedent cannot show the indorsement was a forgery (1718). 

354. Lost instruments.—A mortgagee is incompetent to testify to establish a lost mortgage given by a nowdeceased mortgagor, although the estate be not a party (1719). The interested party may testify to the loss of the original as the foundation for introducing secondary evidence, of a lost bond given by a decedent (1720). 

355. Reformation.-A grantor is an incompetent witness to testify to identify land in an action of reformation brought by the heirs of the grantee (1721). 

356. Fraud.-The defendant, maker of a note, is incompetent to testify to show a fraud as against a now deceased assignee of the note. It seems the alleged fraud concerned only the assignee and not the transaction in which the note was made (1722). 

357. Tort.-Defendants in tort are incompetent to testify to the transaction in which the plaintiff was hurt, where pending the suit the plaintiff died (1723).

Testimonial Evidence 

211 

358. Defenses.-The survivor is incompetent to testify to defenses to contracts he had with the deceased (1724). 

359. Marriage.-A woman is incompetent to testify she married the decedent and became his heir at his death, in an action between heirs and a grantee of the said woman (1725). 

360. Assignment after death.-An assignment after the death of the party who dealt with the assignor does not remove the disability, under the rule, of the assignor 

361. Statements in favor of decedent.-Admissions by an incompetent witness, in favor of the decedent, are admissible (1727).

An heir of the mortgagor is competent to testify to payment of the mortgage as against the mortgagee although he benefits himself, for his testimony is in the interest of the estate (1728). 

362. Transactions with the estate.-Claimants against estates are competent to testify regarding facts arising after the death of the decedent (1729).

Executors and administrators are competent to testify to facts arising in administration, and in their own favor in accounting with the estate (1730). 

363. Deposition of a decedent; opponent may testify. -Where a party to a suit made a deposition and died and the deposition was introduced, then the other party is competent (1731). 

Where the plaintiff's deposition was taken after due notice and attendance of the defendant, and the defendant then died, the plaintiff's deposition is inadmissible against the executor of the defendant (1732). 

364. Waiver.-The rule may be waived, as, when the estate puts the disqualified witness on the stand (1733). 

365. Answer and oath.-An answer may be sworn to by a person within the either-neither rule (1734).

H. PROCEDURE RELATING TO EVIDENCE i. Examination of Witnesses 

366. Examinations by the court.-The court may examine witnesses to clarify testimony. The court must remain neutral in so doing (1735). 

367. Limitation on examination.-Needless repetition of unimportant matters on cross-examination may be limited by the trial court in its discretion (1736). 

368. Limitation on number of witnesses.-On the question of the change of venue, the court has discretion to limit the witnesses to a reasonable number, viz., 100, where 550 were asked for (1737). 

369. Punishment of witness in presence of jury.- 

In a close case it is reversible error in the presence of the jury to commit a witness to jail for perjury (1738). 

370. Consultation of counsel with witness.-In general the defendant in a criminal case may consult with witnesses in private. The court has discretion to appoint the district attorney to be present with tender children in such consultation where the children were afraid of the defendant who had but recently murdered their father when the children were present (1739). 

371. After sequestration.-Counsel may consult with witnesses after the rule of sequestration has been invoked (1740). 

372. Convict.-Defendant's counsel has the privilege of consulting with a witness, who is a convict, in private and out of hearing of an officer but the conference must be in the jail or other place of confinement (1741). 

373. Only one attorney for each side may examine witnesses. By rule 26 of the chancery court, only one solicitor for each party may examine a witness except by permission of the chancellor. Parties whose interests are not distinct are treated as one party, otherwise not. But see Section 25, Constitution of 1890.

Testimonial Evidence 

213 

374. Argumentative cross-examination.-Argumentative cross-examination and brow beating of a witness are reversible error (1742). 

375. Leading questions, rule.-Questions suggesting the answer desired are leading (1743). Questions which merely direct attention to the subject matter are not leading (1744). 

376. No-rule.-There is no fixed rule which will serve in all cases (1745). 

377. Assumption of fact.-A question is leading which assumes a fact to be proved which is not proved (1746). 

378. Argumentative question.-A question is leading which embodies a material fact and admits of a yes or no answer this is objectionable because an argumentative and pregnant mode of interrogation (1747). 

379. Conversations. In asking questions as to conversations or admissions, it is most consistent with fairness to ask what was done and said, rather than whether a particular and material thing was done and said (1748). 

380. Ex parte matters.-In ex parte matters in chancery, leading questions are not permitted (1749). 

381. Discretion of the court.-The asking of leading questions is in the sound discretion of the trial court (1750). It is said that leading questions are permissible when a witness is a child of tender years, or is hostile, or has exhausted his memory without stating the particular required where it is a proper name or a fact which general inquiry does not elicit (1751). On crossexamination, leading questions are permitted (1752). 

382. Questions referring to maps.-Questions referring to maps must be so framed as to be intelligible in connection with the map on appeal (1753).

382-a. Verbal answer required. "Neither the Court nor counsel should accept a nod or a shake of the head in lieu of an answer to a question" (1753-a). 

383. Error in asking improper questions.-Ordinarily, error in asking an improper question is cured by not permitting the question to be answered but whether such error is incurable rests in the sound discretion of the. court (1754). 

384. Remedy for abuse of witness by attorney.-An objection and a reprimand by the court is enough to correct error of counsel in calling a witness a liar. A motion for a new trial is in order if the error is deemed reversible error (1755). 

385. Remedy for answer by witness before ruling on objection made.-A direction by the court to disregard an answer is proper where the witness speaks after objection but before ruling (1756). 

386. Remedy for volunteered statements. It is proper to exclude from the jury unresponsive answers or side-remarks volunteered by a witness. A witness cannot put error into a record by statements not called for by questions or sanctioned by the judge (1757). 

ii. Scope of Cross-Examination 

387. Persons.-Any witness examined at any stage of the proceeding is subject to cross-examination (1758). 

388. Scope. The scope of cross-examination is coextensive with the issues of the case, and is not confined to the topics of direct examination (1759). 

389. Credibility. The scope of permissible cross-examination includes also the memory, accuracy, sincerity, interest, or bias of the witness. This applies to experts (1760). 

390. Collateral issues.-Collateral issues are not subject to cross-examination (1761).

Testimonial Evidence 

215 

391. Leading questions.-Leading questions are permitted on cross-examination (1762). 

392. No offer required. The purpose sought in cross-examination need not be stated in advance (1763). 

393. Verbal completeness.-Where part of a conversation is shown on examination, the remaining part may be brought out on cross-examination and failure to exercise the privilege does not render the direct examination erroneous (1764). 

394. Case in chief on cross-examination.-The defendant may develop his own case on cross-examination (1765). 

395. Defendant as witness. The defendant who takes the stand is subject to cross-examination as any other witness upon all issues, and the privilege against self-incrimination is waived (1766). 

396. Own witness.-Except where taken by surprise or the witness is hostile, a party cannot cross-examine his own witness (1767). 

397. Attester of will versus contestant.-A contestant denying execution of a will is not entitled to call the attester as an adverse witness (1768). 

398. Sequestration.-Violation of the rule excluding witnesses from the court-room while other witnesses are examined goes only to the credibility of the witness and not to his competency. It is error to refuse to permit such witness to testify. The witness is subject to punishment. The court has discretion to exclude from testifying a witness who has violated the rule where the testimony is merely cumulative (1769). 

399. Parties as witnesses. In a civil case a party may be required to testify before other witnesses testify or else to retire (1770). The plaintiff may require the defendant to testify before any of the plaintiff's witnesses testify, but the notice and request must be ex-

press. By rule in chancery it seems the invoking of the rule puts the parties on notice that they must either testify before other witnesses, co-complainant or co-defendant, or retire. At law a co-defendant may remain while another co-defendant testifies (1771).

But the rule that a defendant must testify before others does not apply in a felony case, or to alienists (1772). 

iii. Procedure to Secure Attendance of Witnesses 

400. Subpoena.-Secs. 3001, 3002, 3003, Code, 1930. 

401. Witnesses in another county.-Ch. 250, Laws, 1936; Sec. 3004, Code, 1930. 

402. Uniform act to secure attendance of witnesses from other states.-Ch. 261, Laws, 1938. 

403. Who may issue subpoena.-Secs. 2083, 3462, 5338, 447, Code, 1930; Sec. 11 (j), Ch. 147, p. 300, Laws, 1938; 

Sec. 18, Ch. 113, p. 101, Laws, 1938; Sec. 18, Ch. 114, p. 

111, Laws, 1938; Ch. 295, Sec. 9 (j-k), Laws, 1940. 

404. How served.-Secs. 3005, 2978, Code, 1930. 

405. Attachment for nonattendance. Sec. 3006, Code, 1930. 

406. Attendance from day to day required.-Sec. 3007, Code, 1930. 

407. Punishment for nonattendance.-Secs. 3007, 1981, 2083, Code, 1930. 

408. Refusal to take oath or testify.-Secs. 741, 1535, Code, 1930. 

409. Fees. Ch. 249, Laws, 1936, amending Sec. 1805,

Code, 1930, Secs. 1806, 1807, ib., and decisions requiring certificate of attendance to be secured in term time, and issuance of subpoena, in order for fee to be paid. 

In criminal cases, Sec. 1809, Code, 1930. 

Before railway commission, Sec. 7036, Code, 1930.

Testimonial Evidence 

217 

Before unemployment commission, Sec. 6 (g), Ch. 295, Laws, 1940. 

Attorneys in case receive no fees as witnesses. Sec. 

3706, Code, 1930.

I. PERPETUATION OF TESTIMONY AND DEPOSITIONS. 

410. Perpetuation of testimony.-Statutory proce dure, Secs. 1553-63, Code, 1930. 

411. Perpetuation of testimony in pending case of witness who has become incapacitated. Ch. 253, p. 493, Laws, 1936. 

412. Depositions. Statutory procedure, Secs. 1538, 1551, 1550, 1547, 1604, 1595, 4603, 1552, 3003, 1539, 1540, 1541, 1542, 1543, 1545, 1546, 1548. 

413. Criminal cases.-Depositions are inadmissible in criminal trials (1773). 

414. Amendment of pleadings. Amendment of pleadings does not render inadmissible a deposition previously taken unless the other side is taken by surprise (1774). 

415. Bias of officer.-An uncle of one of the parties is disqualified to act as a commissioner and a deposition taken by him should be suppressed (1775). 

416. Scribe. The answers may be in the handwriting of one of the attorneys if certified by the commissioner and no fraud is shown (1776). 

417. By whom to be used.-Either party may introduce a deposition when it has been taken (1777). 

418. In other cases.-Where the parties in a case are different from those in another case, a deposition taken in the other case is inadmissible (1778). 

419. Record on appeal.-On appeal depositions must be copied into the transcript (1779).

420. Form of questions in depositions.-Precise rules are impractical. The mind of the witness must be directed to the subject matter. The witness should not be prompted what answer to give. Each case and its circumstances must be looked to in determining the degree of permissible particularity in directing the mind of the witness to the subject matter (1780). 

It appears from precedents that greater latitude is permitted in depositions in particularizing the subject matter to which attention is called than in oral examination (1781).

V. REAL EVIDENCE AND VIEW A. REAL EVIDENCE 

1. Defendant.-A defendant may defendant may be viewed in court to determine his race (1782). 

2. Bastard. A bastard may be viewed in court to show resemblance to the defendant, or the want of resemblance (1783). 

3. Parts of body.-Parts of the body of a slain person may be brought into court to show the corpus delicti (1784). 

4. Finger prints. Properly identified finger prints are admissible (1785). 

5. Weapon.-A knife or other weapon found at the scene of the crime is admissible (1786). 

6. Bloody garments. Bloody garments found in the defendant's possession are admissible without scientific proof that the stains are blood (1787). 

7. Boots and shoes.-Boots and shoes taken from the defendant when arrested are admissible (1788). 

8. Traces. Bloody paper found near the scene of crime is admissible (1789).

Real Evidence and View 

219 9. Obscene cards.-Obscene cards which occasioned a fight are admissible (1790). 

10. Apparatus.-A still, kegs, ginger, containers which were found on the defendant's premises are admissible (1791). 

11. Loot. Stolen goods found on defendant's premises are admissible (1792). 

12. Autopsy. It is said that the defendant should be given opportunity to be represented when an autopsy by court order is made (1793).

B. EXPERIMENTAL EVIDENCE 13. Result of experiment.-Testimonial evidence is admissible to show result of experiment on the question whether cider is intoxicating, visibility on a railway track, pattern of gun-fire (1794). 

14. Exhibitions.-A horse and rider may be exhibited to the jury in order that the jury may determine the possibility of certain wounds being inflicted on the victim while on horse-back (1795). 

15. Experiments.-Experiment before the jury is not permissible where the conditions of the case cannot be duplicated. The court expressly omits deciding whether experiments are admissible where conditions can be duplicated (1796). 

16. Tableaux. In a murder case a photographic representation of tableaux vivants arranged by the chief witness for the state to dramatize his version of the case and the position of the parties is not admissible because too vivid (1797). 

17. Use of son of victim.-A demonstration of how wounds were inflicted is prejudicial where the victim's son was used as a model (1798).

C. PRIVILEGE OF PRIVACY 

18. Rule. A plaintiff's body is not subject to examination in an action for tort (1799). 

19. Waiver. Where a plaintiff exhibits the hips and x-ray pictures to the jury in order that an injury to the pubic bone may be shown, this waives the privilege and physicians may be appointed to examine the body and take x-ray pictures, or the defendant's physicians may make a reasonable examination in the presence of the plaintiff's physicians (1800).

Where a plaintiff pointed to his eye to illustrate his testimony of injury to it, this waives the privilege (1801).

D. VIEW 

1930. 20.

Statutory provisions.-Sections 2066, 1490, Code, 

21. Discretion. The court has discretion to grant view but where no reason is shown for view it is reversible error to grant it, where appellant was thereby prejudiced on appeal (1802). Where unchallenged photographs, diagrams and measurements would disclose the facts, view is not permissible (1803). 

22. Change.-View is not permissible where material changes in the place have occurred (1804). 23. 

Testimony at view. Only testimony relating to pointing out the place and information necessary to understand the location is admissible at the scene; to admit other testimony is reversible error (1805). 

24. Procedure of requesting view.-View should not be requested in the presence of the jury, but the movant should ask that the jury should retire. Objection to view should not be made in the presence of the jury, even though the jury is not sent out when the motion is made; but the objector should ask that the jury be retired (1806). 

It is the duty of the court on its own motion to overrule the motion for view if made in the presence of

Real Evidence and View 

221 the jury. The opponent may move that the jury be retired and then object. If his motion is overruled and a view is granted, it is reversible error thus to force him to object in the presence of the jury (1807). 

It is not reversible error to move for a view in the presence of the jury for the objector may move that the jury be retired before he makes objections (1808). 

25. Effect of view on judicial review. It has been held in civil cases that as a general rule view together with substantial testimony will preclude a review of the verdict and judgment on the ground that they are contrary to the overwhelming weight of evidence. In effect, the fact of view is treated as evidence the effect of which the appellate court cannot review, and the winner is given the decision on the conditions indicated (1809). 

26. Defendant's presence. The defendant must be present at the view. In a small house, some jurors may be in one room with the defendant and a few may be on the porch, without error. Defendant's voluntary absence at the view in a criminal case is not error (1810). 

27. Confrontation. It must appear affirmatively in the record in capital cases that the defendant was present during the entire trial and was confronted with the witnesses (1811). 

The right of confrontation may be waived. Earlier decisions are in conflict (1812).

E. ANSWER AS EVIDENCE 

28. The answer as evidence in equity.-An answer under oath denying all the circumstances upon which a bill is founded requires two positive witnesses or one credible witness and strong circumstances to overcome the effect of the answer as evidence (1813).

The answer of one defendant is evidence for a codefendant in privity with him; otherwise, not (1814). In equity the answer of one defendant inures as evidence to the benefit of a co-defendant until it is met (1815).

29. Information and belief.-An answer denying on information and belief the allegations of a bill is not evidence and proves nothing (1816). 

If the answer shows on its face that it is made on information and belief, though it be positive in averment, it is not evidence (1817). 

30. Unsworn answer.-An unsworn answer is not evidence (1818). 

31. Irresponsive answer.-The sworn answer is evidence only if responsive (1819). 

32. Affirmative matter.-Affirmative matter in an answer must be proved; the sworn answer is not proof of the affirmative matter (1820). the bill is 

33. Statutory modifications.—Where sworn to by the complainant in person, not by attorney, the sworn answer need not be overthrown and the answer is entitled only to such weight and credit as in view of the interests of the party making it and the other circumstances it may be fairly entitled (1821). 

34. Waiver of oath to answer.-Waiver of oath to the answer excuses complainant from overthrowing the answer, though sworn (1822). The waiver may be written on the answer, unless the record shows the respondent refused to waive the omission in the bill (1823). 

VI. BURDEN OF PROOF: PROCEDURE OF PROOF A. INCIDENCE AND DEGREE 

1. Criminal cases.-In criminal cases the state has the burden of proving every fact essential to guilt beyond every reasonable doubt arising from the evidence or from the want of evidence; and the jury must believe from the evidence that the defendant is guilty, before they may convict (1824).

2. No definition of reasonable doubt.-Definition of reasonable doubt is not permitted. Definitions only confuse. To define reasonable doubt would be to compute that which is not number and to measure that which is not space. The court has stated in an opinion that the word "reasonable" means just rational, conformable or agreeable to the faculty of the mind by which it distinguishes truth from falsehood and good from evil, and which enables the possessor to deduce inferences from facts or from propositions. It is believed that an exposition of this statement and an illustration of the types of reasoning would be helpful, despite the fact that the reason does not have physical dimensions and measurements in feet, yards or pounds (1825). 

3. Extent of persuasion of the jury.—It is reversible error to refuse to instruct that it is the duty of each juror to decide the issues for himself and if, after consideration of the law and the evidence and full consultation with his fellow jurors, a single juror has a reasonable doubt of the defendant's guilt, it is his duty to stand by his conviction and he should never yield or compromise his convictions simply because every other single juror may disagree with him, or in order to bring in a verdict (1826). 

4. Circumstantial evidence.-Where circumstantial evidence alone is relied on to show guilt, every circumtance necessary to prove guilt must be established beyond a reasonable doubt and to the exclusion of every reasonable hypothesis of innocence (1827).

The rule requiring the exclusion of every reasonable hypothesis of innocence is not applicable to cases not resting entirely on circumstantial evidence (1828). 

5. Two theories. If from the evidence two plausible theories appear, the one of guilt, the other of innocence, and if the jury are unable to determine from the evidence which theory is true, then the jury must accept the theory of innocence (1829).

6. Cautionary instruction. It is error to instruct that circumstantial evidence is as good as any other evidence (1830).
