It is error not to instruct that circumstantial evidence should be viewed with caution and vigilance and it is insufficient unless it excludes every other hypothesis than that of the defendant's guilt (1831). 

7. Metaphysical certainty not required.-It is proper to instruct that metaphysical and demonstrative certainty is not required in proof in criminal cases (1832). 

8. Competency of circumstantial evidence. It is error to refuse to instruct that circumstantial evidence is proper and competent and that direct evidence is not essential, for it is a matter of common knowledge that some persons refuse to act on circumstantial evidence (1833). When this instruction is given, the rule must be stated requiring exclusion of every reasonable hypothesis of innocence (1834). 

9. Burden of proof does not shift.-It is error to instruct that the defendant has the burden of rebutting the presumption of malice from the killing of a human being (1835). 10.

Affirmative defenses.-The defendant does not have the burden of proving affirmative defenses but it is enough for acquittal if merely a reasonable doubt of his guilt is found from the evidence, or want of evidence (1836). 

11. Phraseology in describing grounds for exercise of right of self-defense. Synonyms of "reasonable" grounds in describing the permissible grounds to exercise the privilege of self-defense are; good ground, good reason, good ground honestly entertained. These expressions are correct (1837). 

12. Civil cases. In civil cases the plaintiff has the burden of proving all facts material to his case by a preponderance of the evidence (1838).

In civil actions circumstantial evidence is not required to exclude contrary hypotheses before being accepted as proof of an issue (1839).

The burden of proof in an action for conversion of property is on the complaining party to prove the case by a preponderance of the evidence (1839-a). 

13. Burden of proof does not shift.-Mis-pleading does not shift the burden of proof unless the opponent is misled thereby (1840).

While the duty to come forward and meet a prima facie showing of some issue may shift from side to side, the burden of proof of the issues in the case does not shift (1841). 

14. Number of jurors to be convinced. In civil actions nine jurors may return a verdict, and on request the jury should be instructed thus (1842). 

15. Exception in specific issues.-When a fact is peculiarly in the knowledge of one of the parties so that he may prove it with ease, then he must prove it, although logically the burden would be on the other side to prove such fact as a part of his case (1843).

B. QUANTITATIVE RULES ON BURDEN OF PROOF IN SPECIFIC ISSUES 

16. Rebutting presumption of negligence by railway company. To overcome the presumption of negligence by a railway company the evidence must show clearly how the injury occurred (1844). But see Presumptions 151, supra. 

17. Corroboration of hounds.—Trailing the defendant by hounds must be corroborated by testimony or other evidence showing guilt (1845). 

18. Corroboration of confession.-Confessions must be corroborated by evidence proving the corpus delicti (1846). Slighter proof of the corpus delicti of a crime confessed is required if the corroboration shows the act confessed is not imaginary, than in case of a crime not

confessed. The confession may be considered along with the other evidence in finding the corpus delicti (1847). 

19. Divorce.-In divorce admissions of adultery by the husband must be corroborated by circumstances or other proof (1848). 

20. Burden of proof under rules of evidence; physician-patient privilege; tacit admissions.-The burden of proof of the existence of the physician-patient privilege is on the person exercising the privilege. The burden is on the state to prove that the defendant did not deny an accusation made in his presence (1849). 

21. Competency of dying declarations.-The burden of proof is on the state to prove beyond a reasonable doubt that the deceased was conscious of immediate, impending death in order to make his dying declarations admissible (1850). 

22. Harmful error. The person seeking reversal for error in ruling on evidence has the burden of proving harmful error (1851). 

23. Voluntariness of confession.-The state has the burden of proving beyond a reasonable doubt that confessions are free and voluntary (1852). 

24. Unconstitutionality of statutes.-Unconstitutionality of statutes must be proved beyond a reasonable doubt (1853). 

25. Return of process.-Clear proof is required to overcome the presumption that an officer's return is correct (1854). 

26. Defendant as a witness.-The defendant as a witness does not require corroboration before his testimony is believed by the jury, despite his interest in the case (1855). 

27. Testimony of accomplices.-The testimony of an accomplice alone is sufficient to sustain a conviction if not improbable on its face (1856).

28. Treason.-Two witnesses to the same overt act of treason, or a confession in open court is required in cases of treason (1857). 

29. Alienage.-Evidence of alienage must be clear and satisfactory (1858). 

30. Rape vi et armis.-The testimony of the prosecutrix in rape by violence may be sufficient to convict but uncorroborated it should be viewed with caution (1859).

In an action for rape vi et armis the testimony of the prosecutrix does not have to be corroborated, if consistent with the surrounding circumstances and conditions shown by the evidence (1859-a). 

31. Statutory rape.-No person shall be convicted of statutory rape on the uncorroborated testimony of the prosecutrix. Proof of sexual intercourse must be corroborated, but not of the other facts (1860). 

32. Seduction over age of eighteen, by fraud.—The testimony of a female over 18 seduced by promise of, or feigned, marriage must be corroborated as to the act of intercourse, the promise of marriage, and the previous chaste character of the prosecutrix (1861). 

33. Attempt to rape.-Corroboration is required of testimony of the injured female in an action for an attempt to violate the age of consent statute. (1862). 

34. Larceny.-Corroboration is not required of a witness to larceny (1863). 

35. Fraud.-Clear and convincing proof of fraud is required (1864). 

36. Perjury.-Two witnesses or one witness and corroborating circumstances are required to overcome the presumption of innocence and the defendant's oath in an action of perjury (1865). 

37. Domestic relations; bigamy and criminal conversation. It is said that in bigamy and criminal conversation, marriage may not be proved by cohabitation and holding out (1866). But see Circ. Ev. 22.

38. Annulment.-Duress as cause of annulment of marriage must be proved clearly and convincingly (1867). 

39. Proof in divorce.-In uncontested divorce the testimony of the complainant must be corroborated and also in ex parte matters where proof is required by the chancellor (1868). 

40. Adultery.-Circumstantial evidence of adultery must exclude a reasonable theory of innocence (1869). 

Admissions of adultery require corroboration (1870). 

41. Insanity of Testator.-The clearest and most satisfactory proof is required to establish incapacity of a testator by reason of monomania (1871). 

42. Proof of a will.-The clearest and most convincing and satisfactory proof of a lost will is required, and declarations of a testator must be corroborated (1872). 

43. Claims against a decedent's estate.-Clear and reasonably positive proof is required of claims against a decedent's estate (1873). 

44. Noncupative wills.-The clearest and most indisputable evidence is required to establish a nuncupative will (1874). 

45. Reformation.—Mistake must be shown beyond a reasonable doubt in reformation (1875). 

46. Parol declaration of trust.-Clear and unambiguous proof of a parol declaration of a trust is required (1876). 

46-a. Adverse possession.-Clear and unequivocal evidence of claims and acts adverse to the title of the true owner of land are required to overcome the presumption that possession is in subordination to the true title (1876-a).

47. Falsity of an officer's return.-Clear and convincing proof is required to show the falsity of an officer's return on process (1877). 

48. Acknowledgement of signature. The uncorroborated denial by a person of his signature is insufficient to overcome an acknowledgement thereof (1878). 

49. Experts in accounts.—An auditor must be introduced to tabulate and summarize extensive accounts, otherwise the original papers and books are valueless (1879). 

50. Inference on inference.-Inference will not be piled on inference where more direct proof is available (1880).

C. PROCEDURE IN PROOF 51. Discovery. Statutory remedies and procedure, Section 744, Chapter 19, Courts, Provisions Common to, 

Code, 1930; and Sections 385, 1551, 1825, 1601, 1671, 3450, Code, 1930.

The bill must be sworn and good cause must be shown, and the facts to be proved must relate to the plaintiff's case, and not to defensive matter. Discovery will not be granted where the plaintiff has the same means of information as the defendant has (1881).

Evidence sought to be elicited by interrogatories to nonresidents under Section 1551, Code, 1930, must be pertinent or relevant to the litigation under consideration. It was not intended that the resident party should explore all the business affairs of the nonresident. And interrogatories asking detailed information regarding loans amounting to 10 million dollars are improper, although the issue in the case was whether the non-resident was doing business in the state without filing its charter (1881-a).

The proper procedure to challenge improper interrogatories to a nonresident, under Section 1551, Code,

1930, is by motion to suppress before undertaking to answer, or else to take exception thereto (1881-a). 

52. Notice to produce.-A party who has been notified to produce a document may not introduce the document without first exhibiting it to the other side (1882). 

Order of Evidence 

53. Rule. Generally, parties are not restricted in the order of introducing their proof in chief (1883).

The court has discretion in admitting evidence where the relevancy is not apparent and must be separately shown (1884).

Where joint defendants are sued the court has discretion to require either one of them to introduce his evidence first (1885).

In a criminal case a defendant may insist of right upon testifying as to the involuntariness of a confession before it is admitted into evidence (1886). 

Re-Examination of Witnesses 

54. Rule.-Re-examination of witnesses in open court may be permitted in the discretion of the court, even after the jury retires (1887). 

55. Scope. In re-examination after cross-examination, the re-examination is restricted to the cross-examination and in explanation of the direct examination, but the court has discretion to relax this rule (1888). 

56. When dispute as to what a witness said.-Where a dispute arises as to what a witness said, the court may recall the witness, but the court may not express an opinion as to what the witness said (1889). 

57. New issue. Where a new phase of a case has been developed after a witness has been examined the party has a right to recall and re-examine the witness upon this issue (1890). 

58. Scope of rebuttal.-The plaintiff cannot put on the case in chief in rebuttal, as a matter of right (1891).

The greater number of cases in Mississippi permit the introduction of evidence of the substantive right in rebuttal, in the discretion of the court, that justice may be done (1892). 

59. Reopening. After a demurrer to evidence has been taken the party should be permitted to offer further evidence on seasonable application in order that defect of proof may be remedied and justice done (1893).

Where a case is reopened it is essential that the other side be given opportunity to put in additional evidence (1894). 

60. Remand in equity.-In equity a case will be remanded to the docket for further proof if the court is satisfied that the merits cannot be reached without this, but after the final decree, the case cannot be reopened in equity (1895). 

61. Proof after motion to exclude.-A defendant in chancery may introduce proof after losing a motion to exclude the complainant's evidence (1896). 

62. Bill of review. On showing due diligence in using available sources of information a bill of review may be allowed in the discretion of the court (1897). 

Attendance of Witnesses, and Continuances and New Trials 

63. Rule.-A continuance will be granted because of the absence of a material witness only when a subpoena has been issued, due diligence has been used to compel his attendance, and in case of a non-resident witness it must appear that the witness probably would be present at the trial at the next term of court (1898). 

64. New trials.-Unless diligence to have a material witness at the trial is shown, a new trial will not be granted (1899). 

65. New evidence. To obtain a new trial for newly discovered evidence the following are required: An af-

fidavit of the witness stating the substance of the new evidence, so that it may appear that it is material, an affidavit by the defendant and by the attorney for the defendant, or by the other side, that they were ignorant of the facts before the trial and used due diligence to learn the facts. If the affidavit of the witness cannot be obtained, this must be shown (1900). 

A new trial will not be granted for newly discovered, impeaching, cumulative, or hearsay evidence (1901). Before evidence is classed as cumulative, the party is entitled to have his own and one witness' testimony on the point at issue (1902).

Additional details not shown by another witness are not cumulative, if material (1903). 

66. When the record is closed.-Facts arising after the trial cannot be considered by the Supreme Court, and a suggestion of such facts will not be considered (1904). 

The Offer in the Record 

67. Rule. In order to show error in excluding testimony, the witness must be placed on the stand, questioned, and his answers made a part of the record or else the witness must be presented and a specific statement made of the substance of what his testimony would be. The purpose of the offer may not be varied on appeal (1905). 

68. Privileged matter.—But privileged matter is an exception to the foregoing rule, and the offerer should not state such matter into the record. Only the existence of the privileged relationship may be shown (1906). 

69. Cross-examination.-The purpose of cross-examination need not be disclosed in advance in an offer (1907). 

70. Excluded documents, etc.-Excluded depositions or photographs, diagrams, must be included in the record on appeal if the ruling is to be reviewed (1908).

71. Exhibits.-Exhibits should be sent to the Supreme Court only when the inspection of them would aid the court in determining the matter before it (1909). 

72. Control of court papers.-The court may detain documents where needed, to prosecute for perjury (1910). 

A party may withdraw an account filed by him, or any paper or document, on following the statutory procedure (1911).

The Record of Evidence 

See Ch. 18, Code, 1930, Court Reporting; and Ch. 

132, Sec. 3, Laws of 1940. 

73. Notice; two stenographers.-Where two court stenographers took testimony, notice to one to transcribe the testimony is sufficient (1912). Notice is essential (1913). 

74. Completeness.-A transcript which does not include all testimony given will be stricken on motion (1914). 

75. Death of stenographer; bill of exceptions.—

Where the stenographer dies, the court has no power to extend the 60 days allowed for filing a bill of exceptions (1915). 

76. Corrections.-The trial judge has authority to correct the transcript of evidence (1916). The Supreme 

Court has no authority to compel the stenographer to file his notes (1917).

The Objection 

77. Silence. The silence of a party when evidence is offered waives any objection to it (1918). 

78. When. An objection to the competency of a witness must be made while the witness is on the stand (1919).

79. Waiver; qualification of witness.-Failure to object to incompetency of a witness waives the point (1920). 

80. Best evidence rule.-Failure to object to violation of the best evidence rule waives the point (1921). 

81. Parol evidence.-Failure to object to violation of the parol evidence rule waives the point of evidence, but not the substantive consequences (1922). 

82. Illegally obtained evidence.-Nonobjection to the introduction of illegally obtained evidence waives the point (1923). 

83. Involuntary confession.--Nonobjection to an involuntary confession does not waive the point (1924). 

84. Objection on court's own motion. In favor of infants the court will interpose an objection of its own motion, although the guardian make no objection (1925).

The court of its own motion may exclude improper volunteered remarks of a witness, though no objection is made (1926). 

85. Improper argument.-An objection to testimonial use of impeaching evidence is timely if made at the time of the argument (1927). 

86. Forced confessions.-Nonobjection to forced confessions does not waive the point (1928). 

87. Total failure of proof.-Nonobjection does not waive total failure of proof of the operative facts, or violation of the parol evidence rule or introduction of hearsay (1929). 

88. Waiver.-Withdrawal of an objection waives error in admitting evidence (1930). 

89. The general objection.-A general objection overruled will not be considered on appeal if the evidence was competent for any purpose (1931).

A general objection sustained will be sustained on appeal if in any point of view regarding the whole record the ruling was proper (1932). 

90. The specific objection.-Where part of testimony is admissible and part is not, an objection must specify the inadmissible part, to save the point. If a defect is curable, the objection must specify it (1933). 

If evidence is competent for any purpose, a specific objection must be made, as where a defect was curable, or the evidence would be admissible if pleadings were amended (1934).

An objection cannot be made in the Supreme Court for the first time unless due process is involved (1935). 

91. Blanket objections to line of questions.—A blanket objection to a line of questions is permitted in equity, and at law (1936). Where an objection has been made promptly at the first appearance of objectionable evidence, and has been overruled, it is not necessary to repeat the objection upon the recurrence of the same question in the further progress of the case (1936-a). 

92. No shifting of grounds of specific objection. 

The ground of a specific objection may not be shifted on appeal (1937). 

93. Motion to strike.-Formerly, a motion to strike the complainant's evidence did not lie in chancery, but 

Ch. 265, Laws of 1938, seems to recognize the practice of making such motion (1938).

A motion to exclude testimony may be made after the witnesses for the other side have been examined. 

The movant may wait until the testimony has been developed and then move to exclude it (1939). 

The motion to exclude must be restricted to the incompetent part of the testimony (1940). 

94. The ruling must be obtained.-Unless the record show the ruling, the ruling is not reviewable on appeal (1941).

Where a ruling is reserved, the objection must be renewed and a ruling requested later if the point is to be saved (1942). 

On some occasions, a ruling may be reserved in a jury case, but if objectionable evidence is admitted the later exclusion will not cure the error, unless the Supreme Court can safely say that the jury was not materially influenced by it (1943). 

95. The exception.-Exceptions should not be made or noted unless otherwise the ruling would not appear of record. Only the objection need be made and noted (1944). 

96. Objection to evidence on points of pleading; or insufficiency.-Objection to evidence because of variance from the pleadings must be made when the evidence is offered or before the verdict (1945).

An objection to insufficiency of evidence must be made at the trial or the point is lost (1946).

A motion for a directed verdict challenges the sufficiency but not the competency of the evidence (1947).

Where defendants introduced evidence after moving to exclude the plaintiff's evidence, this waives the objection to the action of the court in refusing to exclude the plaintiff's evidence (1947-a). 

97. Cross-assignment.—The appellee must make a cross-assignment of error in ruling on evidence if he desires to appeal on the rulings affecting him (1948). 

98. Motion for new trial not required.—A motion for a new trial is not required on the ground of error in ruling on evidence in order to appeal from such ruling (1949). Appearance 

99. Record of appearance by attorney or client.-In a civil case an appearance by a defendant may be in writing executed after suit filed, acknowledged and filed in court (1950).

A plea filed by an attorney for parties constitutes an appearance (1951).

A statement in the record made under sanction of the court that named parties appeared by attorney is sufficient (1952). 

100. Retirement of the jury.-The jury should be retired when the competency of a witness or of a confession is being ascertained (1953).

Unless a request is made that the jury retire, the point is waived (1954).

D. ERROR 

101. Nonprejudicial error in evidence.-Where point is proved beyond a doubt by competent testimony, the erroneous admission of incompetent evidence is harmless (1955).

The improper exclusion of evidence of a fact otherwise abundantly proved is harmless (1956).

Where on the whole case justice is done, the erroneous admission of evidence is harmless (1957). 

102. Harmful error.-Error in admitting evidence is harmful unless the court can say the improper evidence could not have changed the result (1958).

Error in excluding evidence must be made to appear clearly in the record (1959).

Prejudicial error is not shown where, regardless of the incompetent evidence admitted, no case is shown on the merits (1960). 

103. Immaterial matters.-Error in immaterial matters in evidence is harmless (1961). 

104. Material facts.-Admission of incompetent evidence on a material issue is harmful, and exclusion of competent evidence on a material issue is harmful, unless the court can say no effect was thereby produced. 

In close cases, this last cannot be found and hence error

in ruling on evidence bearing on material issues is harmful (1962). 

105. Instructions on evidence.-Error in instructions on evidence are harmless where on the whole case the same result would have been reached (1963). 

106. Curing error.-The erroneous admission of evidence may be cured by an instruction to disregard it only when the court can say that the evidence did not change the result of the case (1964).

Error in excluding evidence is cured by offering later to admit it or by actually admitting it by the same or another witness (1965). 

107. Admissibility by estoppel.-Where one party is permitted to introduce evidence on a collateral issue, it is error to exclude the other party's evidence on that issue (1966). 

Where a party himself introduces a bill of exceptions or a dying declaration, he cannot be heard to object to use of parts of such evidence by his opponent, although the evidence may ordinarily be objectionable (1967).

Index 

VIII. INDEX 

Explanatory Note: References in the Index have been grouped first by topics, and under each topic subjects are listed alphabetically. The basis of the topical analysis is given in the Preface, and the rulings are indexed under these general topics, as summarized below. To locate the topic under which any point is indexed, the point sought should be classified as coming under the topic either of (I) Relevancy; or (II) Facts in General, viz., presumptions, circumstantial evidence, judicial notice; or (III)

Documentary Evidence, viz., parol evidence, best evidence, formal requisites, Statute of Frauds; or (IV) Testimonial Evidence, viz., qualifications of witnesses, opinion evidence and hypothetical questions, memoranda, maps, photographs, moving pictures, hearsay, and hearsay exceptions, as admissions, confessions, etc., etc., impeachment, persons eligible as witnesses, privileges, examination, questions, cross-examination, process, depositions; or (V) Real

Evidence and View, viz., real or demonstrative evidence, privacy of the person, view of the place, and the sworn answer as proof; or (VI) Burden and Procedure of Proof, viz., incidence and degree of proof, quantitative rules, discovery, order of evidence, attendance of witnesses, the offer, the record, the objection, and the curing of error in admission of evidence. 

The  follows the same topical arrangement, but gives the rulings in analytic order under the topics, insofar as the decisions permit of logical arrangement. 

The complete alphabetic list of Index topics is as follows:
